The card's design follows the block device's choice (section 9.6) of something a first driver can copy over a general queue interface: no feature negotiation, one descriptor format for both directions, and every slot of a ring usable thanks to free-running counters. A driver is about forty instructions (`examples/net/netlib.S`).

`examples/net` is a pair of programs sharing that driver: `net-pong` waits for a frame on its receive interrupt (in `wfi`), prints it and answers its sender; `net-ping` broadcasts a frame (EtherType 0x88b5, "local experimental") and polls the receive ring for the answer. `crates/grail/tests/net.rs` runs them on two machines on one hub, and checks a capture of the exchange byte for byte against `crates/grail/tests/golden/net-ping.pcap`. One `grail` process runs one machine, so there `net-ping` gets no answer, but its frame is in the capture: `grail --net hub --pcap ping.pcap examples/net/net-ping.elf`.

#### User networking (`--net user`)

`--net user` gives a card internet and host access with nothing installed on the host: its backend is a **NAT gateway** in Grail's process (what QEMU calls "slirp" user networking), which speaks Ethernet and IPv4 to the guest and uses ordinary `std::net` sockets on the host. A TAP device or a bridge would need a kernel driver on Windows, which this project does not use, so the host never sees the guest as a machine on its LAN; the guest sees a small private network instead, with the same plan as QEMU's (so its documentation applies):

| Address | What |
|---|---|
| 10.0.2.0/24 | the guest network (netmask 255.255.255.0) |
| 10.0.2.2 | the gateway: router and DHCP server, MAC `52:55:0a:00:02:02`. Connecting to it reaches the host's 127.0.0.1 (TCP and UDP), so a guest uses services running on the host |
| 10.0.2.3 | the DNS server |
| 10.0.2.15 to 10.0.2.254 | DHCP leases, one per MAC address, from .15 up; a card's lease never changes while the gateway lives |

The card's MAC address is `52:54:00:12:34:56` unless `mac=` sets one. The gateway lives in its own crate, `crates/gateway`, which depends on nothing but std and not on Grail: it takes frames from the guest (`Gateway::send`) and gives frames for it (`Gateway::recv`), and Grail wraps it as a card's `NetBackend` (`crates/grail/src/net/user.rs`). Anything else that moves Ethernet frames can attach the same gateway without a dependency cycle, and an Avalon switch does (below). What it does with each frame from the guest:

- **ARP**: requests for 10.0.2.2 and 10.0.2.3 are answered with the gateway's MAC. The gateway learns each guest's MAC from what it sends (DHCP, ARP, IP), and asks with ARP for an address it has not heard from (a forwarded connection to a guest that has not spoken yet); packets wait for the answer for at most a second.
- **DHCP** (RFC 2131): DISCOVER, OFFER, REQUEST, ACK (a REQUEST for another address gets a NAK, which takes nothing from the pool; INFORM is answered; RELEASE and DECLINE are ignored). A client asks for its own card: a message whose `chaddr` is not the frame's source MAC address, or is a group address, is ignored, so a card cannot drain the pool with made-up `chaddr`s. Leases never expire while the gateway lives. A guest that also makes up its frames' source MAC addresses could use up the 240 addresses; in Grail the gateway serves one card, and an Avalon switch passes its gateway only the frames whose source address is the sending card's own (below), so each card gets one lease at most. Every OFFER and ACK carries the subnet mask, router 10.0.2.2, DNS server 10.0.2.3 and a lease time of a day; replies go to the client's MAC and its new address, or are broadcast when the client sets the broadcast flag.
- **ICMP**: echo requests to 10.0.2.2 and 10.0.2.3 are answered. ICMP to any other address is dropped, so `ping` to the internet does not work: forwarding it would need raw sockets, which need privileges on the host.
- **DNS**: queries to 10.0.2.3 port 53 are answered by the gateway itself, not forwarded: an A query is looked up with the host's resolver (`ToSocketAddrs`, which is what every program on the host uses, hosts file included, and the only resolver std has) on a helper thread, and answered with its IPv4 addresses (TTL 60 s, as many as fit in 512 bytes); a name the resolver cannot find is NXDOMAIN. AAAA and other types get an empty answer (the guest network is IPv4 only), a class other than IN is NOTIMP, a malformed query is FORMERR.
- **UDP** to any other address leaves through a host `UdpSocket`, one per guest port; datagrams that come back to that socket go to the guest, from the address they came from (127.0.0.1 shows as 10.0.2.2). A datagram larger than one frame can carry is dropped (no fragmentation either way).
- **TCP**: the gateway plays the remote end. A guest's SYN makes it connect a host `TcpStream` (on a helper thread, since std cannot connect without blocking; 10 s at most); the guest gets its SYN-ACK once that succeeds, or a reset. From then on the gateway runs a small TCP toward the guest (in-order delivery, retransmission with a doubling timeout from 200 ms up to 5 s and a reset after 8 tries, zero-window probes, the guest's MSS and window respected) and copies bytes to and from the host stream, with a FIN or reset passed on either way.
- Packets for other guest addresses (another machine on the same segment), for loopback, broadcast or multicast addresses, IPv4 fragments, other EtherTypes and anything malformed (truncated, a bad header or payload checksum) are dropped and never a panic. The one answer is to a TCP segment the gateway cannot take (to such an address, or of no connection it knows): a reset, as a host with nothing listening sends.

**Host to guest** (`hostfwd`): `--net user,hostfwd=RULE` (repeatable) forwards a host port to a guest port. RULE is QEMU's syntax, `[tcp|udp]:[HOSTADDR]:HOSTPORT-[GUESTADDR]:GUESTPORT`: the protocol is TCP if left empty or out (`::8080-:80`), HOSTADDR is 127.0.0.1 if left out (so only programs on the host can connect; give 0.0.0.0 to open the port to the LAN), GUESTADDR is 10.0.2.15, the first lease. `hostfwd=tcp::8080-:80` (or `tcp:8080-:80`) lets a browser on Windows open `http://127.0.0.1:8080` and reach the guest's web server on port 80. The gateway binds the host ports when the machine is built (a port in use is an error then); each connection accepted becomes a TCP connection to the guest from 10.0.2.2 and a port from 49152 up, and each host client of a forwarded UDP port gets its own such port, so the guest's answers go back to the right client. Port 0 lets the system pick (a host using the library reads the port from `Gateway::hostfwd`, which lists the rules in the order given).

**Limits**, so a guest cannot exhaust the host (`gateway::Limits`, the defaults below): at most 256 flows per guest machine (TCP connections either way, UDP ports it sends from, host clients of forwarded UDP ports; a SYN past it is reset, a datagram dropped, a forwarded connection closed), where a machine is a MAC address: a flow counts against the card that opened it, whatever source address it used (a forwarded port's flows against the card its guest address was learned from), so making up addresses in 10.0.2.0/24 gains nothing; at most 1024 flows on the gateway in all, the bound for a guest that makes up MAC addresses too, and so for the host sockets and connect threads, each of which belongs to a flow; 64 KiB buffered per TCP connection each way, the window advertised to the guest being what is left (so a guest that sends faster than the host takes waits, and nothing more is read from a host socket than the guest has room for); 512 frames queued for the guest, beyond which frames are dropped (and while it is half full no host socket is read, TCP or UDP, and no forwarded connection accepted: what the host sends waits in the host's socket buffers); 8 DNS lookups at once (a query past them is dropped, and the guest asks again). Idle flows are closed: a UDP flow (and a forwarded UDP client) after 60 s without a datagram, a TCP connection after 2 hours without traffic, or 60 s while it is being opened or closed. No raw sockets, and no threads besides the short-lived connect and lookup helpers: everything else runs in the two calls the card makes, the host sockets being serviced (at most once a millisecond) when the card polls for received frames, so a paused machine's sockets wait for it.

`examples/net/net-user` is a bare-metal guest for it, sharing the card driver of `net-ping` and `net-pong`: it gets its address by DHCP, then reads commands from the UART: `c PORT` opens a TCP connection to 10.0.2.2:PORT, sends a greeting and prints the answer until the host closes; `l PORT` accepts one connection on PORT and echoes it back; `q` exits. Its TCP is the least that works over the gateway (one connection, no retransmission of its own: the gateway's link never loses the guest's frames). `crates/grail/tests/net_user.rs` runs it with a listener of the test on 127.0.0.1 and a `hostfwd` port the test connects to, then through `grail --net user`; `crates/gateway` has the unit tests, which drive the gateway with crafted frames. None of it needs the internet. By hand: `printf 'c 8000\nq\n' | grail --net user examples/net/net-user.elf` with something listening on port 8000.

#### User networking on an Avalon switch

An Avalon switch can have a NAT gateway of its own (`switch create NAME --nat [--hostfwd RULE]...` in Round Table, `switch nat NAME` to add one later and `switch nat NAME --off` to take it away; the switches page in Pendragon; `CreateSwitch` and `SetSwitchNat` in the protocol, section 6.7). It is the same `crates/gateway`, with the same network plan, behaviour and limits as above, on a port of the switch instead of behind one card:

- **One gateway for every machine on the switch.** Each card asks for its own lease by DHCP (10.0.2.15 to the first to ask, .16 to the next, one per MAC address, never changing while the gateway lives), and the flow limits count per card, so one VM cannot use up another's flows. The machines also reach each other directly across the switch, as on any switch.
- **The gateway's port is a port like the others.** It learns addresses, has the gateway's MAC `52:55:0a:00:02:02`, counts frames and drops in `SwitchList`, and its frames are in the switch's capture. Frames for it wait in a bounded queue (256 frames), and a full queue drops and counts them: a VM's thread hands a frame over and never waits for the gateway. The gateway runs on a thread of its own, which takes the queued frames, services the host sockets and forwards what the gateway answers back into the switch; it polls every millisecond while there is traffic and every 10 ms when idle, so unlike Grail's a gateway on a switch keeps its sockets serviced while a VM is paused.
- **No made-up addresses.** The switch passes a card's frame to its gateway only when the frame's source MAC address is the card's own; others still reach the other cards (so a guest experimenting with addresses can talk to its neighbours) but never take a lease or a flow on the gateway. A card cannot be plugged into a switch whose gateway has its MAC, nor a gateway added to a switch where such a card is plugged in.
- **Forwarded ports** follow the rules above, and a rule's guest address defaults to 10.0.2.15, which is the first card to ask for a lease. To reach a particular VM, name its address (`tcp::8080-10.0.2.16:80`): leases go out in the order the cards ask, so a VM that must be at a known address should be the only one, or the first, to start on the switch, or the host asks `switch show` (Round Table) or `SwitchList` which address each card has and forwards a port to it on a switch made for that. The host ports are bound on 127.0.0.1 unless a rule names another address; they are bound when the request is made, so a port that is taken fails the request (`Io`, naming the rule) and changes nothing. The rules are fixed while the gateway lives: to change them, take the gateway away and give the switch a new one.
- **Ending.** Deleting the switch or taking its gateway away ends the gateway's thread and closes every host socket (forwarded ports and connections) before the reply, so the ports can be bound again at once; the cards stay plugged in when only the gateway goes.

`crates/avalon/tests/nat.rs` runs two VMs with `examples/net/net-user` on one switch with a gateway: they get different leases, one connects through 10.0.2.2 to a listener of the test on 127.0.0.1, the test reaches the other through a forwarded port, the gateway's port shows in the counters and the capture, and deleting the switch frees the port.

## 10. Toolchain (all our own)

Everything that runs on the host is written by us, with no third-party libraries doing the heavy lifting (std only). The target side is code produced by our own tools.

| Tool | Input → output | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Assembler (`merlin-as`) | `.s` → `.o` | Full RV32I or RV64I (`-march`, per object) +M, A, Zicsr, Zifencei, privileged, Xcal mnemonics, standard pseudo-instructions (`li`, `la`, `call`, `ret`, `mv`, `j`, `beqz`, `sext.w`...), `.insn`, directives (`.text .data .bss .section .globl .byte .half .word .dword .ascii .align .equ`), `%hi/%lo/%pcrel_hi/%pcrel_lo`; ELF32 or ELF64 objects. See "Assembler" below |
| Linker (`merlin-ld`) | `.o` files → executable | Symbol resolution, relocation, section merging, simple linker script with load addresses (`AT`), or fixed layout: `.text` at 0x8000_0000; an ELF32 or ELF64 executable, the class of its objects. See "Linker" below |
| Emulator (`grail`) | executable → runs it | Loads ELF directly, no boot loader needed early on |
| Disassembler (`merlin-objdump`) | `.o`/exe → text | Shares the decoder with the emulator; essential for debugging |
| Debugger (`merlin-dbg`) | a running program → breakpoints, steps, state | A GDB remote protocol client for Grail's stub (`grail --gdb`, section 7, "Debugging"), for RV32 and RV64 machines (the width from the stub's target description, as GDB takes it): machine-level breakpoints, watchpoints, steps, registers, memory, disassembly, backtraces by frame pointer, harts, and source-level ones from the line tables of `merlin -g` (`break FILE:LINE`, `step`/`next` by line, `list`; see "Debug information"); see `docs/merlin-dbg.md` |
| Compiler (`merlin`) | C subset → `.s` | Emits assembly for our assembler, for RV32 (ILP32) or RV64 (LP64) (`-march`, per translation unit). C99 (preprocessor and varargs included) without floating point so far; builds the whole kernel (see "Merlin C subset" and "Kernel build") |

### Merlin C subset

`merlin [-march=ISA] [-mabi=ABI] [-I dir] [-D name[=value]] [-U name] input.c... [-o output.s]` compiles each input as its own translation unit, to `input.s` next to it (`-o` names the output of a single input); `-E` prints the preprocessed source instead (with a space between two tokens wherever writing them together would read as other tokens), and `--dump-ast` the checked syntax tree. An assembly source to preprocess, a `.S` file (or any file after `-x assembler-with-cpp`; `-x c` makes the rest C), is only preprocessed, with `-E`, as `clang -E -x assembler-with-cpp` does it and writes it (line markers included), for merlin-as to assemble (#193, "Examples" below): merlin-as assembles no `.S` itself, as LLVM's assembler does not. `-P` leaves the line markers out, as `clang -E -P` does, with the blank lines clang drops then dropped too: for a preprocessed linker script (`kernel/kernel.lds.S`, "Building the kernel"), which can't have them. A program of several files is compiled file by file and linked with merlin-ld, together with any hand-written `.s` files: calls and `extern` variables work across files, and `static` names stay in theirs. Errors are reported as `file.c:line:col: error: message` (columns count characters from 1; an error in a header names the header); preprocessing, lexing and parsing stop at the first error, name resolution reports all of them.

The target is `-march=rv32ima_zicsr_zifencei`, the default until #159, with the ILP32 ABI, or `-march=rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei` with LP64, the ISAs merlin-as assembles (anything else is `unsupported -march`; the last `-march` wins); `-mabi=ilp32` or `-mabi=lp64` may say the ABI, which must be the ISA's (any other is `unknown target ABI`, as clang says). Everything below holds for both, with LP64's sizes and code where the text says so. The target is a parameter of the compiler's types, sizes and conversions (`Options::xlen`, recorded in the program the parser makes), not a global, so one process compiles for both widths.

The language is a strict subset of C99: a program whose behavior C defines means the same thing to clang (`--target=riscv32 -mabi=ilp32` or `--target=riscv64 -mabi=lp64`, `-std=c99`); anything outside the subset is an error, never a different meaning. The one deliberate extension is C11's typedef redefinition (`typedef int T; typedef int T;`, see below), which clang accepts in C99 mode too, with only a pedantic warning. It grows toward what the kernel needs (tracking issue #15). v0 was `int` only; the current subset is C99 without floating point, variable-length arrays, bit-fields and flexible array members (each an error):

- **Preprocessor** (C99 6.10, `crates/merlin/src/preprocess.rs`): `#include "file"` (the including file's directory, then each `-I` directory in order) and `#include <file>` (only the `-I` directories: merlin has no system headers), also with the name from a macro; `#define` of object-like and function-like macros, variadic ones too (`...`, `__VA_ARGS__`), with `#` (stringizing) and `##` (pasting), and C's rescanning rules (a macro is not replaced inside its own expansion; replacement follows Prosser's hide-set algorithm); `#undef`; `#if`/`#elif` with `defined` and integer constant expressions evaluated in 64 bits (identifiers left after replacement are 0; a literal too large for 64 bits is an error), `#ifdef`, `#ifndef`, `#else`, `#endif`; `#error`; `#line`; `#pragma once`; any other pragma means nothing to merlin and is passed on to the output as clang passes it on (`#pragma` and its tokens, unreplaced, a line of its own, which the parser skips), and so is `#ident "string"` (`#sccs` too, written `#ident`; macros replaced, anything after the string ignored, no string an error); `__FILE__` and `__LINE__`. A header found next to the file that includes it is named by that file's directory, `.` for one named without a directory, as clang names it (`./sub/s.h` next to `b.c`, its `__FILE__` and line table name too; clang on Windows writes `.\sub/s.h`, merlin `/` everywhere). Predefined: `__STDC__` 1, `__STDC_VERSION__` 199901L, `__STDC_HOSTED__` 0, `__merlin__`, `__riscv`, `__riscv_xlen` (32 or 64), `_ILP32` and `__ILP32__` on RV32, `_LP64` and `__LP64__` on RV64, `__CHAR_BIT__` 8, `__CHAR_UNSIGNED__`, `__SIZEOF_SHORT__` 2, `__SIZEOF_INT__` 4, `__SIZEOF_LONG__`, `__SIZEOF_POINTER__`, `__SIZEOF_SIZE_T__` and `__SIZEOF_PTRDIFF_T__` (4 on RV32, 8 on RV64), `__SIZEOF_LONG_LONG__` 8; the limits `__SCHAR_MAX__` 127, `__SHRT_MAX__` 32767, `__INT_MAX__` and `__INT32_MAX__` 2147483647, `__UINT32_MAX__` 4294967295U, `__LONG_LONG_MAX__` 9223372036854775807LL, and per width `__LONG_MAX__` (2147483647L, 9223372036854775807L), `__PTRDIFF_MAX__` and `__INTPTR_MAX__` (2147483647, 9223372036854775807L), `__SIZE_MAX__` and `__UINTPTR_MAX__` (4294967295U, 18446744073709551615UL), `__INT64_MAX__` (9223372036854775807LL, 9223372036854775807L) and `__UINT64_MAX__` (18446744073709551615ULL, 18446744073709551615UL); and the types `<stddef.h>` and `<stdint.h>` are built from, `__INT8_TYPE__` `signed char`, `__INT16_TYPE__` `short`, `__INT32_TYPE__` `int`, `__UINT8_TYPE__` `unsigned char`, `__UINT16_TYPE__` `unsigned short`, `__UINT32_TYPE__` `unsigned int`, and per width `__SIZE_TYPE__` and `__UINTPTR_TYPE__` (`unsigned int`, `long unsigned int`), `__PTRDIFF_TYPE__` and `__INTPTR_TYPE__` (`int`, `long int`), `__INT64_TYPE__` (`long long int`, `long int`) and `__UINT64_TYPE__` (`long long unsigned int`, `long unsigned int`); `__ELF__` 1; and the ISA of the `-march` (`rv32ima_zicsr_zifencei`, `rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei`) and the code model merlin's code follows (pc-relative, `auipc` for globals and calls: `-mcmodel=medany`): `__riscv_i` and `__riscv_a` 2001000, `__riscv_m`, `__riscv_zicsr` and `__riscv_zifencei` 2000000, `__riscv_zmmul`, `__riscv_zaamo`, `__riscv_zalrsc` and `__riscv_v_intrinsic` 1000000, `__riscv_mul`, `__riscv_div`, `__riscv_muldiv`, `__riscv_atomic`, `__riscv_arch_test`, `__riscv_float_abi_soft`, `__riscv_misaligned_avoid` and `__riscv_cmodel_medany` 1; each as clang defines it for `--target=riscv32 -mabi=ilp32` and `--target=riscv64 -mabi=lp64` with `-ffreestanding -std=c99 -mcmodel=medany` (the harness's ignored `predefined_macros_match_clang` checks every one against `clang -E -dM`, and that merlin has every one clang has about the ISA, the ABI, the object format and the language, in C and in assembly). `-D name` defines `name` as 1, `-D name=value` as `value`, `-D 'f(x)=value'` a function-like macro; `-U name` undefines; `-D` and `-U` apply in command-line order, after the predefined macros. Redefining a macro differently is an error (from `-D`, the last one wins). Includes nest at most 200 deep. Three things C leaves undefined are errors: a directive inside the arguments of a macro call, `defined` produced by a macro expansion in `#if`, and `#` on an argument that ends in a lone backslash (`s(\)`, which would be the invalid literal `"\"`). Line splices (a backslash at the end of a line) join lines first, as in C.

- **Types**: `_Bool`, `char`, `signed char`, `unsigned char`, `short`, `unsigned short`, `int`, `unsigned` (`unsigned int`), `long`, `unsigned long`, `long long`, `unsigned long long` (specifiers in any order, `signed`/`short int`/`long int`/`long long int` spellings included; `long long long` is an error), `enum`s, `void` as a function result and behind pointers, pointers (to functions too), arrays (any number of dimensions), structs and unions, and the qualifiers `const`, `volatile` (and `restrict`, accepted and ignored). Sizes are ILP32 on RV32: `_Bool` and `char` 1, `short` 2, `int`/`long`/enums/pointers 4, `long long` 8; and LP64 on RV64: the same but `long` and pointers 8. Each is aligned to its size (`long long` to 8 on RV32 too, as the psABI says). Plain `char` is **unsigned**, as on every RISC-V target. Floating point (`float`, `double`, floating literals), `auto` and `register` are errors.
- **Structs and unions** are laid out as clang does for ILP32 and LP64: each member at the next offset aligned for its type, the alignment that of the most aligned member, the size rounded up to it; every member of a union at offset 0. Tags (`struct s`) have their own namespace and block scope; `struct s;` declares an incomplete type, usable behind pointers until it is completed, and a definition inside a block makes a new type. Members are reached with `.` and `->`, nest, and may be arrays or structs; structs may be assigned (`a = b` copies the bytes), initialized from another struct, compared with nothing (as in C), passed and returned by value (see the calling convention below). A member of a `const` struct is `const`, and a struct with a `const` member cannot be assigned as a whole. Bit-fields, flexible array members and anonymous struct/union members (C11) are errors.
- **Enums**: `enum e { A, B = 5, C }`, constants with implicit or explicit (integer constant expression) values, which are `int` constants usable anywhere one is (array sizes, `case`). An enum type is `unsigned int` when no constant is negative and `int` otherwise, as clang chooses; every value must fit `int`, as C99 requires. An enum must be defined before `enum e` alone names it (C has no incomplete enums).
- **`typedef`** names any type, including function pointer and array types, and is scoped like a variable (an inner declaration may hide it). Redefining a typedef to the same type is allowed (C11, as clang does), to a different one an error.
- **Qualifiers**: `const` and `volatile` are accepted anywhere C allows them (`const char *s`, `int *const p`, `volatile int x`, and in a parameter's outermost array brackets, `int a[const 3]`, which qualify the pointer the parameter becomes: `int *const a`; `static` there, `int a[static 3]`, is accepted and ignored), and kept in the type: a `const` object, or one reached through a pointer to `const`, cannot be assigned, incremented or decremented, and a conversion may add qualifiers to what a pointer points to but not drop them ("... discards qualifiers"). Global and `static` `const` objects (not `const volatile`) go to `.rodata`. `volatile` needs no special code: merlin performs every read and write the source asks for, once, in order, and never caches a value in a register across statements, so no access to a `volatile` object is ever removed, merged or reordered.
- **Declarations**: one specifier list, then declarators with `*`s (and qualifiers), a name, `[N]`s, parameter lists and parentheses, so every C declarator works: `int *p[4]` (array of pointers), `int (*p)[4]` (pointer to array), `int (*f)(int)` (function pointer), `void (*table[3])(void)`, `int (*get(int))(char)` (a function returning a function pointer). An optional initializer follows (`int a = 1, *p = &a, v[4];`). Array sizes are integer constant expressions made of literals, enum constants, operators, casts and `sizeof` (a variable-length array, `int a[n]`, is an error). The parser sizes arrays before sema has typed anything, so for `sizeof expr` it works out the operand's type itself from what is declared so far: variables (an array of unknown size once a string literal, or a list of scalars without designators, has sized it), literals, casts and compound literals (one of unknown array size sized by its list the same way), and `*`, `&`, `[]`, `.`, `->`, `+` and `-` applied to those (`char buf[sizeof x]`, `int b[sizeof a / sizeof a[0]]`, `char c[sizeof p->name]`); any other operand there, a call for instance, is an error. Enumerator values and `_Static_assert` work the same way. `int a[] = {...}` and `char s[] = "..."` take their size from the initializer, unless an earlier declaration gave one (`int a[3]; int a[] = {1};` is an `int[3]`). A name is in scope inside its own initializer, as in C, for globals and `static` locals too (`static void *head[2] = {head, head};`). Functions cannot be declared inside a function. `_Static_assert(constant, "message");` (C11, which clang accepts in C99 mode too) is checked at file and block scope, and fails compilation with the message when the constant is 0.
- **Initializers**: a scalar takes an expression (optionally in braces); an array or struct a brace list, filled element by element or member by member (a union: its first member), or for a `char` array a string literal, or for a struct an expression of the same struct type. Inner braces may be left out (C's brace elision: `int m[2][2] = {1, 2, 3, 4};`, `struct pt line[2] = {1, 2, 3, 4};`). Designators (C99 6.7.8) name the subobject an item initializes, `.member` and `[constant]`, chained (`[1].at[0].y = 5`): initialization goes on from the subobject after the designated one, at its level, so `{[3] = 1, 2}` sets elements 3 and 4; a later item for the same subobject replaces the earlier one (only the part it names, for `.a.b = 1` after `.a = {...}`, and for `[0][3] = 'z'` after a string literal gave `[0]`, whose other characters stay; a designator into part of a struct that an expression initialized, `{ pv, .p.y = 9 }`, is an error, since C would keep the rest of `pv`); a union takes the member a designator names; an array of unknown size gets its size from the highest index. Missing elements are zero; too many are an error. Array designators go up to index 1048575 (each element up to the designated one is an entry in sema's list).
- **Functions**: `T f(T1 a, T2 b)` with at most 127 parameters (C99's translation limit), and a call with at most 127 arguments; arguments past `a0-a7` go on the stack (see the calling convention below). `void` functions and `return;`. `f(void)` and `f()` both mean no parameters, and so does `f(V)` with `typedef void V;` (C: one unnamed parameter of unqualified type `void`, however spelled). Array and function parameters are pointers (`int m[3][3]` is `int (*m)[3]`, `int f(int)` is `int (*f)(int)`). Prototypes may leave parameter names out; definitions may not. Arguments are converted to the parameter types as by assignment. `static` functions get no `.globl`. `inline` is accepted and changes no code: a `static inline` function is a `static` one, an `extern inline` one (or one that some declaration declares without `inline`) an ordinary external definition, and a C99 *inline definition* (every declaration `inline`, none `extern`) is compiled as a local function, without `.globl`, rather than left out as C allows.
- **Variadic functions**: `T f(T1 a, ...)`, with at least one named parameter (as C99 requires), declared, defined, called directly or through a pointer. The arguments `...` takes get the default argument promotions (`char` and `short` become `int`; arrays and functions pointers; structs go as they are). There are no system headers, so `<stdarg.h>` is spelled with clang's builtins, which a program wraps in its own macros (as `kernel/include/varargs.h` does): `__builtin_va_list` (a typedef for `void *`, a pointer walking the argument words), `__builtin_va_start(ap, last)` (`last` must be the last named parameter), `__builtin_va_arg(ap, T)` (for a complete object type that promotion leaves alone: `va_arg(ap, char)` is an error, as it would be undefined), `__builtin_va_end(ap)` and `__builtin_va_copy(dst, src)`. A `va_list` may be passed to another function (`vkprintf(fmt, ap)`) and used there.
- **Function pointers**: a function's name is a pointer to it wherever a value is needed (`f`, `&f` and `*f` are the same pointer, `.word f` in data); pointers to functions may be stored, passed, returned, compared, put in tables and structs, and called (`p(x)`, `(*p)(x)`, `table[i](x)`, `s->op(x)`) with the same argument checks as direct calls. They do not convert to or from `void *` or pointers to objects (C leaves that undefined) except through a cast.
- **Compound literals**: `(T){ initializer list }` inside a function is an unnamed object of type `T` (an array of unknown size takes its size from the list), initialized each time the expression is evaluated and living until the function returns; it is an lvalue (`&(int){1}`, `(struct pt){.x = 1}.x`, `(char *[]){"a", "b", 0}`). At file scope (in a global's initializer) one is an object with static storage, as C99 says, so its list must be constant like a global's: merlin makes it a hidden `static` variable named `.compoundliteral.<n>` (clang's name, numbered from 0 through the file), emitted with the globals, and the expression names it (`int *primes = (int[]){2, 3, 5};`, `struct pt *o = &(struct pt){0};`). Only its address is a constant: initializing a global with a literal's value (`int m = (int){5};`, a GNU extension clang accepts) is an error. In the operand of `sizeof`, which is not evaluated, a literal is no object and nothing is emitted for it.
- **Globals**: file-scope variables, `static` or not, and `extern` declarations; repeated declarations must agree on the type, and a tentative definition (`int x;`) counts once. Initializers must be constant: integer constant expressions, `0` for a null pointer, the address of a global, a function, or an element or member of a global (`&table[2]`, `table + 4`, `&s.field`), a string literal, or a brace list of those. As in clang, such an address converted to an integer type exactly as wide as a pointer (`long`, `unsigned long`, `intptr_t`; on RV32 `int` and `unsigned` too, on RV64 `long long` and `unsigned long long`) is still one, and adding or subtracting an integer moves it by that many bytes: `long a = (long)&x + 4;` is `.word x+4` on RV32, `.quad x+4` on RV64. Converted to a narrower or wider type (`(int)&x` on RV64, `(long long)&x` on RV32), or in any other arithmetic (`(long)&x * 2`, `4 - (long)&x`), it is not constant, as clang has it. A variable with an initializer goes to `.data`, one without to `.bss` (zero, as C requires), a `const` one to `.rodata`. A `static` local is a global too, named `func.name` (`func.name.1` for a second one with the same name), initialized once before the program starts.
- **Literals**: decimal, hex (`0x`) and octal (`0`) integers with `u`, `l` and `ll` (or `LL`) suffixes and their mixes (`ul`, `ull`, `LLU`...), typed by C99's rules (6.4.4.1): the first type that holds the value, from `int`, `long`, `long long` for a decimal literal and from those and their unsigned types in turn for a hex or octal one (`0xffffffff` is `unsigned int`), a suffix starting the list further on. So `2147483648` is a `long long` on RV32 and a `long` on RV64. A decimal literal past `LLONG_MAX` is an error (`too large to be represented in a signed integer type`; C99 gives it no type), and so is one past 64 bits. Character constants (`'a'`, type `int`) and string literals (`char[N]`, in `.rodata`, adjacent ones joined) with the escapes `\n \t \r \0 \\ \' \" \a \b \f \v \?`, octal `\ooo` and hex `\xhh`. String literals are read-only: storing into one is undefined. Trigraphs (`??=`, `??/`...), which C rewrites before reading tokens, are errors wherever they would change the meaning; write `\?` for a `?` that would start a trigraph.
- **Statements**: blocks, declarations (anywhere in a block, and in a `for` initializer), expression statements, `if`/`else`, `while`, `do`/`while`, `for`, `return`, `break`, `continue`, `switch` with `case` and `default` labels, labels and `goto`. A `switch` controls an integer expression (promoted; `case` values, integer constant expressions, are converted to its type and may not repeat); `case` and `default` may sit anywhere inside its body, nested statements included (Duff's device works), and control falls through from one to the next. `break` leaves the innermost loop or `switch`, `continue` the innermost loop. Labels belong to the whole function (each name once) and `goto` may jump to any of them, forward or backward, into or out of blocks and loops.
- **Expressions**: every C operator: `= += -= *= /= %= <<= >>= &= ^= |=`, `?:`, `,`, `|| &&`, `| ^ &`, `== != < <= > >=`, `<< >>`, `+ - * / %`, unary `- + ! ~ & * ++ --` (prefix and postfix), casts (to scalar types and `void`), `sizeof` (of a type or an expression, which is not evaluated; its result is C's `size_t`: `unsigned int` on RV32, `unsigned long` on RV64), indexing (`a[i]` is `*(a + i)`, so `i[a]` works too), member access `.` and `->`, and calls, direct or through any expression of function or function-pointer type, compound literals, and the `__builtin_va_*` builtins above.
- **Conversions**: integer promotion and the usual arithmetic conversions as in C, by rank and range (an `unsigned int` operand makes an `int` operation unsigned; on ILP32 `long` and `int` are both 32 bits, so `long` with `unsigned int` is `unsigned long`, and on LP64 it is `long`, which holds every `unsigned int`; `_Bool` promotes to `int`). Converting any scalar to `_Bool` gives 0 or 1 (`_Bool b = 256;` is 1). Arrays and functions decay to pointers. Pointer arithmetic counts in elements, and the difference of two pointers is a `ptrdiff_t`: an `int` on RV32, a `long` on RV64. Implicit conversions between integers and pointers (other than a null pointer constant) and between different pointer types (other than to and from `void *`) are errors where clang only warns; casts allow them. Converting an out-of-range value to a signed type keeps the low bytes, as clang does.
- Names must be declared before use, as in C99: a call to a function not yet declared is an error (no implicit declarations), and so is a wrong argument count. Block scoping follows C: inner blocks and `for` initializers may shadow outer names; parameters share the scope of the function body's outermost block.
- Statements and expressions nest at most 256 levels deep (each nested statement, `=`, unary operator, parenthesis, call argument or chained binary operator is a level); deeper input is an error. So is a declarator with more than 256 `*`s or 256 array dimensions, and a function whose locals need a frame over 2 GiB.
- Where C leaves behavior undefined, merlin defines it the way the hardware does: signed arithmetic wraps at the width of its type, `/` and `%` round toward zero (`div`/`rem`, so `INT_MIN / -1` wraps and division by zero gives RISC-V's results; `long long` division on RV32 gives the same, from the C library's routines), a shift uses the low 5 bits of its amount for a 32-bit type and the low 6 for a 64-bit one (`sll`/`srl`/`sra`, `sllw`/`srlw`/`sraw` for `int` on RV64), and falling off the end of a non-`void` function returns 0. Argument evaluation order is unspecified; tests must not depend on it.
- There is no inline `asm()`, on purpose: code that needs particular instructions (CSRs, `sfence.vma`, Xcal) lives in `.s` files and is called like any function, which the kernel already does.

merlin builds the whole Camelot kernel and its user programs (`kernel/*.c`, `kernel/user/*.c`; see "Kernel build"), and the result passes every kernel, filesystem and Grail kernel test (`kernel_sources_compile` in the harness also checks each file compiles and assembles on its own, every file on both widths: on RV64 the kernel's headers leave out their three `_Static_assert`s of RV32 structure sizes, `CPU_SIZE` until #154 and `THREAD_SIZE` and `PROC_SIZE` until #155, by `#if __riscv_xlen == 32`, and every other check, the filesystem's on-disk layouts among them, holds).

Code generation (`crates/merlin/src/codegen.rs`, whose module docs walk through it) is a stack machine: every expression leaves its value in `a0`, operands waiting for the other side are pushed on the stack, every variable lives in memory. The output is plain merlin-as source (`.text`/`.data`/`.bss`/`.section .rodata`, `.globl f`, `.type f, @function` (`@object` for a variable), `f:`, `.size f, . - f` after a function (`.size v, <bytes>` before a variable), local labels `.L.<what>.<n>`, string literals `.L.str.<n>`) that clang assembles to the same bytes and relocations in every section, on both widths. Values fill their registers: `char` and `short` are widened when loaded (`lbu`/`lb`, `lhu`/`lh`) and narrowed when stored (`sb`, `sh`); unsigned operations use `divu`, `remu`, `sltu` and `srl`; a binary operator's operands are converted to the type it computes in. Below, the numbers are RV32's; on RV64 a register, a pushed temporary, a saved register and an argument word are 8 bytes (`ld`/`sd`), as the bullets say. Functions follow the ILP32 or LP64 convention:

- RV64 keeps the *32-bit invariant*: an `int` or `unsigned int` lives in its register sign-extended from bit 31, whatever its signedness (so an `unsigned` 0xffffffff is -1 there), as the psABI wants of arguments and results and the hand-written assembly expects. `lw` loads it so; its arithmetic is the W instructions (`addw`, `subw`, `mulw`, `divw`, `divuw`, `remw`, `remuw`, `sllw`, `srlw`, `sraw`, `negw`, `addiw`), which sign-extend their 32-bit results; `and`/`or`/`xor`/`slt`/`sltu` are right on such values as they are. Converting a `long` or a pointer to a 32-bit type is `sext.w`; an `unsigned int` to `long` or `unsigned long`, or used as an index, is zero-extended (`slli`/`srli` by 32); an `int` needs nothing. `long` and pointers use `ld`/`sd`, and `li` takes 64-bit constants (counted as 8 instructions for branch relaxation). 8-byte data is `.quad`, as clang writes it.
- On RV32 a `long long` is a register pair, its low word in `a0` and its high word in `a1` (a binary operator's left operand in `a2:a3`), done inline as LLVM expands it with M: `add`/`sub` with a carry or borrow from `sltu`, `mul` with `mulhu`, comparisons on the high words and then the low ones unsigned, shifts by the low 6 bits of the amount in two halves (by whether it reaches 32), conversions by sign (`srai a1, a0, 31`) or 0. Division and remainder call libgcc's `__divdi3`, `__udivdi3`, `__moddi3` and `__umoddi3`, as clang's code does: the C library has them (`kernel/user/lib/libgcc.c`, with `__muldi3` for code built without M), and so does the kernel (`kernel/libgcc.c` includes it); they give RISC-V's results for division by zero and `LLONG_MIN / -1`. On RV64 a `long long` is a register, like a `long`.

- Frame: `s0` is the frame pointer, equal to `sp` on entry; the caller's stack arguments, if any, start at `0(s0)`. `ra` is saved at `-4(s0)`, the caller's `s0` at `-8(s0)` (on RV64 `-8(s0)` and `-16(s0)`), then the locals (parameters first, as numbered by `--dump-ast`, then the declared locals, then hidden ones for struct arguments and results) downward from there, each at the next offset aligned to its type (arrays and structs to at least a register, structs taking whole registers), so with only `int`s local `#i` is at `-(12 + 4*i)(s0)` (`-(20 + 4*i)` on RV64). The frame is rounded up to 16 bytes; a comment above each function lists every local's offset. Prologue and epilogue use `t0` for frames or offsets beyond a 12-bit immediate. A variadic function first stores the argument registers its named parameters leave free (`a<n>` to `a7`) at the top of its frame, right below `s0`, so that they and the caller's stack words form one run of words; `ra`, the caller's `s0` and the locals move down below them (`-4(s0)`..`-28(s0)` hold `a7`..`a1` for `int f(int n, ...)`, `ra` is at `-32(s0)`; on RV64 `-8(s0)`..`-56(s0)` and `-64(s0)`). `va_start` points the `va_list` at the first variadic word, and `va_arg` loads from it and steps past the argument's words (two for a struct of two words; a bigger struct's word is its copy's address; a pair aligned to 8 bytes, a `long long` on RV32, first rounds the pointer up to 8), as clang does.
- Arguments and results follow the RISC-V ILP32 or LP64 integer calling convention, as clang does (`crates/merlin/src/abi.rs`): a scalar takes one of `a0-a7`, widened by its type (to 32 bits, then on RV64 sign-extended to 64); a struct or union of up to one register (4 bytes, 8 on RV64) takes one register and of up to two registers two, holding its bytes as they lie in memory; a bigger one is copied by the caller and passed as the copy's address (the callee may change the copy). A `long long` on RV32 takes two registers, the low word first. A result comes back the same way in `a0` (and `a1`); a struct bigger than two registers is returned through memory: the caller passes the address for it as a hidden first argument in `a0` (moving the others up a register), and the callee stores it there. Words past `a7` go on the stack, from `0(sp)` up at the call, each argument in whole words (a pair may start in `a7` and end at `0(sp)`); the caller makes the room, keeping `sp` 16-byte aligned. Variadic arguments travel exactly like named ones, but for a pair aligned to two words (on RV32 a `long long`, or a struct holding one, which clang passes as an `i64`): variadic, it starts in an even register (an "aligned register pair", skipping `a7` for the stack if it must), and on the stack it starts at an even word either way, as LLVM's `CC_RISCVAssign2XLen` does. `tests/abi.rs` checks all of this against clang-compiled code, both ways, on both widths.
- Parameters are stored to their slots in the prologue (one on the stack loaded from above `s0` first). Arguments are evaluated left to right (C leaves the order unspecified) before any argument register is loaded, and `sp` is padded to a multiple of 16 around a call made while temporaries are pushed. With stack arguments, every argument is pushed, then the stack words are copied below them and the registers loaded, and one `addi` frees it all after the call. A call through a pointer is `jalr t1`.
- Structs are handled by address: an expression of struct type leaves the struct's address in `a0`, member access adds the member's offset, and assigning or passing copies the bytes in units of the struct's alignment (at most a register, so no access is misaligned), unrolled up to 16 units, a loop beyond.
- A local declared without an initializer is not cleared (C: indeterminate). A local array or struct with an initializer list is cleared first (register-wide stores, or a loop for more than 16 of them), then its elements or members are stored. A compound literal is a hidden local initialized the same way where it is evaluated.
- A `switch` is a chain of compares (`li t0, value` + `beq a0, t0, .L.case.<function>.<n>`, one per `case`, in source order; on RV32 a `long long` compares both words, `xor`, `or` and `beqz`), then a jump to `default` or past the body; the body follows unchanged, so cases fall through. Simple to read, and right for any set of values; a jump table would be faster for dense ones. `goto L` jumps to `.L.label.<function>.L`.
- A function or global may not be named like a register (`sp`, `a0`, `x5`, `fp`...), since merlin-as never reads those as symbols: it is a compile error. Names of instructions, pseudo-instructions or CSRs (`add`, `call`, `mstatus`) are fine.
- merlin-as does not relax branches, so codegen does: before printing a function it estimates every instruction's address (pseudo-instructions at their largest) and writes a conditional branch that may not reach its label (±4 KiB) as the opposite branch over a `j` (±1 MiB). Every `j` it writes stays inside its function, so a function must be under 1 MiB of code (estimated the same way); a bigger one is a merlin error naming the function.

Test programs live in `crates/merlin/tests/cases/*.c`, each starting with `// expect: N` (the exit code; a second line `// expect-lp64: N` when the sizes make RV64's differ), and programs of several files (C files sharing headers, and hand-written `.s` files, `NAME.rv32.s` and `NAME.rv64.s` for one width) in `crates/merlin/tests/multi/<name>/`, whose `main.c` starts with the expectation; every one runs on both widths; the ones in `crates/merlin/tests/cases-merlin/` rely on the definitions above for what C leaves undefined, so they are not checked against clang. The harness links them with a small `crt0.s` (the same for both widths: set `sp` to the top of RAM, `call main`, `xcal.halt a0`; plus `memset` and `memcpy`, which clang calls to clear large local arrays and copy large structs) and the C library's `libgcc.c`, and runs them in Grail, so the exit code is the low 8 bits of `main`'s return value. A reference interpreter in the harness (`tests/common/interp.rs`, byte-addressed memory with every object bounds-checked, values the numbers they are in the target's types) runs the checked syntax tree too, and must agree. `crates/merlin/tests/abi.rs` links merlin-compiled code with a committed clang-compiled object (`tests/abi/clang_side.o` for RV32 and `clang_side.rv64.o` for RV64, regenerated with `MERLIN_BLESS=1 cargo test -p merlin --test abi -- --ignored clang_side`) that pass structs of every size class, `int`, `unsigned`, `long`, `long long` and pointers mixed, arguments on the stack and variadic arguments to each other: among them a `long long` in a6:a7 and across a7, structs from odd stack words, pairs and `struct {long long}` after an odd number of named arguments (variadic, so from an even register), 9- and 10-byte structs aligned to 1 and 2 (by reference on RV32, two registers or a7 and the stack on RV64, named and variadic), `struct {char; long long}`, `unsigned int` arguments and results that clang takes as sign-extended on RV64, and pointers to functions returning `long long`.

### Object and executable format

**ELF64 (little-endian, EM_RISCV, `e_flags` 0: lp64 soft-float, no RVC)**, a subset. RV32 uses ELF32 with the same subset, until it is removed (#159); the steps of #141 bring each tool to ELF64:

- **The objects of RV64 code:**
  - lp64: `int` 32-bit; `long`, `long long` and pointers 64-bit;
  - code linked at 0x8000_0000 is built with `-mcmodel=medany` (section 1);
  - `.riscv.attributes` names `rv64i2p1_m2p0_a2p1_zicsr2p0_zifencei2p0_zmmul1p0_zaamo1p0_zalrsc1p0`;
  - 8-byte data that holds an address (`.dword`/`.quad`/`.8byte`) is `R_RISCV_64`;
  - debug information is DWARF 5 with 8-byte addresses and 32-bit offsets (`R_RISCV_32` for the offsets into `.debug_*` sections).
- Relocatable `.o`: sections `.text .data .rodata .bss`, `.symtab`, `.strtab`, `.rela.*`.
- Relocations needed: `R_RISCV_64` (RV64 data), `R_RISCV_32`, `R_RISCV_BRANCH`, `R_RISCV_JAL`, `R_RISCV_HI20`, `R_RISCV_LO12_I`, `R_RISCV_LO12_S`, `R_RISCV_PCREL_HI20`, `R_RISCV_PCREL_LO12_I/S`, `R_RISCV_CALL` and `R_RISCV_CALL_PLT` (what clang and merlin-as emit for `call`; without shared libraries it means the same). No linker relaxation: `R_RISCV_RELAX` hints are ignored, `R_RISCV_ALIGN` is an error (build with `-mno-relax`).
- Executable: `ET_EXEC`, program headers only for `PT_LOAD` (and a `PT_PHDR` when the headers are loaded, as ld.lld writes it), entry point in `e_entry`.

Why ELF rather than a custom format: it costs little more to write, and it means clang/lld, `llvm-objdump` and `llvm-readelf` can check our output and produce reference files, and riscv-tests ELFs load directly. A custom format can still be added later as an exercise.

All reading goes through one crate, `elf` (`crates/elf`: header, program headers, sections, symbols, `.rela`/`.rel` entries, RISC-V relocation names, DWARF line tables and compile units). It reads ELF32 and ELF64 alike, and keeps both after #159, as readelf and llvm-objdump read every class. The class comes from `e_ident[EI_CLASS]`; ELF64 widens addresses, offsets and sizes to 8 bytes and moves a few narrow fields so the wide ones stay aligned (`p_flags` second in a program header, `st_info`/`st_other`/`st_shndx` before `st_value` in a symbol, `r_info` = `sym << 32 | type` and an 8-byte addend in a relocation). Both classes are read into the same types, whose fields are `u64` (an `i64` addend), and each parsed file records its class. Its line tables take 4- or 8-byte addresses (`DW_LNE_set_address`, DWARF 5's `address_size`), the size of the file's class (another size is an error), and in an object it applies the relocations of `.debug_line` first: `R_RISCV_32` and `R_RISCV_64` (addresses), and the `ADD`/`SUB`/`SET` pairs of relaxed code, `R_RISCV_ADD64` and `R_RISCV_SUB64` included. `crates/elf/tests/llvm.rs` checks it against LLVM: an object, a relaxed object and a linked program, built from one C file by clang and ld.lld for each width, read back as `llvm-readelf` and `llvm-dwarfdump` print them (`scripts/gen-elf-golden.sh`). Grail's loader uses it for both classes (an ELF64 program makes the machine RV64); merlin-objdump reads both classes (#150), and so does merlin-dbg (#153), which takes the width from the machine and refuses a program of the other one; camelot-fs's bootable images (the bootloader and kernel it installs) accept ELF32 only until their RV64 step. merlin-ld reads its objects through it (`Elf::parse_sections_only`: a linker has no use for an object's program headers), its `src/object.rs` checking what merlin-ld accepts before and after the crate reads each part, and `strip_debug` reads the executables it takes apart with it. Writers stay in each tool.

### Assembler (`merlin-as`)

`merlin-as [-march=ISA] [-o out.o] input.s`. `-march=rv32ima_zicsr_zifencei`, the default until RV32 is removed (#159), assembles RV32 and writes ELF32; `-march=rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei` assembles RV64 and writes ELF64. Those are the only two values, clang's spelling of the ISA of section 1: any other (`rv64gc`, `rv32i`) is `merlin-as: unsupported -march 'rv64gc' (merlin-as takes rv32ima_zicsr_zifencei or rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei)` with exit status 2. Only the `=` form is read, as by clang (`-march ISA` is an unknown option), and the last one wins. The width is the whole file's and comes only from `-march`: an `.option` or `.attribute` that would change it is an error (see "Differences from clang"). As a library, `merlin_as::assemble_with` and `assemble_to_elf_with` take `Options { xlen }` (`parse_march` reads the option's value), and `assemble` and `assemble_to_elf` assemble RV32.

The reference is clang's integrated assembler with `-mno-relax` and the same `-march` (`--target=riscv32`, or `--target=riscv64 -mabi=lp64`; the examples are compared with builds for `rv32ima_zicsr_zifencei`; only `examples/smp` uses A instructions): for the same source, `llvm-objdump -dr` must print the same text for both objects (checked for every source in `examples/` at each width it is built for, which clang assembles with `-include examples/xcal.inc`: the RV64 builds' sources with `--target=riscv64 -march=rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei -mabi=lp64 -mcmodel=medany`), and every encoding matches LLVM's: `crates/merlin-as/tests/golden/encode.golden` for RV32 and `golden/rv64/encode.golden` for RV64 (`encode.s` again, then the RV64-only lines of `golden/rv64/encode.s`), and for `li` the corpora `golden/li.golden` (425 values) and `golden/rv64/li.golden` (2246 values). An RV64 object is checked against clang's field by field (`tests/elf64.rs`: header, sections, symbols, relocations, line table), and so are hello's words and relocations (`golden/rv64/hello.ref`) and the line tables (`tests/debug_line/rv64`). The encoder, the pseudo-instruction expansions and `li`'s choice of sequence are documented in `crates/merlin-as/ENCODER.md`.

- **The two widths** (section 4). RV64 adds `ld`, `lwu` and `sd` (and their pc-relative forms `ld rd, sym`, `lwu rd, sym`, `sd rs, sym, rt`), `addiw slliw srliw sraiw`, `addw subw sllw srlw sraw` and `mulw divw divuw remw remuw`, the `.d` atomics with every ordering suffix, and the pseudo-instructions `sext.w` (`addiw rd, rs, 0`), `negw` (`subw rd, zero, rs`) and `zext.w` (`slli rd, rs, 32` + `srli rd, rd, 32`, as LLVM expands it without Zba). Its shift amounts take 6 bits (`shift amount 64 out of range (0..=63)`); the W shifts, like every RV32 shift, take 5. On RV32 each RV64-only mnemonic is `unknown instruction 'mulw'`, as before. On RV64 the high counter halves are refused: `'rdcycleh' exists only on RV32 (on RV64, rdcycle reads all 64 bits)` (and `rdtimeh`, `rdinstreth`), and so are the names of the RV32-only CSRs of section 5, `CSR 'cycleh' exists only on RV32`; a CSR given by number is accepted on both widths, as LLVM does (`csrr a0, 0xc80`).
- **`li rd, value`** picks LLVM's sequence, `RISCVMatInt::generateInstSeq` for the base ISA (no C, Zba, Zbb or Zbs). On RV32 the value is a word, [-2^31, 2^32) (`li value 4294967296 does not fit in 32 bits` otherwise), built in 1 or 2 instructions. On RV64 it is any 64-bit value, built in 1 to 8 (`lui`, `addi`, `addiw`, `slli`, `srli`, `xori`). The length depends only on the value, so the assembler stays single pass, and 8 words is the most a `li` takes. The same line can differ between widths: `li a0, 0xffffffff` is `addi a0, zero, -1` on RV32, and `addi a0, zero, -1; srli a0, a0, 32` (zero-extended) on RV64; `li sp, 0x80400000` is one `lui` on RV32, and `addi sp, zero, 513; slli sp, sp, 22` on RV64.
- **`%hi`/`%lo` of a constant** are folded into the instruction: on RV64 any 64-bit value, `hi = ((v + 0x800) >> 12) & 0xfffff` and `lo` its low 12 bits sign-extended, as LLVM folds them; on RV32 the value must be a word (`%hi() value 4886718345 does not fit in 32 bits`).
- **Data**: `.byte`, `.half` (`.short`, `.2byte`), `.word` (`.long`, `.4byte`) and `.dword` (`.quad`, `.8byte`), on both widths, as LLVM. A number in N-byte data must fit as a signed or an unsigned value, in [-2^(8N-1), 2^(8N) - 1] (LLVM's rule: `.byte 255` and `.byte -1` are the same byte), or it is `data value 256 does not fit in 1 byte` (`... in 4 bytes`); a `.dword` takes any 64-bit value. A symbol in a `.word` becomes `R_RISCV_32` and in a `.dword` `R_RISCV_64`, whatever the width, since the data's size picks the relocation, as in LLVM (merlin-ld applies `R_RISCV_64` from #149 on); in 1- or 2-byte data it is `only .word and .dword can hold a symbol address`.
- **`.space n[, fill]`** (`.zero`, `.skip`) makes `n` bytes of `fill`'s low byte (zero without one: `.space 3, 0x1ff` is three 0xff), as LLVM does; a negative `n` is `invalid number of bytes`, LLVM's error. A section holds at most 4 GiB - 1 bytes, since its size and offsets are 32-bit (ELF32's limit, and merlin-as's on RV64 too): whatever would take it further (`.space 0x100000003`, data after `.space 0xffffffff` in `.bss`, a `.lcomm`) is `section .bss would be 4 GiB or larger`, where LLVM on riscv32 writes the size's low 32 bits.
- **Expressions** are computed in 64 bits, wrapping around, and `>>` is a logical shift, as in LLVM (MCAsmInfo's `UseLogicalShr`, true by default and left on by RISC-V): `-16 >> 2` is 0x3fff_ffff_ffff_fffc, so `.word -16 >> 2` is an error. A `.equ`/`.set` value is not range checked where it is defined (LLVM checks none), only where it is used: an immediate, `li`, data.

- **Single pass.** No instruction's size depends on a label's address; `.equ`/`.set` constants must be defined before use. Every label reference is first recorded as a fixup with its immediate left zero.
- **Local labels are resolved by the assembler**, as clang does with `-mno-relax`. When the file has been read, a pc-relative reference to a label that is local (not `.globl`, also when `.globl` comes later in the file) and defined in the same section is patched in and gets no relocation: branches, `jal`/`j`, `call`/`tail` (both words), and `auipc` + `%pcrel_lo` pairs (`la`, `lla`, `lw rd, sym`, `sw rs, sym, rt`, on RV64 `ld rd, sym`, `lwu rd, sym` and `sd rs, sym, rt`, explicit `%pcrel_hi`/`%pcrel_lo`). A branch or jump out of range is an error on its line. Everything else stays a relocation for the linker: references to global or undefined symbols, to labels in other sections, and absolute ones (`%hi`, `%lo`, `.word sym`, `.dword sym`).
- **`.`** is the location counter: the address of the instruction (its first word, for a pseudo-instruction) or data word it appears in. Each use becomes a hidden local label there, so `j .`, `beq a0, a1, .-8` and `la a0, .` resolve like any local label, and `.word .` gets an `R_RISCV_32` against that label (clang's `.L0`), `.dword .` an `R_RISCV_64`.
- **Symbols**: `.L` labels (numeric labels like `1:`, hidden `.Lpcrel_hiN` labels on `auipc`s, `.Ldot$N` labels for `.`) are written to `.symtab` only when a relocation refers to them, as in clang, and so is a section's symbol (`STT_SECTION`, for debug information), which is made with its section, where LLVM registers it. The locals are in the order the source makes them, clang's: a label where it is defined, or first named (`.globl`, `.local`, `.type`, an operand: LLVM's `registerSymbol`), the mapping symbols among them; `.size` names none (LLVM's `emitELFSize` registers nothing), so a symbol sized before its label is placed at the label, and one only `.size` names is not in the table. `.local` keeps an undefined symbol local, as clang writes it (a local `UND` symbol), where any other undefined symbol is global. `.file "t.c"` (a name without a number) is a local `STT_FILE` symbol, absolute, of value 0, each one (a name given twice too): the first is the object's first local symbol, before those made earlier, the others where the source has them, as LLVM's `ELFObjectWriter` places them; `.file N "dir" "name"`, a file of the line table, makes none. `.strtab` holds each name once.
- **Mapping symbols** (the psABI's, as clang 23 writes them, LLVM's `RISCVELFStreamer`): every section marks where its contents change kind with a local `STT_NOTYPE` symbol of size 0, `$x<isa>` before a run of instructions and `$d` before a run of data, `<isa>` being the object's `Tag_RISCV_arch` (`$xrv64i2p1_m2p0_a2p1_zicsr2p0_zifencei2p0_zmmul1p0_zaamo1p0_zalrsc1p0` on RV64). A section starts with neither and going back to it carries on where it stopped (`.data` after `.text` and back is no new `$x`). Every instruction (`.insn` too, in any section) asks for code, and every data directive for data, even one that writes nothing (`.zero 0`, `.ascii ""`; `.byte` alone writes nothing and asks nothing), `.comm`'s and `.lcomm`'s zeros in `.bss` included; alignment padding asks for neither, so a `.p2align` between code and data stays in the run before it. Every `$x` names `-march`'s ISA: an `.option arch` or `.attribute arch` that would name another, and so a new or another `$x` in clang, is an error (see "Differences from clang"), and `.option push`/`pop` change nothing. The line table's `.debug_line` and `.debug_line_str` and `.riscv.attributes`, which clang writes at the end, have their `$d`s too, in that order: `.riscv.attributes` first. A mapping symbol comes after the labels its line defines (a `.Lpcrel_hiN` on an `auipc`, the label of a `.` in an operand or a `.word`: clang makes them when it reads the line) and before the symbols the line is the first to name (`j fwd`, `.word fwd`), so the symbol table is clang's, entry for entry. `crates/merlin-as/tests/mapping.rs` checks them against clang's objects on both widths: of `tests/mapping/*.s` (the whole symbol table, and merlin-objdump's listing against llvm-objdump's, `scripts/gen-mapping-golden.sh`), of the line table corpus and of the encoder's.
- **`.insn`** spells out an instruction by fields, with LLVM's forms and encodings: `.insn r opcode, funct3, funct7, rd, rs1, rs2`, `.insn i opcode, funct3, rd, rs1, imm` (or `rd, imm(rs1)`), `.insn s opcode, funct3, rs2, imm(rs1)`, `.insn b opcode, funct3, rs1, rs2, target`, `.insn u opcode, rd, imm20`, and `.insn value` / `.insn 4, value`. Opcodes must be 32-bit ones (low bits `11`). The examples write the Xcal instructions as mnemonics (`xcal.dbg a0`); clang does not know them, so `examples/xcal.inc` defines them as assembler macros that expand to the `.insn` with the same encoding, and clang reference builds pass `-include examples/xcal.inc`.
- **Strings** (`.ascii`, `.asciz`, `.string`) take the escapes `\n \t \r \b \f \\ \" \'`, hex `\xhh` and octal `\ooo` (one to three digits, at most `\377`), as GNU as and llvm-mc read them: clang writes a byte it can't print as an octal escape (`"\377"`, `"a\200"`), and bytes 8 and 12 as `\b` and `\f` (clang 23's switch tables in `/bin/ld`).
- **`.type sym, @function` / `@object`** sets a symbol's type, and **`.size sym, expr`** its size: a constant, or `.` and labels already defined combined with `+` and `-` so that the labels of each section cancel out (`.size f, . - f`, which clang and merlin write after every function). Any value is a size, as in LLVM: its bits are the symbol's `st_size`, all 64 in an ELF64 object and the low 32 in an ELF32 one (on RV32 `.size f, 0x100000000` is 0 and `.size f, -4` is 0xfffffffc, as clang writes them). The same goes for data: `.word .Lend - f` (or `.dword`) is the distance, a number (merlin-as never moves code once placed); a lone label is a relocation.
- **Sections** have the type, flags and entry size LLVM gives them (`src/section.rs`, LLVM's `ELFAsmParser`), so the section headers are clang's. `.section name[, "flags"[, @type[, entsize]]]`: a name LLVM knows by its prefix has its flags and type without being told (`.text*` `AX`, `.rodata*` `A`, `.data*` `WA`, `.bss*` `WA` and `NOBITS`, `.init_array` `INIT_ARRAY`, `.note*` `NOTE`, ...), and any other name none, so it is not loaded: `.section .sdata` alone is not `.data`, as in LLVM (write `.section .sdata, "aw"`). The flags string adds to the name's: letters (`a` alloc, `w` write, `x` exec, `M` merge, `S` strings, `T` TLS, `e` exclude, `R` retain) or a number (`"0x3"`); the type replaces the name's, written `@progbits`, `%progbits` or `"progbits"` (`progbits`, `nobits`, `note`, `init_array`, `fini_array`, `preinit_array`, or a number); a mergeable section must give its type and then its entry size, at least 1 and at most 32 bits (`.section .rodata.str1.1, "aMS", @progbits, 1`, clang's C string literals, is `AMS` with entries of 1 byte). The sections LLVM makes before the first line keep their own attributes: `.debug_str` and `.debug_line_str` are `MS` with entries of 1, `.rodata.cst4` (8, 16, 32) `AM` with entries of 4 (8, 16, 32). Going back to a section with nothing after its name takes it as it is; with flags, a type or an entry size that would change it, it is LLVM's error, `changed section flags for .foo, expected: 0x2` (also `type` and `entsize`). `.pushsection`, `.popsection` and `.previous` are not supported (`unknown directive '.pushsection'`), nor are section groups (`G`), linked-to sections (`o`) and `unique` (`.section: flag 'G': section groups are not supported`). `.align` pads an executable section as LLVM's `writeNopData` does, a zero byte to an even offset, a `c.nop` (`01 00`) to a multiple of 4, then `nop`s, and any other with zeros; `.p2align n, fill, max` (`.align` too) and `.balign bytes, fill, max` take LLVM's fill and maximum: an explicit fill pads with its low byte, in code too (`.byte 1; .p2align 2, 0` is `01 00 00 00`), the fill may be left out (`.p2align 3,,4`), a padding longer than the maximum is left out while the section's alignment is still raised, a maximum below 1 is LLVM's error (`alignment directive can never be satisfied in this many bytes, ignoring maximum bytes expression`) and one of at least the alignment changes nothing (LLVM warns; merlin-as has no warnings), and instructions are allowed anywhere but in a `NOBITS` section and in debug information (`instructions are not allowed in .bss`). `.text` is 4-aligned from the start, as LLVM makes it before the first line; every other section, `.text.start` and `"ax"` ones included, starts 1-aligned, as in clang, and is raised only by `.align` (and `.lcomm`).
- **Debug information**: sections named `.debug_*` are not loaded (no `SHF_ALLOC`) and hold data only. A `.word` there naming a `.L` label becomes an `R_RISCV_32` against the label's section symbol (`STT_SECTION`) plus its offset, and a `.dword` (`.quad`, as clang's RV64 `.debug_info` writes addresses) an `R_RISCV_64`, so the labels compilers make need no symbols. `.file N ["dir"] "name" [md5 0x...]` and `.loc FILE LINE [COLUMN] [options]` become a DWARF 5 line table: see "Debug information" below.
- **clang's C output** (`clang -S`, which the clang reference build of the kernel's user programs is, see "Kernel build"): `.local sym` then `.comm sym, size, align` (how clang writes a `static` variable without an initializer), and `.lcomm sym, size`, reserve `size` zero bytes for `sym` in `.bss` at that alignment (`.lcomm`: 1), as an object symbol of that size, without changing the current section. As in LLVM, `.lcomm` takes no alignment (`alignment not supported on this target`), a negative size is `size must be non-negative` and an alignment that is not a power of 2 `alignment must be a power of 2`; merlin-as's own limit is an alignment of at most 65536, as for `.p2align`. A `.comm` for a symbol that is not `.local` would be a common symbol, which merlin-ld rejects, so it is an error (compile with `-fno-common`). `.set name, label+offset` (or `name = label+offset`) with a label defined earlier in the file makes `name` a label at that address in the label's section (the sum is computed in 64 bits, wrapping around, and must fall in the section, at most 4 GiB - 1 past its start: `.set y, x - 1` with `x` at the section's start is `.set: 'y' would be outside its section`, where LLVM writes the value's low bits, 0xffffffff on riscv32): clang names the parts of the block it merges a file's `static` variables into (`.L_MergedGlobals`) this way. `.addrsig` and `.addrsig_sym` are ignored, like `.ident`; `.option` and `.attribute` must not name another ISA (see "Differences from clang").
- **ELF**: an RV32 object is ELF32, an RV64 one ELF64, from one writer (`src/elf.rs`): class 2, `e_flags` 0, the 64-byte header and section headers, 24-byte symbols (`st_info`, `st_other`, `st_shndx` before the 8-byte `st_value` and `st_size`; a constant's value is its 64 bits) and 24-byte relocations (`r_info` = `sym << 32 | type`, the whole 64-bit addend), with `.rela.*` and `.symtab` 8-aligned (`sh_addralign` 8, `sh_entsize` 0x18) and placed at 8-byte file offsets, as are the section headers. The sections are in clang's order, the source's, then `.riscv.attributes`, then the line table's if the source did not make them, but clang puts `.strtab` first, with the section names in it (merlin-as writes a `.shstrtab` of its own), and each `.rela.X` after its section: the files differ as wholes, and no tool cares.
- **`.riscv.attributes`**: every object carries clang's `Tag_RISCV_arch` for its `-march`: `rv32i2p1_m2p0_a2p1_zicsr2p0_zifencei2p0_zmmul1p0_zaamo1p0_zalrsc1p0` for `rv32ima_zicsr_zifencei`, `rv64i2p1_m2p0_a2p1_zicsr2p0_zifencei2p0_zmmul1p0_zaamo1p0_zalrsc1p0` for `rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei`, so llvm-objdump decodes M and A instructions without `--mattr`.
- **Differences from clang**, on purpose:
  - No branch relaxation. clang rewrites a conditional branch it cannot resolve or reach (to a global or undefined symbol, another section, or beyond ±4 KiB) into the inverted branch over a `jal`, even with `-mno-relax`; merlin-as keeps one instruction with `R_RISCV_BRANCH`, or reports the branch out of range. (LLVM no longer has an option that turns this off, so `scripts/gen-encode-golden.sh` takes the golden lines of such branches from `llvm-mc -show-encoding`, which prints the instruction and its fixup as written, before relaxation, and assembles the rest with clang.)
  - A numeric label that a relocation refers to is named `.L<n>$<k>` (the k-th definition of `n:`); clang names it `.Ltmp<m>`.
  - `.insn j` is not accepted: LLVM (21, and still 23) packs its numeric offset without the J-type bit scrambling (`.insn j 0x6f, a0, 2048` gives `0040056f`, `jal a0, 0x4`).
  - Register names are never symbols: `lw a0, a1` is an error, where LLVM reads `a1` as a symbol.
  - Some operand forms LLVM accepts are not: `jalr rd, rs` and `jalr off(rs)` (write `jalr rd, 0(rs)`, `jalr ra, off(rs)`), `jr off(rs)` and `jr rs, off` (write `jalr zero, off(rs)`), `call rd, sym`, `jump sym, rt`, `%hi(sym)` in `.insn u`, named opcodes in `.insn` (`.insn r OP, ...`), upper-case CSR names (`MSTATUS`) and standard CSRs Excalibur doesn't have (`fflags`). The other way round, `call`/`tail` accept `sym+offset` and `.`, where clang wants a bare symbol name.
  - LLVM takes an immediate in a register operation and assembles the immediate form: `add a0, a1, 5` is `addi`, and on RV64 `addw a0, a1, 5` is `addiw` and `sllw a0, a1, 3` is `slliw`. merlin-as wants the immediate mnemonic (`expected a register, found an immediate`).
  - `li rd, %lo(sym)` (LLVM's `addi rd, zero, %lo(sym)` with `R_RISCV_LO12_I`) is an error (`li takes a constant`), and `li rd, %hi(c)` of a constant is accepted, where LLVM refuses it.
  - On RV32, `%hi`/`%lo` of a constant must be a word (`%hi() value 4886718345 does not fit in 32 bits`); LLVM folds any 64-bit value on both widths, dropping the bits above 31, as merlin-as does on RV64.
  - `sext.b`, `sext.h` and `zext.h` are not provided (LLVM expands them without Zbb into two shifts by XLEN - 8 or XLEN - 16); `zext.b` (`andi rd, rs, 255`) and, on RV64, `sext.w` and `zext.w` are.
  - The width and the extensions come only from `-march`. In LLVM `.option arch` (a full ISA string, or `+ext`/`-ext` items) and `.option rvc` change the ISA of the code after them, with a new `$x<isa>` whenever the ISA string changes (`.option arch, +zicond` makes `$xrv32i2p1_m2p0_a2p1_zicond1p0_...`, `.option pop` back), and `.attribute arch` (or `.attribute 5`) sets `Tag_RISCV_arch` and the name of every `$x`. merlin-as has no extensions besides `-march`'s, so one of them that names another ISA is an error (`.option arch: merlin-as assembles only -march's ISA (rv32i2p1_m2p0_a2p1_zicsr2p0_zifencei2p0_zmmul1p0_zaamo1p0_zalrsc1p0), and this names another`), and one that names the same ISA, in any spelling LLVM takes (`rv32ima_zicsr_zifencei`, clang `-S`'s `.attribute 5, "rv32i2p1_..."`, `.option arch, +m` or `-a`, which LLVM's implications leave the same), is accepted and changes nothing (`src/isa.rs`, LLVM's `RISCVISAInfo` for these extensions: `m` implies `zmmul`, `a` implies `zaamo` and `zalrsc`, which together make `a`); LLVM's own errors come first (`can't disable zmmul extension; m extension requires zmmul extension`, `extension version number parsing not currently implemented` for `+m2p0`). The other attributes (`.attribute 4, 16`, the stack alignment) and options (`push`, `pop`, `norvc`, `relax`, `norelax`, `pic`, `nopic`) are ignored.
  - The error messages are merlin-as's own (`data value 256 does not fit in 1 byte` where LLVM says `out of range literal value`; `'rdcycleh' exists only on RV32 ...` and `CSR 'cycleh' exists only on RV32` where it says `instruction requires the following: RV32I Base Instruction Set` and `system register 'cycleh' is RV32 only`).

### Linker (`merlin-ld`)

`merlin-ld a.o b.o [--start-lib l1.o l2.o --end-lib] [-T link.ld] [-e entry] [-o out.elf] [--Map out.map] [-S] [@file]`, where `@file` stands for the words of the file (a response file, as GNU ld and ld.lld read them, without quoting; one cannot name another): how a link line longer than a shell takes is given, on Camelot as on the host ("Self-hosting"). The reference is ld.lld: for the same objects and script, the allocated sections (names, types, addresses, sizes, alignments, A/W/X flags), the `PT_LOAD`s (file offsets, load addresses and alignments included), the `PT_PHDR` and symbol values must match, and so must every loaded byte but the ELF and program headers themselves, except for the differences listed at the end of this subsection. This is checked against ld.lld links committed with their `llvm-readelf` and `llvm-objdump -d` output (`crates/merlin-ld/tests/fixtures`, made by clang and LLD 23.1.4 and regenerated with `cargo xtask ld-fixtures`, or with `--out DIR` into a scratch copy, to `diff -r` with the committed one), in two sets: RV32's, and RV64's in `fixtures/rv64`, assembled with `--target=riscv64 -march=rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei -mabi=lp64 -mcmodel=medany` (its own `relocs` and `at`, since RV64 code does not reach what RV32 code does, and copies of the RV32 sources that link and run the same on both widths). They include layout edge cases (empty and assignment-only output sections, gaps, `NOBITS` before `PROGBITS`, a first section not at the start of a page, the headers loaded), load addresses (`AT`) and every relocation type at the edges of its range; every fixture also runs in Grail. Both sets' disassembly is compared with merlin-objdump's (where ld.lld makes a script-assigned symbol relative to a section, merlin-ld's is `SHN_ABS`, see below, so a label, or the name of a target outside every section, may differ at the same address). riscv-tests checks the rest: every program is relinked from its object (`tests/riscv-tests/obj`, and `tests/riscv-tests/rv64/obj` for RV64) and compared with the committed ld.lld executable, every program header field for RV64, whose executables the same LLD linked.

- **Inputs**: `ET_REL`, `EM_RISCV`, ELFCLASS32 (RV32) or ELFCLASS64 (RV64), little-endian, `e_flags` 0 (ilp32 or lp64 soft-float, no RVC). Every object of a link has one class, which is the executable's (there is no option for it, as ld.lld needs none); one of another class is ld.lld's error, `b.o is incompatible with a.o`, and nothing is linked. As in ld.lld, every one is reported, in command-line order, library members too, needed or not (ld.lld checks its lazy objects), and compared with the script's `OUTPUT_FORMAT` if it names one, else with the first file of the class that is not a library member; before there is one the class came from a member, and ld.lld names nothing: `a.o is incompatible`. ld.lld reads the script when it meets `-T`, before any object, and compares the classes only when everything read, so merlin-ld reports the script's errors first, then every object's, and the classes only after both: a broken script or an unknown `OUTPUT_FORMAT` is reported, not hidden by an object of the other class. Allocated sections must be `PROGBITS` or `NOBITS`. As in ld.lld, the `sh_addralign` of a section with code or data (not of the symbol and string tables or the relocations) must be a power of two (0 means 1) that fits in 32 bits, so at most 2^31 (`section '.data' has sh_addralign 0xc, not a power of 2`), and the symbol table's `sh_size` a multiple of its `sh_entsize`. Rejected with an error: `SHT_REL`, section groups/COMDAT, TLS, common symbols (`-fno-common`), extended section indices. Non-allocated sections are dropped (`.riscv.attributes`, `.comment`), except debug information (`.debug_*`), which is kept: see "Debug information". Mergeable sections (`SHF_MERGE`, e.g. clang's `.rodata.str1.1` for C string literals) are placed like any other section: see the differences below. Build clang objects with `-mno-relax`: there is no linker relaxation.
- **Symbols**: locals stay per object. Two strong definitions of a global are an error (`b.o: duplicate symbol 'foo' (first defined in a.o)`); a strong definition beats a weak one; an undefined weak symbol is 0. An undefined global nobody defines is an error (`a.o: undefined reference to 'foo'`). Symbols assigned in the linker script override object definitions (as in ld.lld).
- **Libraries**: the objects between `--start-lib` and `--end-lib` (ld.lld's options, which make them behave like the members of an archive) are linked only when needed: a member is pulled in when it defines a global that an object already in the link refers to and nothing earlier defines, and what it refers to may pull in more members, until nothing changes. A weak reference pulls nothing in (it stays 0, as with an archive). The members that are needed are linked after every other input, in library order. `merlin_ld::link_files` (a command line's files in its order, each marked as a member or not: what the command line does), `merlin_ld::link_with_library` (the objects, then the members) and `merlin_ld::library::needed` do the same for a caller that has the objects in memory (`cargo xtask kernel` links each user program with the C library this way). Nested or unbalanced `--start-lib`/`--end-lib`, or a link with only library members, is an error.
- **Default layout** (no `-T`): `.text` at `0x8000_0000` (`.text.start` and `.text.init` first), then `.rodata` (+ `.srodata`), `.data` (+ `.sdata`), `.bss` (+ `.sbss`), each aligned to its most-aligned input. Other allocated sections need a script. The addresses are the same on both widths; on RV64 that takes pc-relative code only (`-mcmodel=medany`, section 1), since `lui %hi(x)` cannot reach 0x8000_0000 there (see "Relocations"). It is merlin-ld's own, not what ld.lld does without a script (which starts at 0x10000), and it is a script too (`script::DEFAULT_SCRIPT`).
