- **Linker scripts**, a subset (anything else is an error naming what it found): `OUTPUT_ARCH(riscv)`, `OUTPUT_FORMAT(elf32-littleriscv)` or `OUTPUT_FORMAT(elf64-littleriscv)` (one argument or three, `OUTPUT_FORMAT(default, big, little)`, as ld.lld takes them, and the `-freebsd` names too), `ENTRY(sym)`, `MEMORY` with one region, and `SECTIONS` containing `. = expr;`, `. += expr;`, `sym = expr;` and output sections `name [(NOLOAD)] : [AT(lma)] [ALIGN(n)] { *(pattern ...) ... } [> REGION]`, `AT` before `ALIGN` as ld.lld takes them (the other order is an error). `OUTPUT_FORMAT` names the executable's class, and then every object must have it: one of the other class is ld.lld's `a.o is incompatible with elf32-littleriscv` (or `elf64-littleriscv`), each such object reported; as in ld.lld only the first argument counts, but every one must be one of the two names. `ALIGN(n)` (a power of two, at most 2^31 on both widths: ld.lld keeps an output section's alignment in 32 bits on RV64 too, and ignores a larger one) makes the section start at least that aligned, as if an input wanted it; `(NOLOAD)` makes it `NOBITS` (memory, no file bytes) whatever its inputs hold, as ld.lld does. `/DISCARD/ : { *(pattern ...) }` throws the sections it matches away (the kernel's script drops `.comment` and the like this way; its `(NOLOAD)` and `AT` are read and mean nothing, as in ld.lld, which does not even evaluate the `AT`, and its `ALIGN` is checked as any other's); a relocation against a symbol in a discarded section is an error, and global symbols defined there are left out. Expressions: numbers (`0x..`, decimal, `K`/`M` suffixes), `.`, symbols already placed, `ALIGN(n)`, `ORIGIN(r)`, `LENGTH(r)`, `ADDR(section)`, `+`, `-`, parentheses. Names (of output sections, input patterns, the symbols assignments set, `ENTRY`'s symbol, regions, formats, and what `ADDR`, `ORIGIN` and `LENGTH` take) have `-` among their characters, as ld.lld's lexer reads them outside expressions (`OUTPUT_FORMAT(elf64-littleriscv)` unquoted, `*(.text-x)`), while in an expression `a-b` is `a - b`. Every `ALIGN(n)`, in an expression or on a section, is read as ld.lld's `checkAlignment` reads it: 0 is 1, and any other value that is not a power of two is ld.lld's error, `ALIGN(3): alignment must be power of 2`. Inside an output section `.` may only move forward with `ALIGN`, `+=` or an expression using `.` (moving it backward is an error, after which it still takes the new value, as in ld.lld). At the top level it moves anywhere, as ld.lld moves it: backward, and on ELF64 round 2^64 (`. += n` and `ALIGN` wrap, and so does a section's alignment of its start); sections that end up over each other are ld.lld's error, `section .text virtual address range overlaps with .data (.text is [0x80000000, 0x8000000f], .data is [0x80000008, 0x80000027])`.
  - **Load addresses**: `AT(lma)` loads a section somewhere other than the address it runs at: its segment's `p_paddr`, where a loader (Grail, the bootloader) puts its bytes. A kernel linked in the top 2 GiB of the address space, where paging maps it, is loaded in RAM this way: Linux's riscv `vmlinux.lds` writes `AT(ADDR(.text) - LOAD_OFFSET)`. As in ld.lld, `.` in the `AT` expression is the section's start, once aligned (`AT(. - OFFSET)` is the same thing); the difference between the load address and the address (modulo 2^64) carries on to every later section without an `AT`, `NOLOAD` and empty ones too, so one `AT` on the first section moves the whole image; a dropped section (see "Placement") evaluates nothing, its `AT` included; file offsets follow the addresses, not the load addresses, and the entry point is an address. Sections with file contents must not be loaded over each other: `section .text load address range overlaps with .rodata (.text is [0x80000000, 0x80000027], .rodata is [0x80000000, 0x8000000b])`, ld.lld's error with its ranges on the same line.
  - **`ADDR(.x)`** is the address of an output section already placed, or of the one being placed (in its `AT` and its assignments; not in its `ALIGN`, which its start depends on). Of a section placed later it is an error, `section '.x' is used before it is placed`, and of no section ld.lld's `undefined section .x`. `ADDR(/DISCARD/)` is 0, as in ld.lld, when the script has a `/DISCARD/` (and ld.lld's `undefined section /DISCARD/` when it does not).
  - **Values** are as wide as the executable's addresses. On ELF64 (RV64) they are 64-bit, numbers up to 2^64 - 1, and `+` and `-` wrap modulo 2^64, as in ld.lld (`0 - 1` is `0xffffffffffffffff`); a section must end below 2^64 (ld.lld's `section .x at 0x... of size 0x... exceeds available address space`), and so must the MEMORY region and every load address range. On ELF32 (RV32, until #159) they are exact, as merlin-ld has always computed them (numbers up to 2^63 - 1, `arithmetic overflow` past that), and the 4 GiB address space is checked wherever a value is used: the location counter, script symbols, section ends, the MEMORY region, the entry point and load address ranges must stay within it. A section past it is ld.lld's error too, with the words it uses on ELF64 (`section .text at 0x100000000 of size 0x10 exceeds available address space`); the other values ld.lld truncates to 32 bits (see the differences below). After a section past the address space, the location counter goes on from its end (wrapped on ELF64), as in ld.lld, so only the sections that really overflow are reported.
- **Placement**: in script order, the first `*(pattern)` that matches an input section takes it; within one `*(...)`, inputs keep command-line order and file order. An allocated input section no rule matches is an error if it is non-empty or defines a global. An empty one (the empty `.text` of an object without code, say) is an orphan, placed as ld.lld places one (`addOrphanSections`, `findOrphanPos`): orphans of one name make one output section, or join the script's output section of that name at its end; a new one goes next to the output section with inputs whose sort rank (`getSectionRank`: read-only, executable, writable and executable, RELRO, writable, `NOBITS` last within each) shares the most leading bits with its own, after it if that one ranks no higher (always, with `MEMORY`), else before it, past symbol assignments, at the very end if no output section with inputs follows, and never before a first `. = ...` that comes before every section. Like any output section with an input it is kept although it is empty: it aligns the location counter to its alignment, and between sections of other permissions it is a run of its own, which counts in the headers' room (see "The headers"). Each input is aligned to its `sh_addralign`, also when it is empty; an output section starts aligned to its largest input alignment. As in ld.lld, an output section is dropped only when no input section matched it (not even an empty one), it contains no assignments, no `ADDR(...)` anywhere in the script names it and its `AT` and `ALIGN` use no symbol (a dropped section evaluates nothing: its `AT` sets no load address); one that holds only assignments (`. += n`, `sym = .`) is `PROGBITS` with the `SHF_ALLOC` and `SHF_WRITE` flags of the previous output section that had inputs (never `SHF_EXECINSTR`, as in ld.lld: after the code it goes with the data that follows), so `.stack : { . += 4K; }` takes file space (a top-level `. += 4K;` reserves memory without it). The location counter starts at 0, also with `MEMORY` (a symbol assigned before the first output section gets 0, as in ld.lld). With `MEMORY`, every output section goes at the region's next free address, as in ld.lld, which means a top-level `. = ...` before a section would be ignored: merlin-ld reports it as an error instead (put the `ALIGN` inside the section). Overflowing the region is an error.
- **Entry**: `-e` (symbol or number), else `ENTRY(...)`, else `_start`. A missing `-e` symbol is an error; otherwise a warning, and the entry falls back to the first executable section.
- **Output**: `ET_EXEC` of the objects' class: ELF32 for RV32, ELF64 for RV64 (a 64-byte ELF header with 8-byte `e_entry`, `e_phoff` and `e_shoff`, 56-byte program headers with `p_flags` second, 64-byte section headers, 24-byte symbols, and `.symtab` and the section header table 8-aligned, as ld.lld aligns them); `e_flags` 0 on both. One `PT_LOAD` per run of consecutive output sections with the same permissions (R, RX, RW), as ld.lld does with a `SECTIONS` command and no `PHDRS`: address gaps and `NOBITS` sections inside a run do not split it but are written as zeros, so only `NOBITS` sections at the end of a run (`.bss`) give `p_memsz > p_filesz`. A run of empty sections has no `PT_LOAD`. A section with an `AT` starts a `PT_LOAD` of its own, even right after one with the same permissions, so all the sections of a segment are loaded at the same distance from their addresses, and `p_paddr` is the load address of the segment's first section (`p_vaddr` without `AT`). So does the section after a run of RELRO sections, the writable ones ld.lld makes read-only after relocation (`-z relro`, its default; `isRelroSection`): `.data.rel.ro`, `.bss.rel.ro`, `.ctors`, `.dtors`, `.jcr`, `.eh_frame`, `.fini_array`, `.init_array`, `.preinit_array`, `.dynamic` and `.toc` (ld.lld's others are thread-local, `SHT_INIT_ARRAY` and the like, or its own `.got`, none of which merlin-ld makes). A segment's `p_align` is the page size, 0x1000 on both widths (ld.lld's RISC-V maximum page size), or the largest alignment of its sections when that is more (`.p2align 13`, `ALIGN(8K)`), as ld.lld raises it; its file offset is congruent to its address modulo `p_align`. The other sections' file offsets are ld.lld's (`computeFileOffset`): a section is at its segment's offset plus its distance from the segment's address, but a `NOBITS` one after the first of its segment is at the end of the file so far, and an empty section whose segment is dropped at the end so far aligned to its alignment. Section headers and a `.symtab` (locals, then globals) are included for merlin-objdump and for Grail's `tohost` lookup. The locals are each object's, in its order, those ld.lld keeps (`includeInSymtab`, `shouldKeepInSymtab`): every one defined in a loaded section or a debug section (mapping symbols included), and the file symbols (`STT_FILE`, absolute, with `-S` too, first in their object's group as the assembler writes them and ld.lld's `sortSymTabSymbols` keeps them), but no section symbol and no `.L` label of a mergeable section; one of `.riscv.attributes` is absolute, its value its offset there, as in ld.lld, which merges those sections into one of its own (only when ld.lld would write that section: not when `/DISCARD/` takes it). The fixture links compare their local symbols with ld.lld's.
  - **The headers** go where ld.lld puts them (`createPhdrs`, `allocateHeaders`, `assignFileOffsets`). ld.lld sizes its program header table before it knows which headers it keeps, for a `PT_PHDR`, a `PT_LOAD` for the headers, one `PT_LOAD` per run (an empty run too), a `PT_GNU_RELRO` per run of RELRO sections (see "Output"), `PT_GNU_STACK` and, when it writes a `.riscv.attributes` (an input has one and `/DISCARD/` does not take it), `PT_RISCV_ATTRIBUTES`; one `PT_LOAD` fewer when the first section is read-only and in no MEMORY region, since it joins the headers'. merlin-ld leaves the same room after the ELF header, with zeros where ld.lld has the headers merlin-ld does not write. When that room fits between the start of the lowest section's page and the section, the headers are loaded: at the start of the page and of the file, in a read-only `PT_LOAD` of their own or in a read-only first section's (which then starts at the page, its load address moved down with it), and a `PT_PHDR` describes the room. Either way the first segment goes at the first file offset past the room that is congruent to its address. So for ELF32 with three runs (a room of 0x114 bytes: 52 for the ELF header, 7 program headers of 32; ELF64's is 0x1c8, 64 + 7 × 56), a first section at the start of a page is at file offset 0x1000 and the headers are not loaded, one at 0x8000_0100 is at 0x1100 (no room below it) and the headers are not loaded, and one at 0x8000_0400 is at 0x400, after headers loaded at 0x8000_0000. A loader of every `PT_LOAD` (Grail, the bootloader) loads them too, read-only; a script keeps them out with a first section at the start of a page. The boot ROM's disk loader, at 0x1100, has its headers loaded at 0x1000, where the ROM's stub is, so Grail takes only its segments at 0x1100 and after (`crates/grail/src/boot.rs`).
- **Relocations**: after layout, every entry of every `.rela.*` section that patches a loaded section goes through one function, `reloc::apply(type, P, S, A, bytes, ctx)` (`crates/merlin-ld/src/reloc.rs`, which explains each formula and field), where `bytes` runs from the patched place to the end of its input section and `ctx` maps each `R_RISCV_PCREL_HI20` place to its S and A. The values follow the psABI, computed as the hardware adds, on both widths: S + A and S + A - P modulo 2^64, read as signed for the range checks (on RV32, where S and P are below 2^32 and A is a 32-bit addend, that is the exact value), as ld.lld computes them:
  - `R_RISCV_64`: S + A, all 8 bytes, never checked, on both widths (assemblers emit it for `.dword sym` on RV32 too; on RV64 it is every pointer in data and every address in debug information). On RV32, in a section that is not loaded (debug information), the value is cut to 32 bits and sign-extended, as ld.lld's `relocateNonAlloc` does: `.dword _start` with `_start` at 0x8000_0000 is 0xffff_ffff_8000_0000 there and 0x8000_0000 in `.data`.
  - `R_RISCV_32`: S + A, which must fit 32 bits (signed or unsigned). On RV64 the value is read as signed, so a higher-half address 0xffff_ffff_8xxx_xxxx fits (what clang's `-mcmodel=medlow` jump tables hold in a kernel linked there, and what `lw` sign-extends back), and 2^32 does not.
  - `R_RISCV_BRANCH` / `R_RISCV_JAL`: S + A - P, even, within -4096..=4094 / -1 MiB..=1 MiB - 2. On RV64 the offset wraps at 2^64, as the hardware's does (a jump from 0 to 2^64 - 16 is -16); on RV32 it does not wrap at 2^32, as in ld.lld. The error gives ld.lld's range, that of a 13- or 21-bit number (`4096 is not in [-4096, 4095]`), and an offset both out of range and odd gets the misalignment error too, after it, as in ld.lld.
  - `R_RISCV_ADD8/16/32/64` and `R_RISCV_SUB8/16/32/64`: the field (a byte, half, word or doubleword) plus or minus S + A. They come in pairs at one place, the label differences an assembler cannot work out, which LLVM emits with `-mno-relax` too: `.dword g - .` is an `ADD64` of `g` and a `SUB64` of the place, `.word end - start` across sections an `ADD32` and a `SUB32`. `R_RISCV_SUB6` and `R_RISCV_SET6` change the low 6 bits of a byte (field - (S + A), S + A), `R_RISCV_SET8/16/32` write S + A, and `R_RISCV_32_PCREL` writes S + A - P in a word (clang's `.eh_frame` pointers with `-funwind-tables`). As in ld.lld, `SET32` and `32_PCREL` must fit 32 bits read as signed (on RV32 too: `relocation R_RISCV_SET32 out of range for 'g' at .data+0x13: 2147488312 is not in [-2147483648, 2147483647]`), and the others keep the low bits of their value. On RV32, in debug information, every value is the 32-bit one sign-extended first, as for `R_RISCV_64` (so `.dword g - _start` is `g - _start` modulo 2^32 there).
  - `R_RISCV_HI20` / `R_RISCV_LO12_I` / `R_RISCV_LO12_S`: S + A split into `hi = (v + 0x800) >> 12` (for `lui`) and the low 12 bits, which `addi`/loads/stores sign-extend: the `+ 0x800` rounds `hi` up when bit 11 is set so the pair adds back to `v`.
  - `R_RISCV_PCREL_HI20`: S + A - P, split the same way (for `auipc`). `R_RISCV_PCREL_LO12_I/S`: its symbol is the label on the matching `auipc`, which must be in the same section; the low part is that of the `PCREL_HI20` found at the label's address (its S + A - P, not the low instruction's). Its own addend must be 0.
  - `R_RISCV_CALL` / `R_RISCV_CALL_PLT`: S + A - P split over the `auipc` and the `jalr` after it.
  - The `hi`/`lo` pairs build `hi << 12` plus the sign-extended 12-bit `lo`. On RV32 they are computed modulo 2^32, as RV32 executes them, so they reach every address and never overflow (ld.lld does the same). On RV64 `lui` and `auipc` sign-extend `hi << 12` to 64 bits, so a pair reaches only the values `-2^31 - 0x800 <= v < 2^31 - 0x800` (±2 GiB moved down by the `+ 0x800` rounding: 0x7fff_f800 would need `hi` = 0x80000, which is -2^31); `R_RISCV_HI20` (on S + A) and `R_RISCV_PCREL_HI20`, `R_RISCV_CALL` and `R_RISCV_CALL_PLT` (on S + A - P) are checked against that window, as ld.lld's `checkInt((v + 0x800) >> 12, 20)`, and the error gives ld.lld's numbers, the `hi` value and the 20-bit range: `a.o: relocation R_RISCV_PCREL_HI20 out of range for 'sym' at .text+0x4: 524288 is not in [-524288, 524287]`. The `LO12` halves are never checked, their `HI20` is, and a `CALL` out of range writes neither word. So on RV64 `lui %hi(x)` reaches only about the lowest and the highest 2 GiB of the address space (0x7fff_f800 is out, 0xffff_ffff_7fff_f800 in), and code linked in RAM at 0x8000_0000 must be pc-relative (`-mcmodel=medany`), which reaches ±2 GiB around itself. ld.lld gives no hint about the code model, and neither does merlin-ld.
  - A weak reference that another object defines is that definition. A branch, jump or call to an undefined weak symbol targets itself (as in ld.lld: address 0 is usually out of reach); every other reference to it sees 0. On RV64 an `la` of one is pc-relative, so 0 - P must be in the window: from RAM, only an `auipc` at most 0x800 bytes above 0x8000_0000 reaches it, and one higher up is out of range, as in ld.lld.
  - Errors name the file, section, offset and symbol: `a.o: relocation R_RISCV_JAL out of range for 'foo' at .text+0x10: 1048580 is not in [-1048576, 1048575]`. Misaligned targets, a field running past its section, a `PCREL_LO12` without its `PCREL_HI20`, `R_RISCV_ALIGN` (see the differences below) and types outside this list (GOT, TLS, compressed, `SET_ULEB128`/`SUB_ULEB128`) are errors too.
- **Differences from ld.lld**, on purpose:
  - `SHF_MERGE` sections are not merged. ld.lld keeps one copy of identical strings and constants (two C files using the same string literal share it) and orders the pieces by hash; merlin-ld concatenates the input sections in order. When an output section holds duplicates it is larger than ld.lld's and every address after it differs, and merlin-ld warns (`.rodata: mergeable (SHF_MERGE) input sections hold N bytes of duplicate strings or constants; ...`). Without duplicates the sizes match, but a symbol inside a merged string section can still have a different address.
  - With `MEMORY`, a top-level `. = ...` that moves `.` before an output section is an error (ld.lld ignores it), see Placement.
  - Script-assigned symbols are `SHN_ABS` in `.symtab`; ld.lld makes them relative to a nearby output section. Their values are the same.
  - Output section flags are only `SHF_ALLOC`, `SHF_WRITE` and `SHF_EXECINSTR` (ld.lld also carries `SHF_MERGE`/`SHF_STRINGS` over from merged inputs).
  - Unmatched allocated sections that are not empty, or that define a global, are errors, not orphans placed by heuristics (an empty one is placed as ld.lld places an orphan, see "Placement"). Output section addresses (`.sec 0x1000 : { }`), `AT>` load regions, `LOADADDR` and `SIZEOF`, and other attributes than `(NOLOAD)`, `AT(lma)` and `ALIGN(n)` are not supported.
  - `ADDR` of a section placed later in the script, or of a section in its own `ALIGN` (`.data : ALIGN(ADDR(.data))`), is an error: ld.lld goes round the script again until the addresses settle, merlin-ld walks it once, as it does for symbols.
  - Every argument of `OUTPUT_FORMAT` must be `elf32-littleriscv` or `elf64-littleriscv` (or one of them with `-freebsd`); ld.lld reads only the first. The class errors name the format without the suffix (`a.o is incompatible with elf64-littleriscv`), where ld.lld repeats it as written.
  - On ELF32, a value past the 4 GiB address space is an error where ld.lld cuts it to 32 bits: a script symbol, the entry point, a load address range (ld.lld truncates `p_paddr`), the headers' load address range below 0 (when they share a read-only first section's `PT_LOAD` but its `AT` is less than its distance from their page: `the headers' load address range would start below 0: they share a segment with section .rodata, loaded at 0x10, 0x400 bytes after them`, on ELF64 too, where ld.lld wraps `p_paddr` around), as well as the location counter and the MEMORY region. On ELF64, a MEMORY region or a load address range past 2^64 is an error (ld.lld lets both wrap around: the region's end wraps to a small address and every section in it then overflows the region, and the load range becomes a segment whose `p_paddr + p_memsz` wraps). A section's `ALIGN(n)` above 2^31 is an error on both (ld.lld ignores it).
  - Only `PT_LOAD` program headers, and a `PT_PHDR` when the headers are loaded (no `PT_GNU_RELRO`, `PT_GNU_STACK` or `PT_RISCV_ATTRIBUTES`: their entries in the program header table's room are zeros, so every segment is where ld.lld puts it), and no `.riscv.attributes` or `.comment` in the output. Without `.riscv.attributes`, llvm-objdump does not know the target has the M extension and prints `mul`/`div`/... in a merlin-ld executable as `<unknown>`: compare the two disassemblies with `llvm-objdump -d --mattr=+m` (the bytes are the same). Undefined weak symbols are left out of `.symtab`.
  - A `PCREL_LO12` with a non-zero addend is an error (ld.lld warns and ignores the addend), and an `R_RISCV_32` value that does not fit 32 bits is an error, on both widths (ld.lld truncates it).
  - `R_RISCV_ALIGN` is an error (`R_RISCV_ALIGN at .text+0xc: merlin-ld does not delete alignment padding as ld.lld does; assemble with -mno-relax`). ld.lld, with `--no-relax` too, deletes the padding bytes beyond what the alignment needs, moving the code, symbols and relocations after them; merlin-ld never deletes bytes. LLVM emits it only with relaxation (`-mrelax`, clang's default), after a `call` or `la` in the same section.
  - A pc-relative relocation in debug information (`R_RISCV_32_PCREL`, a branch) is applied with P its offset in the output section, the value ld.lld gives it, without ld.lld's warning (`has non-ABS relocation`).

### Disassembler output

`merlin-objdump` takes llvm-objdump's options (`-d -r -t -h -x -f -p -s`, `-l` with `-d`, `-M no-aliases`, `--no-show-raw-insn`) and prints the same text as llvm-objdump 23, for ELF32 (RV32) and ELF64 (RV64) files alike, so the two can be diffed: same layout, pseudo-instructions where LLVM prints them (`mv`, `ret`, `beqz`, `csrr`, ...), hex immediates, branch targets as `0x80000044 <puts+0x28>`, `jalr` after `auipc` resolved to `<symbol>`, and relocations under their instruction with `-dr`. Besides:

- **The width** comes from the file's class: `elf32-littleriscv` and `riscv32`, or `elf64-littleriscv` and `riscv64`, and the code decoded for that width. An ELF64 file's addresses take 16 digits: the start address, the `-p` fields, the `-h` VMA column (sizes keep 8), `-t`'s values and sizes, `-r`'s offsets (under an `OFFSET` header as wide), function labels, and relocations under their instruction (indented by two tabs instead of three). Instruction lines keep `%8x` addresses on both, the mnemonic at column 24 (16 with `--no-show-raw-insn`) or the next tab stop on a longer line.
- **Targets are 64-bit** on both widths when a symbol is looked up for them, as LLVM evaluates branches: on RV32 the operand of a branch below 0 wraps at 2^32 (`j 0xffffff04`) and the label does not (`<f+0xffffffffffffff04>`); the `auipc` values behind `jalr` targets are kept the same way.
- **Mapping symbols** (LLVM 23): data the assembler marks with `$d` prints as `.word` while 4 bytes are left before the next symbol or mapping symbol, then `.short`, then `.byte`, each with its bytes one by one (`13 00 00 00  .word 0x00000013`), and a run of zeros as `...` as in code. At each mapping symbol, `$x` or `$d`, the `auipc` values tracked for `jalr` are forgotten. A `$x<isa>` symbol names the ISA of the code after it (`$xrv64i2p1_m2p0_...`, as LLVM's assembler writes them); an ISA LLVM cannot parse (the riscv-tests ELFs' `$x.0`, from GNU's assembler) gets its warning on stderr, once per name (`could not parse ISA mapping symbol '$x.0': string may only contain [a-z0-9_]; falling back to default disassembler`). Nothing else depends on the ISA: LLVM 23 decodes the same instructions whatever it names. merlin-as writes them as clang does (#192), so data in the code of its objects lists as it does in clang's.
- **Warnings** go to stderr as llvm-objdump writes them, a line each. llvm-objdump's own name the program and the file, and merlin-objdump puts its own name in the program's place (where llvm-objdump writes the path it was run as), the rest of the line the same: `merlin-objdump: warning: 'FILE': could not parse ISA mapping symbol ...`. Those of LLVM's DWARF reader (with `-l`) name neither, and are the same line: `warning: address range table at offset 0x0 has a premature terminator entry at offset 0x20`.

The deliberate differences:

- Xcal instructions print as `xcal.dbg`/`xcal.halt` and undecodable words as `.word 0x...` (trailing odd bytes as `.byte`), where LLVM prints `<unknown>` (unlike data under `$d`, which both print as data).
- Reserved fence encodings (non-zero `fm`, `rs1` or `rd`, and `fence.i` with operand bits set) print as the `fence`/`fence.i` Grail executes. Like LLVM, the disassembler still forgets the `auipc` values it tracks at them.
- `dret` (debug mode, which Excalibur does not have) prints as `.word 0x7b200073`.
- CSR names come from `excalibur::csr`; CSRs not listed there print as numbers.

The checks: `crates/merlin-objdump/tests/golden.rs` compares its output with llvm-objdump's for clang-built fixtures (`tests/fixtures`, and their RV64 twins in `tests/fixtures/rv64`: every instruction form, every relocation type, section and symbol kinds, data in code, a linked executable with sections at 2^32 and at 0xffff_ffff_8000_0000, merlin's line tables, compiled C, hello) and riscv-tests ELFs of both widths (`scripts/gen-objdump-golden.sh`, made with LLVM 23.1.4: `tests/golden`, `tests/golden/rv64`); merlin-as's hello object (`crates/merlin-as/tests/encode_hello.rs`) and merlin-ld's links of both fixture sets are compared with clang's and ld.lld's listings in the same way.

The instruction formatting itself (`Inst::format` in `excalibur`) is shared with Grail's `--trace`, which uses the plain form: no pseudo-instructions, decimal immediates, relative branch offsets.

`excalibur` decodes and formats both widths:
- **The width** is a parameter: `decode(word, xlen)` and `Syntax::xlen`. A word means different things on the two (section 4), branch targets wrap at the width, and the RV32-only CSRs are named only on RV32, as in llvm-objdump.
- **RV64's aliases:** `sext.w` for `addiw rd, rs, 0`, `negw` for `subw rd, zero, rs`, and the `.d` atomics with their suffixes.
- **The check:** `crates/excalibur/tests/disasm.rs` decodes every word of a corpus for each width (`tests/golden/rv32.s`, `rv64.s`: every instruction and alias, the edge immediates, and the reserved encodings next to the ones that differ by width, such as funct3 values a load, store, AMO or 32-bit operation does not use, or shift fields with bits the width does not allow) and compares it with llvm-objdump's text, with and without aliases (`scripts/gen-excalibur-golden.sh`, made with LLVM 23).
- **The hints LLVM 23 names:** LLVM 23 decodes some HINT encodings as the extensions that give them meaning, whatever the object's ISA says, and excalibur does the same (Excalibur executes them as the plain instructions they are): Zihintpause's `pause` (`fence w, 0`), Zihintntl's `ntl.p1`, `ntl.pall`, `ntl.s1` and `ntl.all` (`add zero, zero, x2..x5`) and Zicfilp's `lpad imm` (`auipc zero, imm`) as aliases, and Zicbop's `prefetch.i`, `prefetch.r` and `prefetch.w offset(rs1)` (`ori zero, rs1, imm` with `imm[4:0]` 0, 1 or 3) as instructions, so also without aliases. LLVM 21 printed them all as the plain instructions. Grail's trace, the plain form, keeps them plain (`Syntax::zicbop` off): what it prints, merlin-as assembles (`crates/merlin-as/tests/encode_roundtrip.rs`), and merlin-as, like clang for an ISA without Zicbop, takes no `prefetch.*`.

The tools move to RV64 one by one (#141). merlin-as passes the width of its `-march` (#148), merlin-ld, which decodes nothing, links in the class of its objects (#149), and merlin-objdump disassembles for the class of its file (#150); until its step each other tool passes `Xlen::Rv32`.

### Debug information

Source-level debugging (merlin-dbg's `break FILE:LINE`, `step` and `next` by line, `list`) rests on DWARF line tables, which every tool in the chain handles:

- **merlin `-g`** (with `-fdebug-compilation-dir=DIR`, the directory file names are relative to; by default the current one) writes clang's directives: `.file 0 "DIR" "main.c"` first, `.file N "DIR" "include/x.h"` for each header that has code, and `.loc FILE LINE COLUMN` before the code of every statement: a function's first line (column 0) before its prologue, each statement, a loop's condition and step where they run, and the closing brace before the epilogue. The first `.loc` after the prologue says `prologue_end`. A position is a token's: an expression's is its operator's (`i < n` at the `<`). It also writes a DWARF 5 compile unit in `.debug_info` (abbreviations in `.debug_abbrev`): producer `merlin`, language C99, the file and directory, the code's range, `DW_AT_stmt_list` (a label at the start of `.debug_line`), and a `DW_TAG_subprogram` per function (name, range, file, line, external). Debuggers find line tables through compile units: lldb, llvm-objdump and merlin-objdump ignore a `.debug_line` without one. None of it changes the code: every test case compiles to the same loaded bytes, relocations and symbols with and without `-g` (`crates/merlin/tests/debug.rs`).
- **merlin-as** turns `.file`/`.loc` into `.debug_line` (DWARF 5, with `.debug_line_str` for the names) the way LLVM's assembler does, byte for byte: the same directory and file tables (directory 0 is the compilation directory and file 0 the main file of `.file 0`; another file in the compilation directory or with none is split at its last `/`; an MD5 column when every file has one), one sequence per code section in the order their first rows come, and the same opcodes (`is_stmt` carries over from one `.loc` to the next, two `.loc`s in a row both get a row, `.space` makes none). Addresses and strings are relocations against section symbols: an address is 4 bytes and `R_RISCV_32` on RV32, 8 bytes and `R_RISCV_64` on RV64 (the header's `address_size` is 8 and `DW_LNE_set_address` is `00 09 02` and 8 bytes), and the offsets into `.debug_line_str` are `R_RISCV_32` on both, since the format is DWARF32. `crates/merlin-as/tests/debug_line.rs` compares every `.debug_*` section and its relocations with clang's for a corpus of `.s` files, on each width (`scripts/gen-debug-line-golden.sh` makes clang's objects: `tests/debug_line/*.o` for RV32, `tests/debug_line/rv64/*.o` for RV64, from the same sources except `merlin.s`, compiler output that depends on the width, whose RV64 version in `rv64/` is merlin's `-g -march=rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei` output), merlin's own output among them. A numbered `.file` makes the table even when no `.loc` follows, as in LLVM (`.file N` makes the compile unit's line table, and the assembler writes every one there is): its header alone, the directories and files, and no sequence (`tests/debug_line/files.s`). The files are LLVM's (`tryGetFile`): a number is given once (`file number already allocated`, even for the same file); a `.file N` that names the main file (the name of `.file 0`'s, and its checksum or none) is file 0 and leaves its number unassigned, so a `.loc N` to it is `unassigned file number in '.loc' directive`; numbers count from 1 with no gaps (`unassigned file number: 1 for .file directives`). A `.loc`, or a `.file N` without one, needs a `.file 0` (`a line table (.file N) needs .file 0, ...`): LLVM would take the current directory, which would make the object depend on where it is assembled.
- **merlin-ld** keeps every `.debug_*` input section: each output debug section is its inputs concatenated in command-line order, after everything loaded (file data after `.strtab`, section headers after `.strtab`'s, before `.shstrtab`), at no address, so it changes no `PT_LOAD`, file offset of loaded data or section index. Its relocations (`R_RISCV_32` and `R_RISCV_64`: on RV32 the addresses are `R_RISCV_32`, on RV64 `R_RISCV_64` and the offsets into other debug sections `R_RISCV_32`) are applied with symbols in debug sections at their offsets there; a reference to a symbol with no place in the executable (in code `/DISCARD/` threw away, a global by its definition, in whichever object that is, or a weak reference nobody defines) gets ld.lld's tombstone, for either type and whatever the addend: 0, but 1 in `.debug_loc` and `.debug_ranges`, where a pair of zeros would end a DWARF 4 location or range list. The local symbols of the debug sections (the assembler's `$d`, clang's labels) are in `.symtab` with the debug section's index and their offsets there for values, as ld.lld writes them. `-S` (`--strip-debug`) leaves the debug sections out, and their symbols with them: the result is byte for byte the same link without them (`merlin_ld::strip_debug` does it to a linked file, ELF32 or ELF64, writing the symbol and string tables again without those symbols). ld.lld's `-S` keeps those symbols, as absolute ones of value 0.
- **merlin-objdump `-l`** prints `; function():` and `; file:line` above the instructions like llvm-objdump (checked against it on clang's and merlin's objects), finding them as LLVM's symbolizer does. The function is the symbol table's: the last function, object or untyped symbol at or before the address in an allocated section (of several at one address the biggest), if the address is inside it; in an object that is whatever section it is in, as every section starts at 0. Where no symbol covers the address, the name is the debug information's, from the compile unit that covers it (see below): the innermost subprogram or inlined subroutine whose code holds the address, by its linkage name if it has one, else by its name, each looked for through `DW_AT_specification` and `DW_AT_abstract_origin` too (`tests/fixtures/subprograms.s` and `rv64/subprograms.s`). A name gets `()` after it unless it ends with them. The line comes through a compile unit: the one whose address ranges hold the address (those `.debug_aranges` gives a unit, else the unit DIE's own, `DW_AT_low_pc`/`DW_AT_high_pc` or the range list `DW_AT_ranges` in `.debug_rnglists` or `.debug_ranges`; where units overlap, the one already running, else the first), then the row at or before the address in that unit's line table (`DW_AT_stmt_list`, in the address's section in an object), its path after the unit's `DW_AT_comp_dir` as LLVM joins them (not before an absolute directory or DWARF 5's directory 0). Code no unit covers has no lines, and a line table no unit names (hand-written `.file`/`.loc` alone, `tests/fixtures/rv64/nocu.s`) is never read. As in LLVM, a `.debug_aranges` length is read without its relocations: in a relaxed object, where it is an `R_RISCV_ADD64`/`SUB64` pair over 0, it ends the list early, with LLVM's warning (`address range table at offset 0x0 has a premature terminator entry at offset 0x20`). `elf::lines` reads line tables (DWARF 2 to 5, 4- or 8-byte addresses) and `elf::units` the compile units' entries (their abbreviations, every DWARF 5 form, addresses and strings by index through `.debug_addr` and `.debug_str_offsets`), applying an object's relocations first (`R_RISCV_32` and `R_RISCV_64`, and the `ADD`/`SUB`/`SET` pairs of objects built with relaxation; not `R_RISCV_SET_ULEB128`/`SUB_ULEB128`, whose unrelocated value LLVM reads, as it is right).
- **The kernel and its programs** are built with `-g` (`cargo xtask kernel` and `cargo xtask fs`), with the compilation directory relative to the repository root (`kernel`, `kernel/user`): the line tables name `kernel/main.c`, so a debugger run there finds the sources, and the output does not depend on where the repository is. The committed `kernel.elf` and `kernel-disk.elf` carry their line tables; the user programs the kernel embeds and `/bin` holds are stripped, and `cargo xtask kernel` writes them with their line tables to `target/kernel/obj/user/<name>.elf`, for merlin-dbg's `add-symbol-file`. Nothing loaded changes: the kernels' segments and the root image are the same with and without `-g` (`crates/xtask/tests/debug_info.rs`).

### Bootstrapping

clang (`--target=riscv32-unknown-elf -march=rv32ima_zicsr -mabi=ilp32 -mno-relax -ffreestanding -nostdlib`, native Windows LLVM build) and ld.lld are the reference toolchain. Each of our tools was validated by swapping it in for the LLVM one and diffing behavior:

1. Our assembler output linked by `ld.lld` → must run the same.
2. clang `.o` files linked by our linker → must run the same.
3. Our compiler's `.s` → our assembler → our linker → emulator.

Steps 1 and 2 together, our assembler and our linker with no LLVM at all, is how every example is built (below). Step 3 is how the kernel is built (issue #15, "Kernel build"): merlin, merlin-as and merlin-ld turn every C and assembly file of the kernel and its user programs into the committed `kernel/kernel.elf`, and the clang build is kept as a reference to compare against. All three steps are checked on the kernel by tests that need LLVM and are ignored otherwise (`cargo test -p xtask --test kernel -- --ignored`): step 1 and 2 by `merlin_as_and_merlin_ld_take_the_reference_builds_place`, step 3 by `clang_reference_build_behaves_like_merlins`.

### Self-hosting

Our tools are to run on Camelot itself (roadmap step 10, #23). The host tools are Rust, which no tool of ours compiles, so the ones that run on Camelot are rewritten in a language Merlin compiles: the C subset for now, until the Camelot language exists (#122). The host tools stay as the bootstrap, building the first version, and as the reference: a tool on Camelot must write the same output as the host's, byte for byte, for the same input, the way merlin-as and merlin-ld are held to LLVM's. The file formats stay the same, so objects made on either side link together, and merlin-dbg debugs programs made on Camelot.

**The assembler** (`/bin/as`, `kernel/user/as/`, #135) is merlin-as in C: `as [-o out.o] input.s`, with merlin-as's language, rules and messages (`file:line: error: message`, the same errors in the same order) for RV32, merlin-as's default; it learns `-march` and RV64 in #157. Data of every size, `.dword` and its `R_RISCV_64` included, the range check of literal data and the logical `>>` are the same in both (`i64_shr` in `lib/i64.c`). Each C file mirrors a module of merlin-as: `lexer.c`, `parser.c`, `encode.c`, `asm.c` (sections, symbols, directives, local labels), `section.c` (a section's type, flags and entry size), `dwarf.c` (the line table of `.file` and `.loc`, the bytes LLVM writes, as merlin-as's), `elf.c`, and `util.c` for memory; the 64-bit numbers merlin-as computes with, written before merlin had `long long`, are the C library's (`lib/i64.c`, until #157). `tables.h` and `tables.c`, the instructions' encodings, the funct3 values the pseudo-instructions use, the major opcodes, register and CSR names, the relocation types, the ISA string, and the section types and flags with `.section`'s flag letters, type names and the sections LLVM predefines, are generated from merlin-as's and excalibur's own tables by `cargo xtask kernel` (`crates/xtask/src/c_tables.rs`; a test checks the committed files are current), so the two assemblers can't disagree about them: the header declares the tables and defines the numbers, `tables.c` holds the tables once for the program. The relocation types are `RelocKind::ALL`'s, `R_RISCV_64` among them; merlin-as's RV64-only tables (`OP_32_FUNCT` and the like) are not exported, and the ISA string is the RV32 one (`elf::ARCH`), until #157. A fixup's kind is its relocation's psABI number (`R_RISCV_*`), written to `.rela` as it is. Where merlin-as reads the whole file into tokens, then statements, then assembles, `as` takes one statement at a time through all three (a machine has 4 MiB by default; the kernel's biggest assembly is 200 KB), keeping each phase's errors apart so that it reports merlin-as's: the first lexer error alone, else every syntax error, else every assembly error. `crates/xtask/tests/selfhost.rs` boots the disk kernel on an image with the corpus in `/src`, runs `as` on each file and compares the objects with merlin-as's: the golden encodings (`crates/merlin-as/tests/golden/encode.s`), every hand-written `.s` of the repository, every `.S` (the examples, the boot ROM) as merlin's preprocessor makes it for RV32 (Camelot has no preprocessor; the RV64 builds are left out until `as` writes RV64 in #157, as merlin-as's RV64 references are), the line table corpus (`crates/merlin-as/tests/debug_line`, not its `rv64/`), data of every size at the edges of its range, and merlin's assembly of a user program with `-g` and without, on three machines side by side, each with the default 4 MiB of RAM; an ignored test adds merlin's `-g` assembly of the shell and of the kernel's `vm.c`. Inside Grail a run costs about 9 million instructions to start (loading the program from the disk, mostly the kernel's byte-by-byte copies, #133), then about 27,000 per line of merlin's assembly: 97 million for the shell's 3,263 lines.

**The linker** (`/bin/ld`, `kernel/user/ld/`, #136) is merlin-ld in C: `ld [-T script] [-e entry] [-o out] [-S] input.o ... [--start-lib lib.o ... --end-lib] [@file]`, with merlin-ld's rules and messages (`ld: error: a.o: undefined reference to 'foo'`), everything but the link map (`--Map`) and RV64: it reads ELF32 objects only, and writes ELF32 executables, until #157 (an ELFCLASS64 object is `64-bit ELF objects are not supported by this ld`, and an `OUTPUT_FORMAT("elf64-littleriscv")` script is merlin-ld's `a.o is incompatible with elf64-littleriscv`, for every object and library member). Its scripts are merlin-ld's, `AT(lma)` load addresses and `ADDR(section)` included, and so are its executables' headers (where ld.lld puts the segments, the program headers loaded and `PT_PHDR` when they fit below the first section, `p_paddr` and `p_align`) and its relocations, `R_RISCV_64` too. Each C file mirrors a module of merlin-ld: `object.c` (reading objects), `library.c` (the members a program needs), `script.c` (the script subset), `layout.c` (symbols, sections, addresses), `reloc.c` (one relocation's formula and field), `relocate.c`, `write.c` (the executable), `util.c` and `main.c`; the ELF constants and the relocation types' numbers and names (`tables.h`, `tables.c`) are generated from the elf crate's like the assembler's tables. The 64-bit arithmetic both tools do (merlin-ld's location counter reaches 4 GiB exactly, and relocation values are checked before they are cut to a field) is the C library's `lib/i64.c`. With `-S` it writes the file a link without the debug sections gives, which is what merlin-ld's `strip_debug` makes of the full one. A link line is longer than a console line (128 characters) and has more arguments than `exec` takes (16), so both linkers read response files: `@FILE` stands for the words of FILE, separated by white space, without quoting, and a response file cannot name another. `crates/xtask/tests/selfhost.rs` links every user program inside Camelot from the host's objects (its own, the runtime's, the C library's members, each program's command line in a response file) with `-S` and compares the executables with what `cargo xtask kernel` links, on three machines side by side, and four of them with their line tables too (`hello`, `cat`, `grep` and `as`: the debug sections, their relocations and headers); an ignored test links every program with its line tables. #23's acceptance: `hello.S`, preprocessed on the host as for merlin-as, assembled by `/bin/as` and linked by `/bin/ld` is `examples/hello/hello.elf` byte for byte, and runs. Thirty-five links that fail or warn give merlin-ld's messages word for word, its exit status, and its file after a warning (none after an error): undefined and duplicate symbols, script errors at parse and at layout, an unplaced section, a branch out of range, one out of range and odd (two errors), `R_RISCV_ALIGN`, a `SET32` out of range, a bad entry, MEMORY and 4 GiB overflows, a discarded section referred to, inputs that are not objects, of neither class (`EI_CLASS` 3) or cut short, a section alignment that is not a power of two, a symbol table cut mid-entry, the headers' load range below 0, load addresses that overlap or pass 4 GiB, `AT` after `ALIGN`, an `ALIGN` that is not a power of two (in an expression, on a section and on `/DISCARD/`) and one above 2^31, a location counter moved backward inside a section and at the top level (sections over each other), the sections after one past 4 GiB, `ADDR` of a section not placed yet or of none, an unknown `OUTPUT_FORMAT` and `elf64-littleriscv` with RV32 objects and a library member (in command-line order, the member in the middle, after a script read for 64-bit values), a script's errors before a cut-short object's, and the entry (one of them with an `AT` and the headers loaded) and `SHF_MERGE` warnings. Nineteen scripts link the same executable on Camelot as merlin-ld, with and without `-S`: `AT` on the first section, in the middle by `ADDR` with a NOLOAD section and an empty section `ADDR` keeps after it, and `AT(.)`; the headers a page in, loaded in a segment of their own, in a read-only first section's, in a MEMORY region, without `.riscv.attributes` and without any section; a segment aligned to 8 KiB and a section of only an assignment after the code; a RELRO section, an empty section alone in its run, empty sections an `AT` or `ALIGN` keeps through a symbol, and an empty section at exactly 4 GiB; empty orphans of every rank, without MEMORY and with it; label differences and set values (`ADD`, `SUB`, `SET`); a top-level `.` moved backward, with `OUTPUT_FORMAT`'s three arguments, a `-freebsd` name and `ADDR(/DISCARD/)`; names with dashes, unquoted, and `ALIGN(0)`; and `.dword` (`R_RISCV_64`) and `.word` pointers in `.data` and in debug information, of discarded code too. A link costs about 36 million instructions inside Grail, most of them the kernel's: copying file contents a byte at a time (#133), allocating the output's blocks and zeroing the pages the heap grows into.

### Examples

The programs in `examples/` are built by `cargo xtask examples` (`crates/xtask/src/examples.rs`): each source, a `.S` file, goes through merlin's preprocessor (below), the result is assembled by merlin-as and the objects are linked by merlin-ld, with the example's `link.ld` (or the default layout for `muldiv`). The tools run as libraries inside xtask, the same code as their command lines, which the comment at the top of each source spells out; the preprocessed sources are left next to the objects, in `target/examples/<dir>` (`target/examples/rv64/<dir>` for RV64). The linked `examples/<dir>/<name>.elf` files are committed so Grail can run them without building anything, and `cargo test` (`crates/xtask/tests/examples.rs`) rebuilds every example from source, requires the result to equal the committed file byte for byte, and runs it in Grail with its input (UART bytes, key events, pointer input, a disk image, another machine on a hub), checking the output and the exit code. Nothing in that needs LLVM. `examples/multiobj` exists to exercise multi-object linking: two sources with calls, pc-relative addresses and pointers between them (`RISCV_PTR`: `.word` on RV32, `.dword` on RV64), `.data` written at run time, `.bss` in the other object, and a non-zero exit code (42). `examples/smp` exercises a multi-hart machine (section 7, "Harts"): hart 0 wakes the others with an IPI, every hart adds 1000 to one counter with `amoadd.w` and to another with an LR/SC loop, counting its failed `sc.w`s, then hart 0 sends three rounds of IPIs; it prints the totals, which must be N x 1000 on N harts. The test runs it on 1 to 8 harts.

Every example is built for both widths while both exist (#151, until #159), from the same sources (#193): for RV32 into `<name>.elf` and for RV64 into `<name>-rv64.elf`, preprocessed and assembled with `-march=rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei` and linked with the same script into an ELF64 executable, which Grail runs on an RV64 machine. As in Linux, a `.S` file is assembly that goes through the C preprocessor first: merlin's, in assembler-with-cpp mode (`merlin -E`, `merlin::preprocess_asm`), which does what `clang -E -x assembler-with-cpp` does and writes the same text (`crates/merlin/tests/asm_cpp.rs`, ignored by default, compares the two on every `.S` of the repository at both widths, with line markers and without (`-P`): the examples, the boot ROM, the kernel's assembly and linker script, merlin-ld's and merlin-objdump's fixtures). It defines C's predefined macros (`__riscv_xlen` 32 or 64 from `-march`, `__riscv`, the ISA's `__riscv_m`, `__riscv_a`... as above), `__ASSEMBLER__`, and `__STDC_VERSION__` 201710L and `__STDC_HOSTED__` 1, what clang gives a `.S` file it is told no standard for; pragmas and `#ident` pass on as in C, at the start of their line; a `#` line that is not a directive (`# a comment`, `# 2^64 - 1`) passes through, so comments may not start with a directive's name (`# if`, `# define`), and an apostrophe opens a character constant up to the next one on its line (none: to the end of the line), inside which macros are not replaced, as in clang. Comments are still C's, though: a `/*` in a `#` comment line (a glob such as `examples/*/`) starts a C comment that swallows everything to the next `*/`, lines of code in between included, as clang does, so such a comment must not hold one (write `examples/<dir>/`). The output keeps the source's line numbers with blank lines and line markers (`# 120 "examples/disk/disk.S"`), which merlin-as reads as comments; `cargo xtask` names an assembler error's line in the source through them. The width-dependent names are in `include/asm.h` (`#include <asm.h>` with `-I include`, for the examples, the boot ROM and merlin-ld's fixtures alike), which uses the names Linux's `arch/riscv/include/asm/asm.h` gives them, so code reads the same: `REG_L` and `REG_S` (`lw`/`sw`, `ld`/`sd`), `SZREG`, `RISCV_PTR` (`.word`, `.dword`) and `RISCV_SZPTR`, `RISCV_LGPTR`, and three of this project's own for 32-bit values, `LWU`, `ADDW`, `ADDIW` (`lwu`, `addw`, `addiw` on RV64, `lw`, `add`, `addi` on RV32). What differs in more than a size is under `#if __riscv_xlen == 64`:

- return addresses, saved registers and pointers are stored with `REG_S` and loaded with `REG_L` (on RV64 `lw` would sign-extend an address at or above 0x8000_0000), in `SZREG`-byte stack slots (`REG_S ra, 16-SZREG(sp)`), and a table of pointers is `RISCV_PTR`s (`multiobj`'s strings, `sysinfo`'s device names);
- the interrupt bit of `mcause` is bit 63 on RV64 (`CAUSE_MEI` is 0x8000_0000_0000_000B);
- on RV64 the devices have the registers of version 2 of their interfaces (section 9): sysinfo's 64-bit `SYS_RAM_BASE` and `SYS_RAM_SIZE` and 24-byte device table entries, the disk's 64-bit `BLK_CAPACITY`, `BLK_SECTOR` and `BLK_DMA`, the network card's 64-bit ring bases and 16-byte descriptors (`net/netlib.S`), 32-bit registers read with `lwu` where their value is unsigned (a file's `LWU`, `lw` on RV32). The registers' offsets are `.equ` symbols, each width's in an `#if` block, in their own order: the symbols are in the executables' symbol tables, which merging the sources did not change;
- 32-bit quantities stay 32-bit: the counters of `smp` (`amoadd.w`, `lr.w`/`sc.w`), TCP sequence numbers in `net/user.S` (`addw`, `addiw` on RV64, `ADDW` and `ADDIW` from `asm.h`, modulo 2^32 like the fields they go to) and the card's ring counters. `lw` reads a 32-bit counter sign-extended, so a value computed in a register and compared there with one it read must stay sign-extended too, computed with `addw` or `addiw`: `net_send`'s new `TX_TAIL`, compared with `TX_HEAD`, still equals it past 2^31. A value that is only stored with `sw`, which keeps its low 32 bits, and read again with `lw` may be computed with `addi` (`net_release`'s `rx_next` and `RX_TAIL`);
- `muldiv`'s RV64 build checks RV64's M extension: the 64-bit products and quotients (`mulh`, `mulhsu` and `mulhu` give the high 64 bits), the W instructions (`mulw`, `divw`, `divuw`, `remw`, `remuw`, which sign-extend their 32-bit result), division by zero and `INT64_MIN / -1` and `INT32_MIN / -1`: 29 cases.

Both builds print the same, apart from what the width changes: `xcal.dbg` shows a 16-digit pc and register, the programs print addresses and sizes in 16 hex digits (`disk 0x0000000010001000`), `sysinfo` says `v2`, and a pc moves where the RV64 code is longer (a 64-bit `li` of an interrupt cause). The comment at the top of each program's first source gives the commands of both builds (`merlin -E`, merlin-as, merlin-ld), and a test checks them against `cargo xtask examples -v`.

clang and ld.lld are only the reference. clang preprocesses a `.S` file itself (with `-I include` for those that include `asm.h`). Each source carries the command that builds the same program with them:

```
clang --target=riscv32 -march=rv32im_zicsr -mabi=ilp32 -mno-relax -nostdlib -static \
      -fuse-ld=lld -Wl,--no-relax,--discard-none -include examples/xcal.inc \
      -T examples/hello/link.ld examples/hello/hello.S -o hello.elf
```

and for RV64, with the flags of every RV64 reference build (#141):

```
clang --target=riscv64 -march=rv64ima_zicsr_zifencei -mabi=lp64 -mcmodel=medany -mno-relax \
      -nostdlib -static -fuse-ld=lld -Wl,--no-relax,--discard-none -include examples/xcal.inc \
      -T examples/hello/link.ld examples/hello/hello.S -o hello-rv64.elf
```

`-mno-relax` and `--no-relax` because our tools do no relaxation; `--discard-none` because the clang driver passes `-X` to ld.lld, which drops the `.L` labels (`.Lpcrel_hi1`) that merlin-ld keeps; `-include examples/xcal.inc` for the Xcal mnemonics (see `.insn` above). For `hello` and `multiobj` this link is committed (`crates/merlin-ld/tests/fixtures/{hello,multiobj}`, from `cargo xtask ld-fixtures`), and so is the RV64 one (`fixtures/rv64/{hello,multiobj}`, from the same sources), and the merlin-built executables of each width are compared with it: the entry point, every `PT_LOAD` (addresses, sizes, every byte), the allocated section headers and the symbols (names and values) are identical, and merlin-objdump's disassembly is llvm-objdump's text for ld.lld's file (RV32; RV64's from #150, when merlin-objdump reads ELF64). The rest of the file differs, as listed under "Differences from ld.lld" above:

- ld.lld writes `.riscv.attributes` and `.comment` sections and `PT_GNU_STACK` and `PT_RISCV_ATTRIBUTES` program headers; merlin-ld writes only `PT_LOAD`s (and a `PT_PHDR` when the headers are loaded, which no example's are) and no non-allocated sections besides `.symtab`, `.strtab`, `.shstrtab` and the debug sections.
- `__stack_top` (assigned by the script) is `SHN_ABS` in merlin-ld's `.symtab` and relative to an output section in ld.lld's. The value is the same.
- The non-allocated sections come in another order (`.symtab .strtab .shstrtab` against ld.lld's `.symtab .shstrtab .strtab`), so the section header table and those sections sit at other file offsets and the file sizes differ.

With relaxation on (clang's default, which is how the examples used to be built), ld.lld also shrinks every `call` whose target is in reach of a single `jal` from 8 bytes to 4, so everything after the first call moves: in `hello`, `puts` is at 0x8000001c instead of 0x80000020 and `xcal.dbg` reports pc 0x80000014 instead of 0x80000018. That build is kept as a merlin-objdump test fixture (`crates/merlin-objdump/tests/fixtures/hello.elf`).

### Kernel build

The kernel (`kernel/`) is built by `cargo xtask kernel`, a Rust build task (`crates/xtask`), not a Makefile or shell script, entirely with our own tools and no LLVM: merlin compiles each `.c` (with `kernel/include` as `-I`, and `-g`: see "Debug information"), merlin-as assembles the result and each `.S` (preprocessed by merlin first, with `include/` and `kernel/include` as `-I`, one source for both widths), and merlin-ld links the objects, in name order, with `kernel/kernel.lds.S`: one linker script for both widths that goes through the C preprocessor first, as Linux's `vmlinux.lds.S` does (`merlin -E -P`, no line markers, with the assembly's include directories; `#if __riscv_xlen` picks the layout), written to `target/kernel/obj/kernel.lds`. It builds the RV64 kernel the same way, with the same script preprocessed for RV64 ("The RV64 kernel"). The tools run as libraries inside xtask; `-v` prints the equivalent command lines, which are also in `kernel/README.md`, and the intermediate `.s` and `.o` files go to `target/kernel/obj`. The user programs the kernel embeds (`kernel/user/`, see "User programs" below) are built first, the same way (merlin, merlin-as, merlin-ld), and written as data into `userprogs.s`, a generated source: it is made in memory by every build (a copy goes to `target/kernel/obj`), is not committed, and is linked in its name order like the files of `kernel/` (a stale `kernel/userprogs.s` is an error). It then links the same objects a second time with a `userprogs.s` that has no programs (`user_program_count` 0) into `kernel/kernel-disk.elf`, the **disk kernel**. The two kernels split the work as Linux does: `kernel.elf`, which `grail kernel/kernel.elf` runs with or without a disk, carries its programs the way an initramfs carries a diskless machine's (about 1.1 MB of its 1.4 MB), while the disk kernel, a quarter of the size, is what a bootable root image holds (`cargo xtask fs`, "Root image"): its programs are the files in `/bin`, and with no shell there, or no root filesystem at all, it prints `cannot start /bin/sh: error 2` and panics (`panic: no shell in /bin, and no programs built into the kernel`, or `panic: no root filesystem, and no programs built into the kernel`). `kernel/kernel.elf` and `kernel/kernel-disk.elf` are committed, so running the kernel needs no build at all, and `cargo test` rebuilds both in memory and requires them to equal the committed files byte for byte: after changing the kernel, its programs or any of the three tools, run `cargo xtask kernel`. `cargo xtask fs` builds the programs in `/bin` of the root image with the same tools.

`cargo xtask kernel --cc clang` is the reference build, which needs LLVM: clang `-O2` compiles every C file, the user programs' too (same target, `-ffreestanding -nostdlib -mcmodel=medany`, see `kernel/README.md`), clang (or merlin-as, with `--as merlin`) assembles, and ld.lld links, into `target/kernel/clang/kernel.elf`. The user programs it embeds are built from clang's assembly by merlin-as and merlin-ld, as the committed ones are, and merlin-ld's warnings are errors there too, but for the `SHF_MERGE` one: clang puts string literals in mergeable sections, files of `/bin/as` and `/bin/ld` share some, and merlin-ld keeps every copy where ld.lld keeps one (see merlin-ld's differences from ld.lld), which changes the layout and nothing else. The ignored test `clang_reference_build_behaves_like_merlins` builds it, runs both kernels through the same sessions (boot, the built-in programs and faults, the root filesystem and its programs) and requires the same output, apart from what depends on the code (`xcal.dbg`'s pc, addresses in a fault report, tick counts, the free blocks on a disk whose `/bin` holds the other compiler's programs). merlin's code is a stack machine with every variable in memory and no optimization, so the kernel is bigger and slower than clang's (measured with the Grail of this repository, default machine, LLVM 21):

| | merlin | clang -O2 | merlin / clang |
|---|---|---|---|
| `kernel.elf` | 769,844 bytes | 553,096 bytes | 1.39 |
| `.text` | 146,264 bytes | 68,080 bytes | 2.15 |
| `.rodata` (with the embedded programs) | 563,805 bytes | 435,672 bytes | 1.29 |
| boot to the shell and `exit` | 36.3 M instructions | 5.8 M | 6.2 |
| a shell session of programs (`systest`, `badptr`, faults, `ping`, ...) | 94.3 M | 19.2 M | 4.9 |
| a session on the root filesystem (`ls`, pipes, `filetest`, ...) | 264.3 M | 49.1 M | 5.4 |

The tests' step limits allow for that: a boot gets 200 M steps (`BOOT_MAX_STEPS`), and a test of timer interrupts landing in kernel code shortens the tick to 1009 cycles, where 211 were enough for clang's faster interrupt path. The time spent per tick shows in the scheduling: with ticks landing in different places, a background program's output can come before or after the shell's `[N]`, which the tests allow.

Boot contract: Grail's boot ROM jumps to `_start` at 0x8000_0000 in M-mode (or, booting from disk, Camelot's bootloader does, having loaded the segments as Grail's ELF loader would: section 7, "Boot from disk"). `_start` points `mtvec` at an early trap handler, puts the hart's id (`mhartid`) in `tp`, sets `sp` to the top of a 16 KiB stack, zeroes `.bss` (`__bss_start`..`__bss_end`, both 4-byte aligned), and calls `start()`, the M-mode setup below, then executes `mret`, which enters S-mode at `supervisor_entry`. That calls `int kmain(void)` in S-mode; when `kmain` returns, the machine call `MCALL_HALT` stops the machine with its return value as the exit code. That is hart 0's way; the other harts come to `_start` later, when the kernel wakes them, and go to `hart_main()` instead ("Kernel SMP"). Until `start()` installs the M-mode trap handler, any trap prints `mcause`, `mepc` and `mtval` with `xcal.dbg` and halts with exit code 0xFF. `.text` starts at `__text_start` (= `_start`), and `.rodata` (`__rodata_start`) and `.data` (`__data_start`) each start on a page boundary, so the kernel page table can map them with different permissions. `__kernel_end` (page aligned) marks the first free byte of RAM after the image.

### Kernel traps

The kernel runs in S-mode on top of a small M-mode layer (the xv6-riscv design).

`start()` (M-mode, `kernel/start.c`):

- `medeleg` = every exception except ecall from S-mode (0xB1FF), so all faults go to the kernel and ecall from S-mode is left for machine calls. `mideleg` = SSI, STI, SEI (0x222).
- `stvec` = `kernel_vec`, set before S-mode starts, so a fault in the kernel's very first instruction is handled. (On RV64 `supervisor_entry` sets it, once the kernel runs at its link addresses: "The RV64 kernel".)
- `mtvec` = `machine_vec`; `mscratch` points at the hart's block of `machine_scratch`, 8 words per hart: scratch space for a1-a3 (the M-mode handler has no stack), the timer interval, the address of the hart's `mtimecmp`, and the tick word ("Kernel SMP").
- the hart's `mtimecmp` = `mtime` + interval; `mie` = MTIE and MSIE (the IPI).
- `mstatus.MPP` = S, `mepc` = `supervisor_entry`.

`machine_vec` (M-mode, `kernel/machine.S`) handles the only traps left in M-mode:

- Machine timer interrupt: the hart's `mtimecmp += interval` (so ticks don't drift), the tick word = 1, set `mip.SSIP`, `mret`. The kernel sees each tick as a supervisor software interrupt.
- Machine software interrupt (an IPI from another hart): `msip` of this hart = 0, set `mip.SSIP`, `mret` ("Kernel SMP").
- Machine call (ecall from S-mode): function number in a7, arguments in a0-a6, result in a0; `mepc` advances past the `ecall`. Function 0 (`MCALL_HALT`) stops the machine with exit code a0, since `xcal.halt` is M-mode only. Function 1 (`MCALL_IPI`) writes 1 to the `msip` of every hart whose bit is set in a0 and returns 0. Any other function returns -1.
- Anything else is a kernel bug: print `mcause`/`mepc`/`mtval` and halt with 0xFF, like the early trap handler.

Timer: the tick rate is 10 Hz, an interval of `timebase / 10` `mtime` ticks (at least 1). The timebase comes from sysinfo's `SYS_TIMEBASE_HZ` (section 9.5) through one function, `timebase_hz()`, which reads it on its first call (`timer_init`, in M-mode) and keeps it, so S-mode code never touches the register again (it stays valid once the kernel turns paging on). If `SYS_MAGIC` does not read "SYSI", or `SYS_TIMEBASE_HZ` reads 0, it falls back to the nominal 10 MHz (section 7).

`kernel_vec` (S-mode, `kernel/kernelvec.S`) saves x1-x31 and `sepc`, `scause`, `stval`, `sstatus` in a `struct trap_frame` on the current stack (`regs[32]` with x*n* at offset 4*n*, then the four CSRs at 128-140, then `kernel_tp` at 144, which only `user_vec` uses, padded to 160 bytes), calls `kernel_trap(frame)`, restores `sepc`, `sstatus` and the registers from the frame, and returns with `sret`. `stvec` points at `kernel_vec` only while the hart runs in S-mode; traps from U-mode go to `user_vec`, which uses `sscratch` to find the process's kernel stack ("Kernel processes").

`kernel_trap` (`kernel/trap.c`): a supervisor software interrupt clears `sip.SSIP`, serves the TLB flushes other harts asked of this one, and, if the hart's tick word (swapped to 0 with `amoswap.w`) was 1, counts a tick: in the hart's `struct cpu`, and on hart 0 in `ticks` too ("Kernel SMP"). A supervisor external interrupt (cause 9) claims the source from PLIC context 1, services it (the UART's receive handler, see "Kernel console") and completes it; `sip.SEIP` follows the PLIC's line, so there is nothing to clear. An exception at one of the memory probes' load or store (see "Kernel memory") resumes the probe instead. Any other trap is a panic: it prints

```
panic: <cause name>
  scause 0x........  sepc 0x........  stval 0x........  sstatus 0x........
```

then, for a page fault (cause 12, 13 or 15) with paging on, how the active page table maps `stval` (the kernel's mappings are the same in the kernel page table and in every process's): `  va 0x........ is not mapped` or `  va 0x........ maps to pa 0x........, pte 0x........ DAGUXWRV`, the flags printed bit 7 to bit 0 with `-` for a clear bit. Then x1-x31 four per line (`   ra 01010101   sp 80007400 ...`), and it halts with exit code 0xFE. After its self-tests the kernel sets up the PLIC and enables its interrupts (`sie.SSIE`, `sie.SEIE`, `sstatus.SIE`), lists the disks (`disk: none` without any, "Kernel disks" below), idles in `wfi` printing `tick 1` ... `tick 10`, runs the disk self-test if there is a disk, then starts the user-space shell as the first process ("The shell" below) and returns when it, and every process still running, has ended: the exit code is the shell's exit status (its low 8 bits, so a shell `exit -2` gives 0xFE, the same code as a panic: only the console output tells them apart), or the self-test failure count if any failed (the disk self-test's included). A process left blocked reading the console (a second `sh` started with `&`) keeps the machine running until it reads a line or the end of the input.

For testing the panic path, the image has a word `boot_fault` in `.data`, normally 1. Patching it to 2 makes the kernel execute an illegal instruction after the ticks (with every register but sp set to its number in each byte, x5 = 0x05050505); 3 makes it load from the unmapped address 0x100, a load page fault since paging is on; 4 makes it store to `kmain`'s first instruction, a store page fault since kernel text is read and execute only. Values 5 to 11 misuse the memory API after the ticks, and each must end in a plain `panic: <message>` from the check that catches it: 5 maps the first kernel text page again (`map_pages: remap`), 6 unmaps page 0 (`unmap_pages: not mapped`), 7 maps a page with W but not R (`map_pages: bad permissions`), 8 plants a 4 MiB leaf in the root table and walks it (`walk: unexpected megapage`), and 9, 10 and 11 `kfree` a page pointer plus 4, the kernel's first page and the page after the end of RAM (`kfree`, after a `kfree: bad page <pa>` line). Values 12 to 16 misuse a spinlock named `boot_fault` or the interrupt nesting under it ("Kernel locking"): 12 acquires it twice (`acquire: lock "boot_fault" already held by hart 0`), 13 releases it unheld (`release: lock "boot_fault" not held by hart 0`), 14 starts a kernel process that yields holding it (`sched: holding a lock other than proc_lock`), 15 calls `pop_off` with no `push_off` (`pop_off: no push_off to match`) and 16 turns interrupts back on under a `push_off` before its `pop_off` (`pop_off: interrupts are on`).

Kernel C stays inside what Merlin compiles: no libc headers (`-nostdinc`), no floating point, no inline `asm()` (CSR access and Xcal instructions live in `.s` files, with Xcal instructions written as `.word`), and `.s` files use only merlin-as syntax. `kprintf` uses clang's varargs builtins `__builtin_va_list`/`__builtin_va_start`/`__builtin_va_arg`/`__builtin_va_end` (via `kernel/include/varargs.h`), which Merlin provides with the same ILP32 convention (see "Merlin C subset"). merlin builds every kernel and user C file (`-Ikernel/include`, and `-Ikernel/user` for the user programs), and so the kernel (see "Kernel build"); the code must also build with clang, for the reference build.

### Kernel memory

The kernel manages physical memory in 4 KiB pages and runs with Sv32 paging on (`kernel/kalloc.c`, `kernel/vm.c`), the xv6-riscv design.

**RAM** comes from sysinfo (section 9.5), not from a fixed size or a boot argument: `kinit()` checks `SYS_MAGIC` and `SYS_VERSION` (1), reads `SYS_RAM_BASE` and `SYS_RAM_SIZE`, and panics unless the image starts RAM (`__text_start` = `SYS_RAM_BASE`) and RAM is a whole number of pages. So the allocator follows `--ram`. With 2 GiB, RAM ends at 2^32, which wraps to 0 in 32 bits: the kernel counts pages and compares `pa - ram_base < ram_size` instead of computing an end address.

**Page allocator**: one free list threaded through the free pages (each free page's first word points to the next), holding every page from `__kernel_end` to the end of RAM. `kalloc()` pops a page (NULL when none is left), `kfree(pa)` pushes one and panics unless `pa` is a page-aligned address in that range, `kzalloc()` is `kalloc()` plus zeroing, and `kfree_pages()` is the free count. Unlike xv6, pages are not filled with junk on free or alloc: at about a thousand stores a page it would dominate the boot.

**Page tables**: `pagetable_t` is a page of 1024 PTEs. `walk(pt, va, alloc)` returns the level-0 PTE for `va`, creating the second-level table (a zeroed page) when `alloc` is set. `map_pages(pt, va, pa, size, perm)` maps a page-aligned range with permissions R/W/X/U (W without R and remapping a valid PTE panic), returning -1 if a table can't be allocated. `unmap_pages(pt, va, size, free)` clears mapped PTEs (panics on an unmapped page) and optionally frees the pages. `pagetable_free(pt)` frees the second-level tables and the root, and panics if a leaf is still mapped. Only `kvminit()` creates 4 MiB megapages (leaves in the root table), in the kernel's own address space (below); every other mapping is a 4 KiB leaf, and nothing ever unmaps a megapage, so `walk()` and `unmap_pages()` treat a megapage as corruption and panic (`walk: unexpected megapage`). The kernel's own leaves (`kvminit()`, the kernel stacks, the early page table on RV64) are created with A, D and G set, as Linux's `_PAGE_KERNEL` (`PAGE_KERNEL`, `PAGE_KERNEL_READ`, `PAGE_KERNEL_READ_EXEC` in `vm.h`): a hart that does not set A and D in hardware (Svade) would take a page fault on the first access through a leaf without them, which in the kernel is a panic, and the kernel's mappings are the same in every address space (G). User pages are created with A and D clear: Grail sets them (section 5); `map_pages()` refuses U with A, D or G. The memory self-test's alias page is the one kernel mapping made without them, to check that Grail does.

**Kernel address space**: identity mapped (virtual = physical), so physical addresses (page tables, pages from `kalloc`) stay usable as pointers. `kvminit()` maps

| Range | Permissions |
|---|---|
| `__text_start` .. `__rodata_start`: kernel text | R+X |
| `__rodata_start` .. `__data_start`: read-only data | R |
| `__data_start` .. end of RAM: data, `.bss`, boot stack, free pages | R+W |
| every device in the sysinfo device table but the CLINT and the test device (M-mode's), rounded out to whole pages; a page that devices share is mapped once | R+W |

every leaf with A, D and G, and nothing else: no U, nothing executable outside the kernel text, and the boot ROM, the gaps and address 0 unmapped, so a stray access is a page fault. The devices come from the table rather than from constants, so a new Grail device is mapped without a kernel change. `kvminithart()` then executes `sfence.vma`, writes `satp` (MODE = Sv32, PPN = root), and `sfence.vma` again. Grail has no TLB, so the fences cost nothing there, but the ISA requires them. M-mode code (`machine_vec`) ignores `satp` and keeps using physical addresses.

Each 4 MiB-aligned 4 MiB that the data range or a device covers whole, with its root entry still free, is mapped with one megapage instead of a second-level table of 1024 PTEs: the RAM after the first 4 MiB, the PLIC's 64 MiB (16 megapages). Page by page, the PLIC alone took about 13 million instructions of every boot. Processes share the kernel's root entries ("Kernel processes"), megapages included, all without U. When a fault is reported, `vm_print_mapping()` shows a megapage's PTE and the physical address with the 22-bit offset in it.

Boot order in `kmain`: UART banner and `kprintf` self-test, `kinit()`, `kvminit()`, `kvminithart()`, then the memory self-tests, then `trap_init()`, `blk_init()` and `binit()` ("Kernel disks"), the ticks, the disk self-test and the shell, which run with paging on. A boot to the shell takes about 16 million instructions with 128 MiB and 3 million with the default 4 MiB, most of them freeing and (in the self-test) allocating one page at a time; a few machine timer ticks fire meanwhile and pile up in `sip.SSIP`. `trap_init()` clears that bit before it enables the interrupt, so the first tick the kernel counts is a whole interval long, like the others.

**Memory probes** (`kernel/probe.S`): `probe_load(addr, &val, &stval)` and `probe_store(addr, val, &stval)` make one word access and return 0, or the trap cause with `stval` when it faults. `kernel_trap` recognises an exception whose `sepc` is a probe's access instruction, sets a0 = `scause` and a1 = `stval` in the frame and resumes at `probe_fault`, which stores `stval` and returns (an exception table, as in Linux). The self-tests use them to expect faults. Any other page fault in S-mode panics; a fault in U-mode kills the process instead ("Kernel processes"). The kernel reads user memory through the process's page table rather than through probes (`copyin`).

**Self-tests** (`kernel/vmtest.c`), printed as `vm self-test: N/M passed` and counted in `kmain`'s exit code:

- allocate every free page (each aligned and after the kernel) until `kalloc()` returns NULL, check the count, check that `pagetable_create()` and a `map_pages()` needing a new table fail while memory is exhausted, then free every page and check the count again;
- fill a page with all ones (every word a valid-looking PTE) and free it, then check that `kzalloc()` hands it back zeroed; the same for `pagetable_create()`, which `walk()` also uses. RAM starts out zero on Grail, so only a reused page shows the zeroing;
- map a page a second time at 0x4000_0000, write through each address and read through the other, check the hardware set A and D in the PTE, unmap it (freeing the page) and check a load and a store there are page faults 13 and 15 with `stval` = the address;
- a store to the first word of the kernel text and one to `.rodata` are store page faults (cause 15) caught by the probe, the text is unchanged and readable, and a store to `.data` works;
- build a separate page table, map two pages across a 4 MiB boundary and a three-page range, check the PTEs with `walk()`, unmap, free the table, and check every page came back.

The alias test leaves its second-level table in the kernel page table; everything else ends as it started.

### The RV64 kernel

The same sources build the kernel for RV64 too (#154), as `kernel/kernel-rv64.elf` and `kernel/kernel-disk-rv64.elf`, with merlin's LP64 code (#152) and `kernel/kernel.lds.S` preprocessed for RV64; `cargo xtask kernel` builds both widths (`--xlen 32|64` for one), and `cargo test` checks the committed files as it checks the RV32 ones. The kernel's assembly is one `.S` source per file for both widths (`include/asm.h`, #193). The sizes and offsets it shares with the C (the trap frame's, `struct cpu`'s and `struct spinlock`'s layouts, the kernel stacks' sizes, the CSR bits, the machine calls, the scratch block's slots) are defined once, in the kernel's headers, which the `.S` files include (`-I kernel/include`): each header keeps its C under `#ifndef __ASSEMBLER__` and its constants outside, as Linux's do, `_UL()` (`kernel/include/const.h`, Linux's `_AC`) giving a constant its `ul` in C only, and the C side's `_Static_assert`s check the layouts against the structures (`offsetof`), what Linux's generated `asm-offsets.h` guarantees. It has no user programs to build in yet (#156), so the two RV64 files are the same kernel, and no user space (#155): after the boot self-tests it prints `user space: not on RV64 yet` and returns their failure count, the exit code. Everything this section does not name works as on RV32.

**Layout: a higher-half kernel, Linux's.** User space is the lower half of the address space (Linux's `TASK_SIZE`: 2^47 in Sv48, 2^38 in Sv39; #155 builds it); the kernel is the upper half, root entries 256 to 511, as Linux riscv64 lays it out (`Documentation/arch/riscv/vm-layout.rst`):

| Region | Sv48 | Sv39 |
|---|---|---|
| fixmap (`FIXADDR_TOP`, a page per slot below it): sysinfo and the UART, for the boot | below 0xffff_8d7f_ff00_0000 | below 0xffff_ffc4_ff00_0000 |
| vmalloc/ioremap area (`VMALLOC_START`): the kernel stacks, then the devices | 0xffff_8f80_0000_0000, 32 TiB | 0xffff_ffc6_0000_0000, 64 GiB |
| direct map of all RAM (`PAGE_OFFSET`, RAM's base there) | 0xffff_af80_0000_0000, 64 TiB | 0xffff_ffd6_0000_0000, 128 GiB |
| the kernel image (`KERNEL_LINK_ADDR`) | 0xffff_ffff_8000_0000, the top 2 GiB | the same |

The image is linked at `KERNEL_LINK_ADDR` and loaded at the start of RAM: for RV64, `kernel.lds.S` puts an `AT(0x80000000)` on its first section (as Linux's `vmlinux.lds` writes `AT(ADDR(.text) - LOAD_OFFSET)`), so every segment's `p_paddr` is the load address and `p_vaddr` the link one, and Grail and the bootloader start it at the entry's load address. Its link address is the same in both modes, so the kernel is linked once; what the mode changes are run-time values, set at boot as Linux's `kernel_map` and `PAGE_OFFSET` are: `pt_levels` (4 or 3), `satp_mode`, `page_offset`, `vmalloc_start`, `fixaddr_top`, `direct_map_size`, `user_top`.
