The kernel uses the vmalloc area's first root entry (512 GiB in Sv48, 1 GiB in Sv39), whose table `kvminit()` makes before any process copies the kernel's root entries: the kernel stacks' 64 slots at its start (`KSTACK_AREA`, 2 MiB), then the devices (`IOREMAP_START`), then, halfway in, the paging self-tests' pages (`VM_TEST_AREA`). A driver maps its device with `ioremap(pa, size)`, as Linux's does: the whole pages of the range get room in the area, aligned to their size up to 1 GiB (Linux's `IOREMAP_MAX_ORDER`, a PUD), so they are mapped with the largest leaves the physical address allows too (the PLIC's 64 MiB in 2 MiB ones), wherever the device is in the 48-bit physical address space; nothing is ever unmapped, and a full area is a panic. `kvminit()` maps no device: those no driver asks for (the framebuffer, the keyboard, the pointer) stay unmapped, and so do the CLINT and the test device, which only M-mode uses. The **fixmap** (Linux's, `fix_to_virt()`: slot i at `FIXADDR_TOP` - i pages, the same `FIXADDR_TOP` as Linux's, 16 MiB below the vmemmap region) holds the devices the boot uses before `ioremap()` works: `FIX_SYSINFO` and `FIX_UART`, as Linux's earlycon uses `FIX_EARLYCON_MEM_BASE`. `setup_vm()` maps them and points `sysinfo_regs` and `uart_regs` at them, so `kinit()` reads sysinfo and a panic's report gets out from the moment paging is on; the final table keeps the early table's fixmap tables (as Linux's `setup_vm_final()` keeps `fixmap_pgd_next`), and after `kvminithart()` `sysinfo_map()` and `uart_map()` move both to their `ioremap()` mappings. The boot stack is 16 KiB in the image, aligned to 32 KiB (`kernel.lds.S`).

**Physical and virtual addresses.** A kernel pointer is a virtual address; page tables, `satp` and the devices' DMA registers (`BLK_DMA`, the network card's rings and descriptors) take physical ones. `pa(v)` and `va(p)` (`vm.h`) convert, as Linux's `__pa` and `__va`: `pa()` of a direct-map pointer subtracts `va_pa_offset` (`PAGE_OFFSET` - RAM's base), of an image pointer `va_kernel_pa_offset` (`KERNEL_LINK_ADDR` - the load address); `va()` gives a physical page's direct-map pointer. `kalloc()` hands out direct-map pointers and `kfree()` takes them; `kinit()`'s checks and `ram_base`/`ram_size` are physical; a page-table walk reaches the next table through `va()`, and so do `copyin()` and the other user copies (`user_ptr()`). On RV32 both conversions are the identity. RAM beyond the direct map's room (more than 128 GiB in Sv39) is left out: `kinit()` says how much and frees only what is mapped.

**The boot, with paging off, as Linux's `head.S`.** `_start` and `start()` run in M-mode at the physical address, as on RV32: until paging is on, the kernel reaches its own symbols pc-relatively only (`la`, `call`: `-mcmodel=medany`), which works at either address because the image keeps its layout, and no pointer stored in initialised data (a link address) may be used. M-mode never translates, so `mtvec`, `mscratch` and `mepc` get physical addresses, and `machine_vec` keeps running at them. `supervisor_entry`, in S-mode with paging still off, points `stvec` at a vector that reports a trap and stops (exit code 0xFF), and on hart 0 calls `setup_vm()` (`vm.c`), which reads RAM from sysinfo at its physical address (version 2, section 9.5) and builds the early page table out of page tables in `.bss` (`early_pg_dir` and a pool of `EARLY_TABLES`, 11, `entry.S`): the image at its physical address and at its link address, in 2 MiB leaves (as Linux's `early_pg_dir`); all of RAM in the direct map, exactly, with the largest leaves that fit (1 GiB, then 2 MiB and 4 KiB for its end); the fixmap's two pages, sysinfo's and the UART's, the only devices it maps. It chooses the mode first, as Linux's `set_satp_mode()` does: it builds the tables for Sv48, writes them to `satp`, reads `satp` back and writes 0 again. A hart without Sv48 keeps `satp` unchanged (WARL, section 5), and the tables are then built again for Sv39; the probe runs from code and a stack that the table maps where they are. Every hart then writes `early_satp` to `satp` and jumps to the next instruction's link address, computed as its pc-relative address plus `va_kernel_pa_offset` (a link address is more than 2 GiB from RAM, out of `auipc`'s reach), and from there runs high: hart 0 on its boot stack at its link address, the others, which switch to `kernel_satp` (the final table) first, on their own kernel stacks (`hart_sp[]`, "Kernel SMP"); `stvec` = `kernel_vec`. `kmain` then runs as on RV32: `uart_init()` (through the fixmap), `kinit()` (sysinfo through the fixmap), `kvminit()`, `kvminithart()`, then `sysinfo_map()` and `uart_map()`, which `ioremap()` the two. A failure in `setup_vm()`, with paging off, prints `panic: ...` on the UART at its physical address and stops with exit code 0xFE.

**The kernel page table.** `kvminit()` maps the image at its link address in 4 KiB pages (text R+X, rodata R, the rest R+W), all of RAM in the direct map with 1 GiB and 2 MiB leaves wherever both addresses are aligned and the range covers one (R+W), and the fixmap (the early table's own tables), and makes the table under the vmalloc area's root entry, so that what is mapped there later, the stacks and the devices, is in every process's root table, which copies the kernel's root entries (#155). There is no identity mapping: `kvminithart()` drops the early table, and with it the image's mapping at its physical address. `walk()` goes down `pt_levels` levels and panics on a superpage on the way (`walk: unexpected superpage`); `pagetable_free()` and `uvm_free()` recurse over every level; `map_pages()` and `unmap_pages()` take 64-bit addresses. `satp` is `satp_mode` with the root's physical page number (ASID 0). PTEs are 8 bytes, 512 to a table.

**Kernel stacks.** 16 KiB per kernel thread (`KSTACK_SIZE`, Linux riscv64's `THREAD_SIZE`), in the vmalloc area with an unmapped guard page below (Linux's `VMAP_STACK`): `kstack_alloc()` maps a new stack's four pages (zeroed) in the lower half of a free one of 64 slots of 32 KiB, aligned to 32 KiB, leaving the upper half of the slot below unmapped, and fences its own TLB (`sfence.vma`: the slot may have held an older mapping; another hart reaches the stack only through a switch to a thread on it, which fences, as Linux leaves remote harts to their next fence for a new vmalloc mapping). The trap frame of a process's thread is at the top of its stack. A freed stack stays mapped in its hart's cache for that hart's next `kstack_alloc()`, two at most (Linux's `NR_CACHED_STACKS`); any other goes on a deferred list, since threads are freed holding `proc_lock` and unmapping needs a TLB shootdown, which waits for harts that may be spinning on that lock. `kstack_reclaim()` unmaps the deferred ones and frees their pages, with the shootdown, where no lock is held: at the start of `kstack_alloc()`, and in the scheduler after each switch back, once it has let go of `proc_lock` (as Linux's `vfree_atomic()` defers a stack's `vfree()`). So the memory of threads that ended comes back. The boot's `kstack self-test` checks it: rounds of `NPROC` kernel processes that end at once leave only hart 0's cached stacks mapped, and every other stack's pages free. The other harts' scheduler stacks are such stacks too, and hart 0's boot stack is aligned the same way. `kstack_max_used()` measures the deepest use of any of them (the unmapped ones measured as they go) (each was zeroed when made, and its lowest non-zero word shows how far down it has been used, Linux's `CONFIG_DEBUG_STACK_USAGE`); `kmain` prints `kstack: N of 16384 bytes used at most` and counts a failure if less than a quarter is left.

**Stack overflows.** A stack that overflows faults into its guard page, and the trap would push its frame there and fault again. `kernel_vec` checks first, as Linux's `handle_exception` does: with t0 parked in `sscratch` (free in S-mode, since `user_return` sets it again before every return to U-mode), it tests bit 14 (`KSTACK_SHIFT`) of `sp - FRAME_SIZE`, which is 0 on a stack and 1 below it. A frame that does not fit goes to this hart's overflow stack instead (`overflow_stacks`, 4 KiB per hart, the hart's id scaled in `tp` and put back, since every other register holds a value to save), with the faulting `sp` in it, and `kstack_overflow()` reports

```
panic: kernel stack overflow in <process> (hart N)
  sp 0x..., the stack 0x...-0x...
  <cause>
```

then the trap report below, and halts with 0xFE.

**Traps and the M-mode layer.** The interrupt bit of `mcause` and `scause` is bit 63 (`CAUSE_INTERRUPT` is bit XLEN-1); `machine_vec` compares `mcause` with 0x8000_0000_0000_0007 and 0x8000_0000_0000_0003. The scratch block and `struct trap_frame` have 8-byte slots: the frame is `regs[32]` at 8*n*, then `sepc`, `scause`, `stval`, `sstatus`, `kernel_tp` at 256 to 288, padded to 304 bytes (`TRAP_FRAME_SIZE`). `machine_timer` rearms `mtimecmp` with one 8-byte load and store (#147), and `timer_init()` reads `mtime` whole. The CSR accessors take and return a whole register (`uintptr`), so updating `mstatus` keeps SXL, UXL and SD; `r_time()` returns `time`'s 64 bits on both widths (RV32 reads `timeh`, `time`, `timeh`). `struct context` has 8-byte slots, and `struct cpu` and `struct spinlock` are re-laid out with 8-byte pointers (`CPU_NOFF` 8, `CPU_INTENA` 12, `CPU_PAGETABLE` 136, `CPU_THREAD` 160, `CPU_SIZE` 168; `LOCK_CPU` 8). `r_tp()` returns `tp` sign-extended from bit 31 (`addiw`), as the ABI keeps a 32-bit value. The panic report prints the CSRs and the registers with 16 hex digits (`REG_FMT`, `kprintf`'s `%lx`), and a page fault's mapping line shows the leaf, a superpage's too: `  va 0xffffffff80001234 maps to pa 0x0000000080001234, pte 0x... -A--X-RV`. `kprintf` has `%l` (a register's width) and `%ll` (64 bits) for `d`, `u` and `x`, and `%p` prints every digit of a pointer; the kprintf self-test checks the 64-bit conversions too (33 checks on RV64, 26 on RV32). `illegal_instruction()` fills each register with its number in each of its 8 bytes. `boot_fault` 8 plants a 1 GiB leaf in the kernel half's root and walks it (`walk: unexpected superpage`), and 17 (RV64 only) starts a kernel process, `overflow`, that recurses into its guard page.

**Devices.** The RV64 kernel reads version 2 of the device interfaces (section 9): sysinfo's 64-bit `SYS_RAM_BASE`, `SYS_RAM_SIZE` and device table entries of 24 bytes (`SYSINFO_VERSION` 2), the disk's 64-bit `BLK_CAPACITY` (the kernel uses at most 2^32 - 1 sectors of it), `BLK_SECTOR` and `BLK_DMA`, and the network card's 64-bit ring bases and 16-byte descriptors with a 64-bit buffer address. The drivers reach the registers through `ioremap()`, the UART's before `uart_init()` at its physical address (the mapping on RV32).

**Booting from disk.** `kernel/boot/boot.S` builds `boot-rv64.elf` too (`cargo xtask boot`), which reads ELF64 headers and the disk's version 2 registers and starts the kernel at its entry's load address ("Booting" in `docs/camelot-fs.md`); `camelot-fs mkfs --boot` installs it with `kernel-disk-rv64.elf`, and refuses a bootloader and a kernel of different widths.

**Tests** (`crates/xtask/tests/kernel_rv64.rs`, in `cargo xtask ci`): the committed RV64 kernels are what merlin builds; the image runs high and loads low; the boot runs every self-test on 1, 2, 3 and 8 harts, with the mode's layout in its variables, its page table mapping nothing in the lower half and only the root entries above, and each hart on the kernel table with its stack in the vmalloc area; RAM follows sysinfo; the network comes up with DHCP; the stacks keep three quarters free; every panic path of `boot_fault` reports as above; a bootable RV64 disk (the root image's files, the RV64 bootloader and kernel) boots on 1 and 4 harts through the disk and filesystem self-tests. All of it runs again on harts without Sv48 (`--paging sv39`, `MachineConfig::paging`), where the kernel falls back to Sv39, and, when LLVM is installed, on the reference builds (the ignored `the_rv64_reference_builds_pass_the_same_tests`: clang `-O2` with `--target=riscv64-unknown-elf -march=rv64ima_zicsr -mabi=lp64 -mcmodel=medany`, its `.S` files assembled by clang and then by merlin-as, and ld.lld).

### Kernel console

Console input is interrupt-driven; nothing in the kernel waits on RBR.

- **PLIC** (`kernel/plic.c`): `plic_init()` sets context 1's (hart 0, S-mode) threshold to 0 and calls `plic_enable(1)` for the UART's source (section 7.1): `plic_enable(src)` gives a source priority 1 and sets its bit in context 1's enable register, keeping the others (the disk driver enables each disk's source the same way). Context 0 (M-mode) is left alone and `mie.MEIE` stays clear: every device interrupt goes to the kernel, on hart 0; the other harts' contexts stay disabled ("Kernel SMP"). A claimed source other than the UART's goes to the disk driver's handler.
- **UART** (`kernel/uart.c`): `uart_init()` sets 8N1 and IER.ERBFI, so the UART holds its line high while a byte waits. `uart_intr()`, called for source 1, reads RBR until LSR.DR clears into a 128-byte ring buffer. The console's lock (`cons_lock`) covers the ring, which the handler fills and readers drain. Transmitting takes the UART's own lock, `uart_tx_lock`, for each `kprintf` and for each piece of up to 64 bytes a `write` to the console sends, so lines from different processes or harts never mix byte by byte. If it fills up, the handler leaves the remaining bytes in the UART and clears IER instead of dropping them (a byte left in RBR would keep the line high and the interrupt firing). `uart_rx_pop()` sets IER again once the ring is half empty. Transmit still polls LSR.THRE, which Grail always reports as set.
- **Console** (`kernel/console.c`): the console device, which the `read` and `write` system calls reach through an open file of major 1 (the first process's descriptors 0-2, or `/dev/console`; "Kernel files", `docs/camelot-syscalls.md`). Input is line-buffered, like a terminal in canonical mode: `console_read()` takes bytes from the ring buffer into a 128-byte line, editing and echoing as it goes, and sleeps (`sleep` on the line buffer) until the line is complete; the UART interrupt (`console_intr()`: `uart_intr()`, then wake the readers) brings it back. Printable characters (0x20-0x7E) are echoed and kept, up to 127; BS (0x08) and DEL (0x7F) erase one, echoing BS, space, BS; CR, LF or CR LF ends the line, echoing and keeping a LF (a LF right after a CR is skipped, so both raw consoles (CR) and piped text (LF, CR LF) work); Ctrl-D (0x04) ends the line without one, so on an empty line the read returns 0, the end of the input. Other control characters are ignored. A read returns at most the rest of the current line; what it leaves goes to the next read. Echoing happens when a process reads rather than in the interrupt handler, so input typed ahead is echoed after the prompt that asks for it, not in the middle of other output. While everyone sleeps, the scheduler idles in `wfi` ("Kernel processes").

Host side: the `grail` command feeds its stdin to the UART. When stdin is an interactive terminal (a Windows console, or a Linux terminal), Grail switches it to raw mode while it runs: each key is passed on as typed, the terminal does not echo it (the kernel does), and Ctrl-C still stops Grail. The terminal is restored at exit and on Ctrl-C. With a pipe or file nothing changes, so `printf 'ticks\nexit\n' | grail kernel/kernel.elf` runs the shell non-interactively. Such input must end with `exit` (or the run needs `--max-steps`): when it runs out, the kernel just waits at the prompt for more, taking timer ticks, and Grail never exits. In a raw Windows console a lone Ctrl-Z is passed on as 0x1A like any other key (the shell ignores it), not taken as the end of the input. Without `--clock`, a kernel waiting for input still takes every timer tick (`wfi` skips to the next one), so the tick count grows by far more than 10 a second; `--clock 10MHz` makes it real time.

### Kernel processes

User programs run in U-mode, each in its own address space, and take turns on the CPU, one timer tick at a time (`kernel/proc.c`, `kernel/uservec.S`, `kernel/swtch.S`, `kernel/elf.c`, `kernel/syscall.c`). The interface they see, system calls, start-up state and errors, is specified in `docs/camelot-syscalls.md`. The design follows xv6-riscv for one hart, with one difference in the address space, below.

**Address space.** User space is the low 192 MiB, `0` to `USER_TOP` = 0x0C00_0000: root entries 0 to 47 of an Sv32 page table, up to the PLIC, the lowest device the S-mode kernel uses. (Below it are only the test device and the CLINT, which only M-mode touches: the timer and IPIs go through `machine_vec`, and S-mode reads the clock through the `time` CSR, which `start` lets it with `mcounteren.TM`.) Going higher would mean mapping the devices somewhere else in the kernel's half, which the identity-mapped RAM fills up to 0xFFFF_FFFF on a 2 GiB machine. Page 0 is never mapped, so a null pointer faults; programs are linked at 0x1000 (`kernel/user/user.ld`). The layout (`kernel/include/vm.h`):

| Range | What |
|---|---|
| 0x0B80_0000-0x0BFF_FFFF | the **stack**, at most 8 MiB (`USER_STACK_MAX`, Linux's default limit): its top page, 0x0BFF_F000, is mapped at the start with the program's arguments at its top and `sp` below them; the rest is mapped on demand as it grows down |
| 0x0B7F_F000-0x0B7F_FFFF | an unmapped **guard page** under the stack's limit (`USER_STACK_LIMIT`) |
| 0x0000_1000-0x0B7F_EFFF | the program's segments (which must end at or below `USER_LOAD_TOP` = 0x0B7F_F000), then the **heap** (`sbrk`), from the page after the last segment up to the guard page |

**The stack grows on demand**, as Linux's does, so a program pays in RAM only for the stack it uses (`proc_grow_stack`, `kernel/proc.c`). A load or store page fault in U-mode at an address between the stack's lowest mapped page (`stack_low` in `struct proc`) and its limit maps zeroed pages from there down to the faulting one, and the instruction runs again. The kernel reads and writes user memory through the page table and never faults on it, so a system call would see a buffer in a stack frame the program has not touched yet (a big local array passed to `read`) as unmapped: every system call first maps the stack down to the caller's `sp`, since whatever a program has on its stack is at or above `sp`. Below `sp`, the stack's range is not the program's memory yet, and a system call given such an address fails with `-EFAULT`. A thread whose stack is on the heap has `sp` outside the stack's range and grows nothing. Overflowing the 8 MiB reaches the guard page: a page fault, which kills the process with the usual report. If RAM runs out first, the kernel says `pid N (name): no memory to grow its stack to 0x...` and the fault kills the process. Growth takes the process's `heap` lock, as `sbrk` does ("Kernel locking"), and only adds mappings, so it needs no TLB shootdown, only an `sfence.vma` on the hart that faulted.

A process's page table has two user second-level tables from the start (the program's at the bottom, the stack's at the top) and one more per other 4 MiB the heap or the stack reaches. Every other root entry of a process's page table is a copy of the kernel page table's: the process shares the kernel's second-level tables, so the kernel's mappings (text, data, all of RAM and the devices, none with U) are present in every address space without being copied or freed per process. The kernel's own root entries 0 to 47 are empty (only the test device and the CLINT live there, which the kernel doesn't map: S-mode doesn't use them), so its global leaves (G) never stand for a user page; the process's own replace them. This is why the kernel must not add root entries once processes exist.

Why a shared kernel half rather than xv6's trampoline page: the kernel is identity mapped at 0x8000_0000 and up, far from user space, so the two never collide, and with the kernel mapped in every table a trap from U-mode needs no `satp` switch and no trampoline; the scheduler switches `satp` once per context switch. U-mode still can't touch the kernel, since no kernel PTE has U: a load from 0x8000_0000 in U-mode is a load page fault. `sstatus.SUM` stays clear, so S-mode can't touch user pages through user addresses either: the kernel reads user memory through the process's page table instead (`copyin(pt, dst, va, len)` walks it and copies from the physical pages, which the kernel's identity map makes reachable). `uvm_range_ok(pt, va, len, perm)` checks that every byte of a range is below `USER_TOP` and on a page mapped with V, U and `perm`; `copyin` copies only if the whole range passes, so a bad buffer copies nothing.

**Processes and threads** (`struct proc` and `struct thread`, `kernel/include/proc.h`). A process is what a program runs in: a slot in a table of 8 with a state (unused, new, live, zombie), a pid (counting from 1, never reused), a name, the parent, the exit status, the page table, the heap's start and end, its open files (`ofile[16]`, its descriptors) and working directory (an inode, or none without a filesystem; "Kernel files"), and how many of its threads haven't ended. A thread is what runs: a slot in a table of 16 with a state (unused, new, runnable, running, sleeping, zombie), a thread id, its process, while sleeping the channel it sleeps on, a kernel stack (one page), a trap frame and a saved kernel context. A process starts with one thread, its main thread, whose tid is the pid; a program adds more with `thread_create` ("Kernel threads" below). A slot is new while `spawn` builds the process in it: loading a program from the disk sleeps, and meanwhile the slot is taken but not yet runnable. The trap frame is the same `struct trap_frame` `kernel_vec` uses, at the top of the kernel stack page; the thread's kernel code runs on the rest of the page, below it. The state `ps` shows for a live process is its threads': running if one runs, else runnable if one waits for a CPU, else sleeping.

**Kernel processes.** `kproc_create(name, fn, &err)` makes a process whose one thread is a kernel thread: it runs the C function `fn` in S-mode on its own kernel stack (the whole page: there is no trap frame), with interrupts off like any process's kernel code, and ends (`proc_exit(0)`) when `fn` returns. It has no user space: no page table of its own (the scheduler runs it on the kernel page table) and nothing to free but its stack. It takes a slot and a pid like a user process, but has no parent, so the scheduler frees it as soon as it ends. The kernel uses them for work that has to sleep, such as disk requests made outside a system call (the disk self-test, "Kernel disks").

**Trap entry and exit.** While a process runs in U-mode, `stvec` = `user_vec` and `sscratch` points at its trap frame. `user_vec` swaps `sscratch` into a0, saves every register (the user's a0 from `sscratch`) and `sepc`, `scause`, `stval`, `sstatus` in the frame, sets `sp` to the frame (so each trap from U-mode starts on an empty kernel stack) and calls `user_trap(frame)` in C. That first points `stvec` back at `kernel_vec`, and keeps interrupts off (the trap cleared `sstatus.SIE`) until the process is back in U-mode, so the kernel never takes an interrupt on a process's kernel stack. Then:

- supervisor software interrupt (the tick): `ticks` + 1, wake the processes sleeping on `ticks`, and `yield()`, the end of the time slice;
- supervisor external interrupt: serviced as in `kernel_trap` (waking the console's readers), and the process continues;
- ecall from U-mode (cause 8): `sepc` + 4, then `syscall(frame)`, which runs the call numbered a7 with the arguments in a0-a5 and puts the result in the frame's a0 (`docs/camelot-syscalls.md`);
- any other exception: the process is killed (below).

`user_trap_return(frame)` goes back: interrupts off, `stvec` = `user_vec`, the frame's `sstatus` gets `SPP` = U (0) and `SPIE` = 1, then `user_return(frame)` (assembly) sets `sscratch` = frame, `sepc` and `sstatus` from the frame, loads every register (a0 last) and executes `sret`, which enters U-mode with interrupts on.

**Context switch.** `swtch(old, new)` (`kernel/swtch.S`) saves `ra`, `sp` and `s0`-`s11` in `*old` (`struct context`: ra at 0, sp at 4, s0-s11 from 8) and loads them from `*new`; the caller-saved registers are already assumed clobbered by any call. The scheduler runs on `kmain`'s boot stack. A new thread's context has `ra` = `thread_start` and `sp` = its frame, so its first `swtch` lands in `thread_start`, which calls `user_trap_return` with its frame: for a process's main thread, a frame that holds only `sepc` = the ELF entry point, `sp` and the arguments, `a0` = argc and `a1` = argv, which `proc_create` copied to the top of the stack page; for another thread, the frame `thread_create` filled in.

**Scheduler.** `scheduler()` schedules threads, not processes. It loops: turn interrupts on and off again (pending interrupts are taken in between, and their handlers may wake threads), pick the next runnable thread round robin starting from the slot after the last one that ran (from slot 0 at the start, so processes start in the order they were created), mark it running, write its process's page table (the kernel's for a kernel process) to `satp` (with `sfence.vma`), and `swtch` to it. When the thread gives the CPU back, it restores the kernel page table and, if the thread ended, frees its kernel stack, and the process too if that was its last thread and nobody will collect it (an orphan). When nothing is runnable but processes exist (they all sleep), it idles in `wfi`, with interrupts off from the search to the `wfi` so that an interrupt arriving in between still wakes it. It returns, with interrupts on, when every process has been freed. A thread gives up the CPU only in the kernel: `yield()` on a tick or for the `yield` call (it stays runnable), `sleep(chan)` when it must wait (for input, for a child, for ticks to pass, for a disk, for another thread; below), or when it ends (it becomes a zombie). Kernel code is never preempted: a tick that arrives while it runs is taken by the scheduler afterwards. Both tables are protected by one spinlock, held across every switch between a thread and the scheduler ("Kernel locking" below); the scheduler of each hart keeps its registers and the thread it runs in the hart's `struct cpu`. The loops that run on every tick (`pick_next`, `wakeup`) walk the thread table with a pointer rather than an index: merlin's code would otherwise multiply (and, for the round robin's wrap, divide) on every step, and with 16 slots that shows in what a tick costs. On a machine of several harts every hart runs this loop over the same table ("Kernel SMP"); only hart 0's returns.

**Sleep and wakeup** (xv6's). `sleep(chan, lk)` marks the current thread sleeping on `chan`, any address that stands for the event (the console's line buffer, a parent waiting for its children, the tick counter, a disk, a buffer, a pipe's counts, the network interface, a thread being joined, a futex), and switches to the scheduler; `wakeup(chan)` makes every thread sleeping on `chan` runnable, and may be called anywhere, interrupt handlers included. `lk` is the spinlock that protects the condition the caller waits for, held when it checked the condition: `sleep` takes the process lock before it lets go of `lk` (and takes `lk` back before it returns), and `wakeup` takes the process lock too, so a wakeup that comes after the check finds the thread asleep and none is lost ("Kernel locking" below). Callers check their condition in a loop, since a wakeup only says it may have changed.

**Ending.** `exit` (the system call), from any of the process's threads, first ends the process's other threads ("Kernel threads"), then makes the process a zombie that keeps its exit status until its parent collects it with `wait`, wakes the parent, and orphans its children (a zombie child is freed at once, a running one when it ends). A fault in U-mode (any exception but ecall: a page fault, an illegal instruction such as `xcal.halt`, which is M-mode only, a misaligned access...) kills the process and prints

```
pid N (name) killed: <cause name>
  scause 0x........  sepc 0x........  stval 0x........
```

and, for a page fault, how the process's page table maps `stval` (the same line as a panic's), then ends it like `exit(-1)`, whichever thread faulted. The kernel is unaffected. Freeing a process (`uvm_free`) frees every page mapped in user space (its segments, stack and heap), the user-space second-level tables and the root, and clears the copied kernel entries first so their tables stay; each thread's kernel stack went when the thread ended: once a program has been collected, every page it used is back on the free list.

**Loading programs** (`elf_load`, `kernel/elf.c`). User programs are ELF32 executables: files of the root filesystem (the shell's are in `/bin`), or, without one, the copies embedded in the kernel's `.rodata` (`userprogs.s`, generated at build time, see "Kernel build"; the disk kernel has none), whose table `user_programs` holds `{ name, image, size }` for each and `user_program_count` says how many ("Kernel files" says which a path names). The loader reads an image through a `struct elf_file` (`kernel/include/elf.h`): its size and a function that copies bytes from an offset, from memory for an embedded program, through `readi` for a file (locked, in a transaction, for the whole load), so a file is read straight into the pages it is loaded to and never copied whole. The loader treats an image as untrusted: it must be `\x7fELF`, 32-bit, little-endian, RISC-V, `ET_EXEC`, with 32-byte program headers (at most 16) inside the file; each `PT_LOAD` with a non-zero `p_memsz` must have `p_filesz <= p_memsz` and its file bytes inside the image, must be readable (`PF_R`), must lie in 0x1000..0x003F_E000, and must not share a page with another segment. Each page gets a fresh zeroed page from the allocator, the segment's file bytes copied in, mapped with U, R, plus W for `PF_W` and X for `PF_X`. The entry point must be 4-byte aligned (`sepc` would silently clear its low bits, starting the program somewhere other than its header says) and on a page mapped with U and X. A failure gives a reason (`not an ELF file`, `not a 32-bit little-endian RISC-V file`, `not an executable`, `bad program headers`, `segment outside the file`, `segment not readable`, `segment outside user space`, `segments share a page`, `misaligned entry point`, `entry point not in an executable segment`, or `out of memory`), and whatever was allocated is freed; `spawn` prints `spawn: PATH: REASON` (`exec`: `exec: PATH: REASON`) and fails with `-ENOEXEC` (`-ENOMEM` for `out of memory`, without a message). The stack page is mapped after the segments, R+W+U and zeroed. The heap starts at the page after the highest segment.

**System calls** (`kernel/syscall.c`, and `kernel/sysfile.c` for the file ones; numbers in `kernel/include/syscall.h`), specified in `docs/camelot-syscalls.md`: the Linux RISC-V convention (number in a7, arguments in a0-a5, result in a0, negative error numbers with Linux's values, every other register preserved) and 46 calls: `exit`, `getpid`, `yield`, `sleep`, `spawn`, `wait`, `exec`, `sbrk`, `uptime`, `kill` and `procs` for processes, `thread_create`, `thread_exit`, `thread_join`, `gettid`, `futex_wait` and `futex_wake` for threads ("Kernel threads"), `harts` for the harts ("Kernel SMP"), `read`, `write`, `open`, `close`, `lseek`, `fstat`, `dup`, `pipe`, `mkdir`, `unlink`, `link` and `chdir` for files ("Kernel files"), and `netinfo`, `netconf`, `ping` and the thirteen socket calls for the network ("Kernel network"). There is no `fork`: `spawn(path, argv)` starts the program at `path` as a child of the caller, which inherits its descriptors 0-2 and working directory, and `wait` collects it; `exec(path, argv)` replaces the caller's program and keeps the rest of the process. `kill(pid)` marks a process killed (`proc.killed`), and each of its threads: the process exits with status -1 the next time one of them would return to user mode, and a thread sleeping in `sleep`, `wait`, `thread_join`, `futex_wait`, a pipe or the console is woken and gives up the call first, so the kill takes effect even when it never runs another instruction. `procs(buf, max)` copies a `struct procinfo` (pid, parent, state, size, name) for each process, in pid order; `ps` prints them. Pointer arguments are user addresses that the kernel checks against the process's page table (`uvm_range_ok`) and copies through the physical pages (`copyin`, `copyout`, `copyinstr`), so a bad pointer is an `-EFAULT` and never a kernel fault. `xcal.dbg` works in U-mode too (section 9.1).

**User programs** (`kernel/user/`). Every `X.c` or `X.s` there is a program called `X`, and so is every subdirectory `X/` other than `lib/` that holds `.c` or `.s` files: a program of several files, each compiled and assembled on its own and linked together in name order (`kernel/user/as/` is `as`). A program is linked with the runtime, `crt0.s` (`_start`: `call main`, then `call exit` with its result) and `usys.s` (a two-instruction stub per system call), and with the C library of `kernel/user/lib/` (strings and memory, numbers, `malloc`, `printf` and `snprintf`, a line reader, `strerror`, `stat`; `kernel/user/lib/README.md`). Everything is declared in `user.h`, the only header programs include, with the calls' constants and structures. The library is linked like an archive (merlin-ld's `--start-lib`, "Linker" above): each of its files is a member that goes into a program only when the program needs one of its functions, so a small program stays small. `kernel/user/README.md` lists the programs and their usage. C files are compiled freestanding (no libc); see "Kernel build" for how they are built. `cargo xtask fs` puts them in `/bin` on the root image, and the kernel embeds them for when there is no disk.

| Program | What it does |
|---|---|
| `sh` | the shell, below |
| `hello` | prints `hello from user mode`, exits 0 |
| `echo` | prints its arguments separated by single spaces; `-n` leaves out the newline |
| `ls` | lists each directory named (`.` without one), or describes each file: a line per entry as the kernel's boot listing prints it (`%c %4u %2u %7u %s`: type, inode, links, size, name) |
| `cat` | copies each file named, or its input, to its output |
| `wc` | counts the lines, words and bytes of each file named, or of its input: `L W B [NAME]`; `-l`, `-w` and `-c` pick the counts |
| `mkdir`, `rm`, `ln` | make directories, remove files and empty directories, link `OLD` to `NEW` |
| `cp`, `mv` | copy (`cp SRC DST`) or rename (`mv SRC DST`, `link` then `unlink`) files; with a directory as the last argument, copy or move each file into it under its own name |
| `grep` | `grep [-v] [-c] [-n] PATTERN [FILE...]`: prints the lines that contain a fixed string (`-v` the others, `-c` their count, `-n` with line numbers); exits 0 if a line matched, 1 if none did, 2 on an error |
| `ps` | lists the processes (`procs`): pid, parent, state, size, name |
| `kill` | `kill PID...`: kills each process (`kill`) |
| `sleep` | `sleep SECONDS`: sleeps for whole or tenths of seconds (`0.5`), at 10 ticks a second |
| `ticks` | prints `ticks: N`, the timer ticks since boot (`uptime`) |
| `ding`, `dong` | print `ding 1` ... `ding 4` (`dong ...`), half a tick (500 000 instructions) apart, exit 0; run side by side they alternate |
| `regs` | sets every register but t6 to its number in each byte, spins for about three ticks, exits 0 if all still hold their values, else with the first one's number: preemption must not change a register |
| `sysregs` | for each of a table of system calls (`getpid`, `yield`, `sleep`, `read` with a bad descriptor, an unknown number, `sbrk`, `uptime`, `write`), sets every register to a pattern, makes the call and checks that every register but a0 kept its value and that a0 holds the expected result; prints `sysregs: write` (the `write` case), then `sysregs: every register but a0 kept`, and exits 0, else exits with 32 times the case's index plus the first changed register's number: the calling convention's "every register but a0 is preserved", which C programs can't check |
| `data` | checks that its stack page below `sp` and its `.bss` (two pages, the second with no file bytes) read zero, that its `.data` word holds its initial value, and that `.data` and `.bss` take stores; then fills its stack and `.bss` with non-zero bytes, so the pages it frees are dirty for the next program; exits 0 or the failing check's number |
| `as` | the assembler: `as [-o out.o] input.s`, merlin-as rewritten in C (`kernel/user/as/`, "Self-hosting" in section 10), writing the object the host's merlin-as writes for the same input. Only in `/bin`: the kernel does not build it in (`DISK_ONLY` in `crates/xtask/src/userprogs.rs`), since without a disk it has no files to read, and its 100 KB would not fit a 2 MiB machine |
| `ld` | the linker: `ld [-T script] [-e entry] [-o out] [-S] input.o ... [--start-lib lib.o ... --end-lib] [@file]`, merlin-ld rewritten in C (`kernel/user/ld/`, "Self-hosting" in section 10), writing the executable the host's merlin-ld writes for the same objects and script. Only in `/bin`, as `as` |
| `spacetest` | checks the address space: 200 KiB of stack (grown on demand), a system call writing into a stack frame not touched yet, and the pointer checks at the edges of the new layout (the end of user space, the guard page, the stack below `sp`, past the heap); `spacetest heap MIB` grows the heap by MIB MiB, writes every page and shrinks it back; `spacetest overflow` recurses until the kernel kills it; prints `spacetest: N checks, F failed` |
| `badptr` | passes every call that takes a pointer bad ones (kernel memory, page 0, across `USER_TOP`, lengths that wrap, the guard page, read-only memory for `read`, `fstat` and `pipe`, past the heap, paths and `spawn` and `exec` arguments that run off the end of the heap): each must fail with `-EFAULT` and have no effect (a pipe keeps its bytes), and an unknown call number with `-ENOSYS`; then prints `badptr: every bad pointer refused` with a good `write` and exits 0, or prints `badptr: case N failed` and exits with N, the first case that failed |
| `systest` | checks every system call from user space (results, error numbers, exit statuses and arguments through `spawn` and `wait`, a full process table, `sleep`, `sbrk` growing and shrinking the heap, descriptors, pipes, `exec`), with or without a disk; prints `systest: N checks, 0 failed`. `systest exit S`, `systest sleep T` and `systest args ...` are its child modes; `systest sbrk N` grows its heap by N bytes, prints `systest: sbrk N: R` (0 or the error) and exits 0 if the heap is as it should be afterwards, unchanged after a failure (a test runs it on a 2 MiB machine, where memory runs out partway). `systest spin` spins until it is killed: the `kill` checks use it |
| `filetest` | checks the file system calls on a writable root filesystem, in a directory `/ft` it removes again (`open`'s flags, offsets shared and not, directories, links, removing files still open, working directories, running programs from files, every error of a bad path or descriptor, a file past 268 KiB, emptied by `O_TRUNC`); prints `filetest: N checks, 0 failed`. `filetest ops` makes a fixed sequence of changes for the crash tests, `filetest ro` checks a read-only root |
| `ifconfig` | `ifconfig [ADDR [MASK [GATEWAY]]]`: prints the network interface and its counters (`netinfo`), or gives it a static address (`netconf`) |
| `ping` | `ping HOST [N]`: N ICMP echo requests (`ping` call) to an address or a name, a line per reply and a summary; exits 0 if one came |
| `nc` | netcat: `nc [-u] [-v] HOST PORT` connects, `nc -l [-u] [-v] PORT` listens, over TCP or UDP, and copies its input to the socket and the socket to its output at once (two processes: it spawns itself as the sending half, the socket as that one's output) |
| `fetch` | `fetch http://HOST[:PORT][/PATH]`: an HTTP/1.0 GET to its output; exits 1 for a status other than 2xx or an answer cut short |
| `httpd` | `httpd [-p PORT] [-n COUNT] [-t SECONDS] [DIR]`: an HTTP/1.0 server for DIR's files, one connection at a time, logging each request |
| `shd` | `shd [-p PORT] [-n MAX] [-v]`: the shell server, a shell of its own for each TCP connection to PORT (23), at most MAX at once; hangs up a session whose client left (`fstat` on a socket: `nlink` 0 once its other end hung up); `herald --tcp` is its client (`kernel/user/README.md`) |
| `nettest` | `nettest connect ADDR PORT BYTES [TIMES]` and `nettest serve PORT CONNECTIONS [BACKLOG]`: known bytes through TCP and their hash, for the TCP tests |
| `socktest` | checks the socket calls over loopback (every call's errors, ports in use, timeouts, shutdown, running out of sockets); prints `socktest: N checks, 0 failed`; needs a network card |
| `stress` | `stress [WORKERS [ROUNDS]]` (default 4 and 4, at most 6 workers): spawns WORKERS copies of itself that in each round grow the heap by four pages, fill and check them across timer ticks and shrink it back, print `stress W: round R`, send 200 bytes down a pipe shared by all of them that stress reads meanwhile (each byte names its worker and its place in that worker's stream, since writes into a nearly full pipe interleave), and, with a filesystem, write, read back and remove a file `stress.W`; prints `stress: W workers, R rounds, N bytes: ok` and exits 0, else says what failed and exits 1: the locking tests ("Kernel locking") |
| `threads` | `threads [THREADS [ROUNDS]]` (default 4 and 500, at most 8 threads): THREADS threads add 1 to a shared counter ROUNDS times each under a futex mutex, by a read, some work and a write that would lose updates without it, and to another under a spinlock; prints `threads: T threads, R rounds: counter N, spun N: ok` and exits 0 when both hold T times R, else says what went wrong and exits 1 ("Kernel threads") |
| `threadtest` | checks the thread and futex calls (statuses through `thread_join`, joining a thread that already ended, every error, futexes, running out of thread slots, the mutex and `malloc` from several threads, `sbrk` from three at once, a pipe `write` whose buffer another thread unmaps, `exit`, `kill`, `exec` and a fault with other threads running, the last thread's `thread_exit`); prints `threadtest: N checks, 0 failed`. `threadtest fs`, which needs the root filesystem, checks threads in file calls while another `exec`s, exits, is killed or closes their descriptor (`threadtest fs: N checks, 0 failed`). `threadtest live`, `hang`, `last`, `exec`, `alone`, `fault`, `execread`, `execwrite`, `ioexit` and `iokill` are its child modes |
| `libtest` | checks the C library (strings, numbers, `snprintf`, `malloc`, the line reader, addresses); prints `libtest: N checks, 0 failed`. `libtest feed N` is its child mode, which writes N lines in 7-byte pieces that cut lines in two, for the line reader to read |
| `badload` | loads from 0x8000_0000: killed by a load page fault |
| `badstore` | stores to its own `.rodata` (mapped without W): killed by a store page fault |
| `badhalt` | executes `xcal.halt`: killed by an illegal instruction |

**The shell** (`kernel/user/sh.c`), the first process: `kmain` starts it with `proc_run_init("/bin/sh")` (from the root filesystem if it has `/bin/sh`, else the embedded copy; the disk kernel, which has none, panics) and runs the scheduler until it and every process it left running have ended, then stops the machine with its exit status. It prints `Type help for the list of commands.`, then reads lines after the prompt `$ `. A line is split into jobs at `&`, each job into commands at `|` (a pipeline, each command's output the next one's input) and each command into words at blanks, with `< FILE` (input from FILE) and `> FILE` (output to FILE, made or emptied) among them; the operators need no blanks around them. The first word names a built-in or a program: `NAME` is `/bin/NAME`, a word with a `/` the program at that path (from the working directory unless it starts with `/`); the program gets the words as its `argv`. A job followed by `&` runs in the background (the shell prints `[PID]` for each of its programs); the last one runs in the foreground and the shell waits for it, printing `NAME: exit S` for each program whose status is not 0 (-1 when the kernel killed it). With no `fork`, the shell sets a program's descriptors up before spawning it: it puts the file or pipe end at 0 or 1 (`close`, then `open` or `dup`, which take the lowest free descriptor), spawns, and puts its own console back from copies it keeps above 2, which no child inherits. Built-ins (in the shell itself, not in a pipeline): `help`; `exit [N]`, which ends the shell with status N, or else the last foreground program's; `wait`, which collects every background program, printing `[PID] NAME: exit S` for each; `cd [DIR]` (`/` without DIR). A program that can't be started, or a file that can't be opened, prints `sh: NAME: REASON` (`strerror`, e.g. `no such program`, `no such file or directory`, `no free process slot`). The end of the input (Ctrl-D on an empty line) ends the shell like `exit`. For example:

```
$ ding & dong & badload
[2]
[3]
ding 1
ding 2
dong 1
dong 2
pid 4 (badload) killed: load page fault
  scause 0x0000000d  sepc 0x00001018  stval 0x80000000
  va 0x80000000 maps to pa 0x80000000, pte 0x2000004b -A--X-RV
badload: exit -1
$ wait
ding 3
ding 4
dong 3
dong 4
[2] ding: exit 0
[3] dong: exit 0
$ exit
```

Background programs keep printing while the shell waits for its next line, so their output can land after a prompt, as in any Unix shell (above, the input was typed ahead, so `wait` was read and echoed at once).

### Kernel disks

The kernel drives Grail's disks (section 9.6) with an interrupt-driven driver (`kernel/blk.c`) and keeps recently used blocks in a buffer cache (`kernel/bio.c`), the layer a filesystem builds on. Both follow xv6-riscv (`virtio_disk.c`, `bio.c`), simplified for a device that takes one request at a time.

**Finding disks.** `blk_init()`, called after `trap_init()`, walks the sysinfo device table (section 9.5) and takes every entry of type 9 in table order: the first is disk 0 (with Grail, the first `--disk`). Base address, capacity and PLIC source all come from the table and the disk's registers, none from constants. An entry without a source (0) or with one above 31 is skipped, since the driver doesn't poll. For each disk it reads `BLK_CAPACITY` and `BLK_FLAGS`, acknowledges a leftover `BLK_STATUS.DONE` (so the line starts low), sets `BLK_CTRL.0` and enables the source with `plic_enable()`. The disks' pages are already mapped, as `kvminit()` maps every device in the table. `kmain` then prints a line per disk, `disk N: S sectors (K KiB), irq I`, with `, read-only` for a read-only disk, or `disk: none` when there is none, in which case the kernel boots as before.

**Requests.** `blk_read(dev, sector, buf, count)`, `blk_write(dev, sector, buf, count)` and `blk_flush(dev)` each run one command and return its `BLK_ERROR` code: 0 (`BLK_OK`) or one of the codes of section 9.6 (`BLK_ERR_BAD_COMMAND` to `BLK_ERR_IO` in `kernel/include/blk.h`); a refused command moves no data. `buf` is a kernel address, which the kernel's identity map makes the physical address `BLK_DMA` wants. Each disk has at most one request in flight: a process that finds the disk busy sleeps on the disk until the request in flight has been taken. The process then writes `BLK_SECTOR`, `BLK_DMA`, `BLK_COUNT` and `BLK_CMD`, and sleeps on the disk until the completion interrupt; nothing reads `BLK_STATUS`. Requests sleep, so they must come from a process (a system call, or a kernel process, "Kernel processes"): a request made outside one panics, as does a disk number that doesn't exist. Grail runs a command during the store to `BLK_CMD`, so its interrupt is already pending when the process goes to sleep and is taken as soon as the scheduler lets interrupts in; with a slower disk the scheduler runs other processes, or idles in `wfi`, until it comes.

**Interrupt.** A claimed PLIC source other than the UART's goes to `blk_intr(irq)`, which finds the disk with that source, reads `BLK_ERROR`, writes 2 to `BLK_STATUS` (clearing DONE lowers the line, so the source isn't pending again when the trap code completes it), marks the request done and wakes the processes sleeping on the disk. The process that made the request takes the error code, marks the disk free and wakes the next one waiting for it.

**Buffer cache.** A block is 1 KiB (`BSIZE`), two sectors: block `n` of a disk is its sectors 2`n` and 2`n` + 1. 1 KiB rather than one 512-byte sector, as in xv6: half the requests and buffers for the same data, and the filesystem's bitmap and inode blocks hold twice as much. The cache has 54 buffers (`NBUF`: the 44 the log may pin, `LOGSIZE`, and 10 for the blocks operations hold at once, the room xv6's 30 left beside its 20), each a `struct buf` (`kernel/include/bio.h`) holding a block's data, its disk and block number, a valid flag, a lock flag, a reference count and its place in a doubly linked list ordered by when it was last released. They live in `.bss`, so they are part of the kernel image's memory, not taken from the page allocator (`_start` zeroes them with the rest of `.bss`). The interface:

- `bread(dev, blockno)` returns the block's buffer, locked. A block is cached when a buffer holds it with valid data, or with users (its read is under way): that buffer is taken, waiting for its lock if another process holds it. Otherwise the least recently released buffer without users is taken over for the block. If the buffer's data isn't valid, `bread` reads the block with `blk_read`; `b->error` is then `BLK_OK` or the read's error code, and the data of a failed read stays invalid, so a failed read is never cached. Blocks from 2^31 on (whose sectors would pass 2^32) fail with `BLK_ERR_OUT_OF_RANGE` without a request. The counters `bio_hits` and `bio_misses` count the calls served from the cache and the ones that read the disk.
- `bwrite(b)` writes a locked buffer's data to its block at once and returns the error code. The cache is write-through: after a failed write the cached copy is marked invalid and the next `bread` reads what the disk holds. The one exception is the filesystem's log, which changes buffers in place and writes them only when its transaction commits ("Kernel filesystem"): `bpin(b)` pins such a buffer (one more user, so it is never reused for another block) until `bunpin(b)` after it has been written.
- `brelse(b)` unlocks the buffer, wakes the processes waiting for it and, once nobody uses it, moves it to the front of the list, or to the back if its data isn't valid (its read or write failed): a buffer that holds nothing is reused before any cached block.

The buffer's lock is a sleep lock (`struct sleeplock`, "Kernel locking" below), held from `bread` to `brelse` and across the sleep in the driver, so each block has one copy in the cache and only its holder reads or changes it. `bread` panics when every buffer is in use, and `bwrite` and `brelse` panic on a buffer that isn't locked.

**Self-test** (`kernel/blktest.c`). With a disk attached, after the ticks (and the deliberate fault, if there is one), `kmain` tests disk 0 in kernel processes and prints `blk self-test: N/M passed`; a failed check prints `blk self-test FAILED: <check>` and counts in `kmain`'s exit code. A disk 0 with fewer than two blocks (4 sectors) is not tested (`blk self-test: disk 0 is too small, skipped`): one reader needs block 1, and the scratch block must not be block 0, which the readers check. The disk ends as it started:

- three kernel processes at once, two reading block 0 and one block 1. The first reader's request is in flight when the block-1 reader asks for the disk, so that one waits for it; the second block-0 reader finds the buffer locked, waits for the first to release it and gets the same bytes from the cache. Two disk reads in all;
- one kernel process: block 0 in the cache matches the disk (read past the cache with `blk_read`); the scratch block, the disk's last whole block, is read, written with a pattern (byte `i` = `i`·7 + 3), read back from the cache, read past the cache to check the disk holds the pattern, written back with its old contents and checked, and the disk is flushed. On a disk holding a Camelot filesystem (its magic at the start of block 1: disk 0 is usually the root, whose files may own that block), the "pattern" is the block's own contents, so a machine stopped between the two writes leaves the disk as it was. On a read-only disk the write must fail with `BLK_ERR_READ_ONLY`, the next `bread` must read the disk again, and the disk must be unchanged. Then the errors: the block just past the end fails with `BLK_ERR_OUT_OF_RANGE`, twice, since a failed read isn't cached, and a read into the boot ROM's address (not RAM) fails with `BLK_ERR_BAD_DMA`. Last, if the disk has at least `NBUF` + 1 blocks, LRU reuse: blocks 0 to `NBUF` - 1 are read in turn (filling every buffer), then block 0 again, then block `NBUF`, which must take block 1's buffer, the least recently used: blocks 0 and `NBUF` are then hits and block 1 a miss. (Evicting the most recently used buffer instead would drop block 0.)

On a 64 KiB read-write disk that is 16 checks (14 when it is read-only). `crates/xtask/tests/kernel.rs` runs it on in-memory disks and checks, instruction by instruction, that each command is written only once the previous result has been acknowledged in `blk_intr`, that `BLK_STATUS` is never read, which sectors were written with what, the sleeps on the disk and on a buffer, the cache counters and which buffer each miss took, and that a held-back interrupt leaves the kernel idle in `wfi` instead of spinning.

### Kernel filesystem

At boot, after the disk self-test, the kernel mounts disk 0 as its root filesystem. The format, CMFS, is specified in `docs/camelot-fs.md` (superblock, write-ahead log, 128-byte inodes whose contents ext4's extent trees map, free-block bitmap, directories of 16-byte entries, the commit protocol and the consistency rules); the kernel's implementation (`kernel/log.c`, `kernel/fs.c`) follows xv6's, with ext4's extent trees for the contents, on top of the buffer cache, and the host's (`crates/camelot-fs`: mkfs, fsck, a reader, and the `camelot-fs` command) is a second implementation from the same document that the tests check the kernel against.

**Layers.** `log.c`: transactions. A filesystem operation that writes runs between `begin_op()` and `end_op()` and changes blocks with `log_write(b)` instead of `bwrite`: the block is recorded in the transaction (once, however often it changes) and its buffer pinned. Operations of several processes may share a transaction (group commit): `begin_op` waits while a commit runs or while the operations in progress, at `MAXOPBLOCKS` (22) blocks each, the worst case of an operation (docs/camelot-fs.md, "Limits on transactions"), could overflow the log (`LOGSIZE`, 44 blocks, or fewer if the disk's log is smaller; one with fewer than `MAXOPBLOCKS` slots is only read, see "Mounting"), and the last `end_op` commits: blocks to the log's slots, flush, header (the commit point), flush, blocks home, flush, empty header, flush. `fs.c`: mounting (`fs_mount` checks every superblock rule and the log header, replays a committed transaction, checks the root is a directory, finishes the truncations a crash interrupted, and frees the orphans: inodes a crash left allocated with no links, between an unlink and the last `iput`); the block allocator (`balloc` takes its goal, the block after the file's last, if it is free, so a file written in one go is one extent, else the first clear bit of the bitmap at or after `datastart`, and logs the block zeroed; `bfree`); inodes (`ialloc`, and a cache of 120 in-memory inodes, `iget`/`idup`/`iput` counting users and `ilock`/`iunlock` a sleep lock that reads the dinode on first use; `iput` frees an inode whose last link and last user are gone, truncating it); contents (`readi`, `writei`, growing a file block by block, no holes, through its extent tree: `ext_append` grows the last extent, or adds one, in a new leaf and new index nodes when the rightmost ones are full, growing the tree a level when even the root is, with every new tree block allocated before anything changes, so a disk that fills up leaves the tree as it was); directories (`dirlookup`, `dirlink`, reusing free slots); paths (`namei`, `nameiparent`, looking each name up from the root, or from the process's working directory for a path without a leading `/`, so `.` and `..` are ordinary entries); and the operations the file system calls are built on ("Kernel files"), `fs_create` (file, directory with `.` and `..`, or device node; never in a directory that has been removed), `fs_unlink` (directories only when empty), `fs_link` (files only) and `itrunc` (for `open`'s `O_TRUNC`, and `iput`): it frees the blocks from the end in as many transactions as they take, of their own: it ends the caller's operation first (committing what it logged, an unlink's entry say, so that a crash leaves an orphan for the next mount) and begins another after, with the inode unlocked in between each, so a caller holds no other sleep lock when it may free an inode (`fs_create` lets go of the directory first, and `fs_link` looks both its paths up before it writes anything, as Linux's `linkat` does); the inode marked `INODE_TRUNCATING` (on the disk only if it takes more than one) so that a crash leaves the rest to the next mount, as Linux's ext4 does through its orphan list. A file that others can reach is truncated under its write lock, `wlock`, which `write` holds too, taken before `begin_op` like Linux's `i_rwsem`, so no writer comes in between the transactions. Failures come back as minus Linux's error numbers (`ENOENT`, `EEXIST`, `ENOTDIR`, `ENOSPC`, `ENAMETOOLONG`, `ENOTEMPTY`, `EROFS`, ...; `kernel/include/fs.h`). A disk error under a mounted filesystem panics: the log keeps the disk consistent, and the next mount replays or drops the transaction as after a crash.

**Mounting.** `kmain` calls `fs_boot()` (`kernel/fstest.c`), which mounts in a kernel process (disk requests sleep). A disk smaller than two blocks, or without the magic, gets `fs: no filesystem on disk 0`; a broken one `fs: disk 0 not mounted: REASON` (a bad superblock field, a bad log header, a root that is not a directory, a read-only disk whose log needs replaying), and either way the kernel boots on without a filesystem. A read-only disk is mounted read-only: `fs_create`, `fs_unlink` and `fs_link` return `-EROFS`, as does `open` for writing a file, and `log_write` panics. So is a disk whose log has fewer than `MAXOPBLOCKS` slots (`nlog` < 23, which the format allows): an operation alone always starts, and could overflow it. On success the kernel prints `fs: replayed N blocks from the log` if it replayed one, `fs: finished N truncations` if it finished some, `fs: freed N inodes that no directory named` if it freed orphans, then `fs: disk 0 mounted: S blocks (F free), I inodes (J free), L log blocks` (`, read-only`, or `, read-only: the log is too small to write`), then `fs: ls /` and one line per entry of `/`: type (`d`, `f`, `c`), inode, links, size, name (`%c %4u %2u %7u %s`, which `camelot-fs ls` prints too).

**Self-test.** Then, in the same process, read checks on whatever the disk holds, printed as `fs self-test: N/M passed` and counted in `kmain`'s exit code like the other self-tests: the root is inode 1, a directory, and its own `.` and `..`; every spelling of `/` (`//`, `/.`, `/..`, `.`, ...) finds it; each entry of `/` is found by its path; a missing name fails with `-ENOENT`, a 15-byte name with `-ENAMETOOLONG`, a path through a file with `-ENOTDIR`; `nameiparent` splits off the last name and fails on `/`; the first file of `/` reads back whole in pieces of 333 bytes and of 4 KiB, and nothing past its end; and when there is a `/bin`, each file there named after an embedded program is byte for byte that program (`fs: /bin holds N of the kernel's M programs`), or, for the disk kernel, which embeds none, `/bin` holds at least one file (`fs: /bin holds N programs, the kernel none`). With `fs_test` patched to 2 (the tests do; the image ships with 1) on a writable disk, transactions follow: `/fstest` and `/fstest/a` (2500 bytes) in one, `/fstest/b`, removing `a`, a one-block `/fstest/x`, `/fstest/big` (13 KiB + 100 bytes) written 3 KiB per transaction; before big's 13th block, `x` is removed while a reference to it is held (the unlink commits `nlink` 0, the last `iput` frees it a transaction later), and big must go on growing next to its last block (its goal), not in x's freed one, the first free: two extents; making and removing an empty `/fstest/d`; two kernel processes making `/fstest/p1` and `p2` at once, whose operations must share one commit; then begin_op()'s waits, made to happen with more processes and counted (`log_room_waits`, `log_commit_waits`): an operation beginning after one in progress has written must wait for its commit, one that waits because two operations are in progress joins their transaction when one of them ends without writing, and one beginning while a commit sleeps on the disk waits for it; then the contents read back, big removed (both its extents), the errors of `fs_create`, `fs_unlink` and `writei`, that a file past 2^31 - 1 bytes is too big to open, the free block and inode counts, the number of commits (24: removing a file with blocks is two, the unlink and the freeing), and a listing of `/fstest`. With `fs_test` = 3, on a disk with 13 free blocks, `/full` and `/pad` take a block each in turn, four times, so `/full` is four extents (its root full), and `/pad` all but one of the rest: adding a block to `/full` then needs a tree block too, and fails with `-ENOSPC` changing nothing (the block it took goes back); with `/pad` removed, `/full` is written 3 KiB per transaction until the disk is full (`fs: the disk filled up at N bytes`), its tree a level deeper, no block lost. With `fs_test` = 4, on a disk the tests made with two files, `/t1` and `/t2`, whose 1200 blocks each alternate (every block an extent of its own, trees two deep), both read back, a block added to `/t1` reads back, removing `/t1` takes more than one transaction, and truncating `/t2` as `O_TRUNC` does empties it, every block coming back. With `fs_test` = 5, `/g1` and `/g2` are written a block per transaction in turn, 340 each, so their trees grow every way there is: from depth 0 to 1 at the 5th extent, a full leaf split at the 85th, from depth 1 to 2 at the 337th; each of those appends logs what docs/camelot-fs.md counts (4, 4 and 5 blocks, `log_blocks`), none more than an append's worst case, and with `/pad` leaving 2 free blocks the 337th first fails with `-ENOSPC`, giving back the data block and the tree block it took, its tree as it was.

**Root image.** `cargo xtask fs` builds the root image with the `camelot-fs` crate's mkfs: `kernel/fs/` (a README) at `/`, `/dev/console` (a device node, major 1) and every user program, built by merlin-as and merlin-ld as the kernel embeds them, in `/bin` (`target/camelot-fs.img`; `-o` changes that). It is bootable (section 7, "Boot from disk"; `docs/camelot-fs.md`, "Booting"): the bootloader in the boot block and, after the filesystem, the disk kernel (`kernel/kernel-disk.elf`, "Kernel build"), only the parts the bootloader loads. The image is **4 MiB in all**, the boot block, the filesystem and the kernel together: the kernel takes about 300 blocks of 1 KiB and the filesystem the rest, of which the programs leave about three fifths free for the shell to write to (the boot test requires at least a fifth, and says to enlarge the image when `/bin` outgrows it). `--size N` makes an image of N blocks instead, the filesystem growing with it; one too small for the programs is refused with a message that says so. `grail --disk target/camelot-fs.img` boots it in the default 4 MiB of RAM, and the shell then runs its programs from `/bin`; `grail --disk target/camelot-fs.img kernel/kernel.elf` runs the kernel with its own programs on the same filesystem (`--no-boot` builds the filesystem alone, N blocks, as many as the root image by default, so every program has the room it has there).

**Tests** (`crates/xtask/tests/fs.rs`, on in-memory disks that record every sector written and every flush). The kernel's listing of the root image is the host's, and reading changes nothing. After the write test, fsck finds the disk consistent and the host reads back what the kernel wrote, and every commit followed the protocol, step by step. The crash test stops the machine after every sector the write test writes (so also with a block half written): replayed, every one of those disks passes fsck, and its files are the state before or after each transaction, never in between, changing exactly at each commit point; the kernel then boots on each of them, replays the log exactly as the host does (the disks end byte for byte equal) and passes its self-test. One run stops the machine for real right after the first commit point and reboots: the kernel replays the transaction (home blocks, flush, empty header, flush) and the files are there. Disks that can't be mounted are reported and left untouched, and a read-only one is read but never written, nor is one whose log is too small to write. Filling a disk until a block needs a tree block there is no room for leaves the tree as it was (fsck checks), and the disk self-test, stopped after either of its writes to a block that belongs to a file, leaves the disk as it was. The crash test's write test leaves an orphan between two commits, so the reboots also check that the mount frees it as `fsck --recover` does. The tree test runs on trees the host built, so the kernel reads, grows and frees trees it did not make, and the grow test's trees are the kernel's own, which the host reads back; the machine stopped after every sector of the grow test leaves disks that pass fsck, each a commit's state, and the kernel boots on each one around every growth and recovers it as the host does. The shell's `rm` of those big files on a disk with the smallest writable log (23 blocks) shows that their freeing takes transactions of its own, and `cat` of a file whose inode says 2 GiB gets `-EOVERFLOW`; the machine stopped after every sector its truncations write leaves disks that pass fsck, some with `TRUNCATING` set and the rest still to free, which the kernel's next mount finishes as `fsck --recover` does. `crates/camelot-fs` has its own tests: layouts and encodings, mkfs round trips of host directories through the reader and the `camelot-fs` command, every kind of damage fsck must find, and the log's replay.

### Kernel files

The file system calls, after xv6's: descriptors and open files, devices, pipes, the calls that take paths, and running programs from the disk. The interface they give programs is in `docs/camelot-syscalls.md`.

**Open files** (`kernel/file.c`, `kernel/include/file.h`). A process's descriptors are its `ofile[16]`, each NULL or a pointer into the kernel's table of 64 open files (`struct file`): what kind of file it is (an inode of the filesystem, a device, or one end of a pipe), how many descriptors point at it, whether it may be read and written, and for an inode the offset the next read or write uses. `open` and `pipe` take free entries and the lowest free descriptors, both before anything else, so a full table fails (`-ENFILE`, `-EMFILE`) having made nothing; `dup` and `spawn` add a descriptor for the same entry, which then shares its offset; `fileclose` drops one, and the last closes the file: a pipe end is closed, an inode let go (`iput`) in a transaction of its own, which frees it if it was removed while open (an orphan until then, docs/camelot-fs.md). A child gets its parent's descriptors 0-2 and nothing above, so a pipe end the shell keeps for itself doesn't stay open in every program it starts. The first process starts with three console files: 0 for reading, 1 and 2 for writing. `exec` keeps every descriptor; `exit` closes them all and lets go of the working directory.

**Devices.** An inode of type 3 (a device node) opens as a device file, which reads and writes through `devsw[major]`, a driver's pair of functions on user memory. Major 1 is the console (`console_read`, `console_write`, "Kernel console"), and `/dev/console` on the root image is its node; any other major has no driver and `open` fails with `-ENODEV`. A device has no offset: `lseek` on one is `-ESPIPE`, as on a pipe.

**Reading and writing files.** `fileread` and `filewrite` copy between user memory and a kernel page (one per call, `-ENOMEM` if none is free) and between that page and the inode (`readi`, `writei`, the inode locked) at the file's offset, which they advance. A write goes to the disk in pieces of at most 4 KiB (`MAXWRITE`), each a transaction of its own: a piece changes at most its four new blocks and the one it starts in, their bitmap blocks, the extent tree's rightmost leaf, the inode and one split of the tree, within `MAXOPBLOCKS` (22). A crash part way through a longer write leaves the file with a whole number of pieces, never a torn block. The whole write holds the file's write lock (`wlock`), so no truncation, and no other write, comes in between its pieces. A write stops early when the disk fills up or the file reaches the kernel's largest, 2^31 - 1 bytes (its offsets are 32-bit until #155), and fails with `-ENOSPC` or `-EFBIG` if it wrote nothing. Reading never writes the disk (there are no access times). `lseek` stays within the file (`-EINVAL` past its end): files have no holes.

**Pipes** (`kernel/pipe.c`, `kernel/include/pipe.h`). A pipe is a page holding a 512-byte ring buffer (`PIPESIZE`), the counts of bytes read and written, and whether each end is still open; its two ends are open files, one readable and one writable. A writer copies bytes in while there is room and sleeps on the write count when the pipe is full, waking the readers; a reader sleeps on the read count while it is empty and a write end is open, then takes what is there (at most what it asked for) and wakes the writers. With every write end closed, a read of an empty pipe returns 0, the end of the input; with every read end closed, a write fails with `-EPIPE`, or returns the bytes it put in before. Each pipe has a spinlock, held while a reader or a writer looks at the ring and copies, and given up only by `sleep` ("Kernel locking"); each sleep is in a loop on its condition, as everywhere ("Kernel processes"). A write longer than the room left goes in as the room frees up, so the bytes of several writers to one pipe may interleave (there is no atomic `PIPE_BUF` size). The page goes back with the last end.

**Paths and the working directory.** A process's working directory is an inode it holds (`cwd`); `namei` starts there for a path without a leading `/`. The first process gets the root, children their parent's, `chdir` changes it (to a directory: `-ENOTDIR`), letting go of the old one in a transaction, since that may free a directory removed meanwhile. Without a root filesystem a process has none, and every call that takes a path returns `-ENOENT`. The kernel copies a path from user memory with `copyinstr`, at most `MAXPATH` (127) bytes and a NUL: `-EFAULT` if it isn't readable up to its NUL (checked before anything else, so the result is the same with or without a filesystem), `-ENAMETOOLONG` if it is longer, `-ENOENT` if it is empty.

**The calls on paths** (`kernel/sysfile.c`). Each one that may change the disk is one transaction (`begin_op` to `end_op`), so a crash leaves all of it or none, but for emptying a file: `open` with `O_CREAT` makes a file unless the name exists (a file there is opened; a directory is opened only for reading, `-EISDIR` otherwise; a device as a device; a file past 2^31 - 1 bytes is `-EOVERFLOW`), and with `O_TRUNC` empties a regular file opened for writing, after the open's transaction, in as many of its own as freeing its blocks takes (`itrunc`, under the file's write lock); `mkdir` makes a directory with `.` and `..`; `unlink` removes a name (a directory only when empty, never `.` or `..`); `link` gives a file another name in any directory (`-EPERM` for a directory, which has exactly one). A read-only root fails each change with `-EROFS` and opens no file for writing; reading, running programs and `chdir` work as ever.

**Running programs** (`kernel/exec.c`, `kernel/include/exec.h`). `spawn` and `exec` look the path up like any other and need a regular file (`-EACCES` for a directory or a device), which the loader reads with its inode locked in a transaction ("Loading programs"), so it can't be freed under it. `spawn` builds the new process (`proc_create`) and makes it runnable; `exec` builds the new address space first (`proc_exec_load`), reading the whole image, then closes the file (its inode lock and transaction) and only then ends the process's other threads and frees the old space (`proc_exec_commit`): a failure leaves the caller running its old program with the error, and a sibling thread waiting for that inode or for room in the log can finish its call and end. Its name becomes the path's last name. Without a root filesystem, the embedded programs stand in for `/bin`: `/bin/NAME`, or `bin/NAME` from the root, which is then everyone's working directory, names the program NAME, and nothing else exists. The first process runs `/bin/sh` from the filesystem when it has one, else the embedded shell, so a disk without `/bin` still boots to a prompt.

**Tests.** `crates/xtask/tests/fs.rs` boots the committed kernel on images built by mkfs, with commands typed for the shell. On the root image, the shell runs `ls` from `/bin` (its listing is the host's), writes a file with `>`, reads it back through a pipe and with `<`, and runs a program by a path of its own (a link to `/bin/echo`); the host reads the files the kernel wrote, fsck finds the disk consistent and every commit followed the protocol. `filetest` passes on the root image and leaves it as it was, block and inode counts included. The crash test of "Kernel filesystem" runs again for `filetest ops` (making, linking, truncating and removing files and directories, one removed while open): every disk the machine could have stopped at replays to the state before or after a commit, and the kernel, rebooted on each, recovers it as the host does. `filetest ro` on a read-only root gets `-EROFS` for every change and writes nothing. `crates/xtask/tests/kernel.rs` runs `systest` and `badptr` (every call's errors, pipes and `exec` without a disk), and the shell's pipelines and its fallback to the embedded programs.

### Kernel network

The kernel's network stack (`kernel/net/`, one file per layer; `kernel/include/net.h`, `nic.h`) is specified in `docs/camelot-net.md`: a driver for the network card of section 9.8, Ethernet, ARP, IPv4, ICMP echo, UDP and TCP, an address by DHCP or a static configuration, and loopback. In short:

- **Card.** `net_init()`, after the disk list, takes the first `DEV_NET` (11) entry of the device table as the one interface (`net 0: mac ..., irq I, link up`; others are listed `, not used`; with none nothing is printed and the kernel boots as before). Its rings hold 16 receive and 16 transmit descriptors in one page; frames live in a fixed pool of 64 two-KiB buffers (32 pages), so the network can't take the rest of memory: with the pool empty a received frame is dropped and a send fails with `-ENOBUFS`. Frames are received in the card's interrupt (`nic_intr()`, tried after `blk_intr()` in the trap code) and handled there from end to end, answers included; nothing polls.
- **Waiting.** One spinlock, `net_lock`, covers the whole stack ("Kernel locking"). A process waiting for the network sleeps on the interface, with that lock; arrivals and `net_timer()` (every tick, from `clock_tick`, which also ages the ARP cache) wake it to check its condition, as every sleep does.
- **Address.** `net_boot()`, after `fs_boot()`, runs the DHCP client in a kernel process and falls back to a static configuration in four patchable `.data` variables (`net_dhcp`, `net_static_addr`, `net_static_mask`, `net_static_gateway`; by default DHCP, else 10.0.2.15/24 with gateway 10.0.2.2), printing `net: A/N, gateway G, dns D (dhcp)` or `(static)`.
- **Limits.** One interface, no IPv4 options, fragmentation or forwarding (fragments are dropped, packets over 1500 bytes refused, DF always set), an ARP cache of 16 holding one packet per pending address, 8 UDP sockets of 4 queued datagrams. Every frame dropped is counted under its reason (18 drop counters, 22 with the traffic counters, and 5 more for what TCP's connections went through), never a panic.
- **TCP** (RFC 9293, `tcp.c`): 16 sockets, each connection with a 4 KiB send and receive buffer (a page each) and the receive buffer's free room as its window; the MSS option; RFC 5961's checks (a reset believed only at the exact next sequence number, challenge ACKs otherwise and for SYNs); RFC 6298's retransmission timer in ticks with "go back N" on a timeout, zero-window probes, retries that give up with `-ETIMEDOUT`; TIME-WAIT of 60 s; RFC 6528 initial sequence numbers. Out-of-order data is dropped and resent by the peer; no congestion control, delayed ACKs, SACK or window scaling.
- **Interface to programs.** The system calls `netinfo` (24), `netconf` (25) and `ping` (26), used by the `ifconfig` and `ping` programs, and the socket calls, 27 to 39 (`socket`, `bind`, `listen`, `accept`, `connect`, `send`, `recv`, `sendto`, `recvfrom`, `shutdown`, `getsockname`, `getpeername`, `setsockopt`; `docs/camelot-syscalls.md`, "Sockets"): BSD's subset for IPv4 TCP and UDP, with Linux's numbers and `struct sockaddr_in`. A socket is an open file (`FD_SOCKET`, `kernel/net/socket.c`) on UDP's and TCP's kernel-internal APIs, so `read`, `write`, `dup`, `spawn` and `close` work on it; `SO_RCVTIMEO` bounds `recv` and `accept`. The user library resolves names by DNS (`resolve`, to the server DHCP gave), and `nc`, `fetch`, `httpd` and `shd` are built on it all. Port 7 runs a UDP echo service while no socket has it.

**Tests** (`crates/grail/tests/kernel_net.rs`, `kernel_tcp.rs`, `kernel_sockets.rs`; `crates/avalon/tests/sockets.rs`): DHCP and pings on `--net user` (in-process and through the `grail` command), UDP echo through a forwarded port, two kernels on a hub pinging each other, and crafted frames from the test on a hub port: every malformed one dropped and counted under its reason, the stack answering correctly afterwards, and ARP giving up on an address nobody has. For TCP: large transfers both ways with a host server through the gateway, a guest echo server behind a forwarded port, both again through a backend that drops, duplicates and reorders frames, many connections leaving no socket, page or buffer behind, and the test as the other host driving the state machine segment by segment (handshakes, resets, challenge ACKs, closes from either side, retries, zero windows).
