For the sockets: `socktest`'s checks; `httpd` serving files from the machine's disk to host clients through a forwarded port; `fetch` from a host server by address and by a name a test resolver answers; `nc` both ways with host programs, and between two kernels on an Avalon switch over TCP and UDP; `shd` sessions side by side through a forwarded port (`kernel_shd.rs`): one in `sleep 5000` while another and the console keep working, a client leaving in the middle of a command (hung up), the session limit, and no process, socket or page left behind.

### Kernel locking

Every structure that more than one thread of control can reach (several processes, several threads of one process, a thread and an interrupt handler, several harts: "Kernel SMP" below) is protected by a lock, after xv6-riscv: spinlocks for short stretches, sleep locks for data held across a sleep, and `sleep(chan, lk)` to wait for a condition without losing a wakeup.

**Spinlocks** (`kernel/spinlock.S`, `kernel/spinlock.c`, `kernel/include/spinlock.h`). `struct spinlock` is `locked` (0 or 1), the `struct cpu` holding it and a name. `acquire(lk)` first turns interrupts off on the hart (`push_off`), then swaps a 1 into `locked` with `amoswap.w.aq` until it gets a 0 back, then executes `fence rw, rw` and records the holder; `release(lk)` clears the holder, executes the fence, stores 0 with `amoswap.w.rl` and calls `pop_off`. Interrupts stay off while a hart holds any spinlock, so an interrupt handler that takes a lock (the console's, the disk's, the network's, the tick counter's) never interrupts code holding the same lock and spins forever. `push_off()`/`pop_off()` nest: each `struct cpu` counts them (`noff`) and remembers whether interrupts were on before the outermost one (`intena`); the last `pop_off` turns them back on only then. The four are hand-written assembly: they need `amoswap.w`, `fence` and `sstatus`, which C reaches only through inline assembly (Merlin has none), and they run on every `kalloc`, `kprintf`, `sleep` and `wakeup`. Misuse panics, naming the lock and the hart: `acquire: lock "NAME" already held by hart N` for a lock the hart already holds (it would spin on itself forever), `release: lock "NAME" not held by hart N`, `pop_off: interrupts are on` (something turned them on under a lock) and `pop_off: no push_off to match`. A lock's name is set by `initlock(lk, name)`.

**Per-hart state.** `struct cpu cpus[NCPU]` (8, Grail's most harts) holds, for each hart, the thread it runs and that thread's process (`mythread()` is `mycpu()->thread`, `myproc()` is `mycpu()->proc`), `noff` and `intena`, its id, and the scheduler's saved context (`swtch` goes back and forth between it and a thread). The kernel keeps the hart's id in `tp`: `_start` sets it from `mhartid` in M-mode and nothing in the kernel changes it, so `mycpu()` is `&cpus[tp]`, three instructions. User programs may use `tp` as they like (it is an ordinary register to them), so each trap frame holds the hart's id (`kernel_tp`, at offset 144, written by `user_trap_return`) and `user_vec` loads `tp` from it after saving the user's; `user_return` restores the user's `tp` with the other registers.

**Sleep locks** (`kernel/sleeplock.c`, `kernel/include/sleeplock.h`). A sleep lock is a flag, the holder's tid and a spinlock protecting both: `acquiresleep` sleeps (on the lock, with its spinlock) while another thread holds it, and `releasesleep` wakes the waiters. They guard what a thread holds while it sleeps: a buffer from `bread` to `brelse` (across the disk request), an inode from `ilock` to `iunlock` (across the reads and writes of its blocks) and a process's heap during `sbrk` (which allocates and shoots down). They may be held across `sleep`; a spinlock may not.

**Sleep and wakeup.** `sleep(chan, lk)` takes the process lock, lets go of `lk`, marks the thread sleeping on `chan` and switches to the scheduler; when it runs again it lets go of the process lock and takes `lk` back. `wakeup(chan)` takes the process lock to mark the sleepers runnable. A waiter checks its condition holding `lk`, and whoever changes the condition holds `lk` too when it calls `wakeup`, so the wakeup can't fall between the check and the sleep: it either comes before the check (which then sees the change) or finds the thread asleep. `sched()`, the way into the scheduler, panics unless the caller holds the process lock and nothing else (`sched: proc_lock not held`, `sched: holding a lock other than proc_lock`), so a thread can't sleep holding a spinlock another would need to wake it.

**The locks**, by what they protect:

| Lock | Kind | Protects |
|---|---|---|
| `kmem` (`kalloc.c`) | spin | the free page list and the count of free pages |
| `proc` (`proc.c`) | spin | the process and thread tables: every process's state, parent, thread count, `killed`, `exiting` and exit status, every thread's state, channel, `killed` and status, pid and tid allocation, and each hart's switch to and from a thread (held across `swtch`, as in x86 xv6, rather than one lock per process); futexes wait and wake under it |
| `files` (`proc.c`) | spin, one per process | the process's descriptors (`ofile`) and working directory, which any of its threads may change (`fd_get`, `fd_install`, `fd_take`, `proc_cwd`, `proc_set_cwd`) |
| `heap` (`proc.c`) | sleep, one per process | the heap's end, the stack's lowest page and the page table's user mappings: `sbrk` and stack growth one thread at a time |
| `uvm` (`vm.c`) | spin | every user copy (`copyin`, `copyout`, `copyinstr`) against `unmap_pages`, which takes it once before freeing what it unmapped, so no thread copies through a page another thread's `sbrk` has freed |
| `ticks` (`trap.c`) | spin | the tick count, written by the timer interrupt; `sleep` (the system call) waits on it |
| `console` (`console.c`) | spin | the line being edited and the UART's receive ring, between the UART interrupt and the readers |
| `uart` (`uart.c`) | spin | transmitting: each `kprintf`, echo and piece of up to 64 bytes of a console `write` goes out whole; not taken while panicking |
| `disk` (`blk.c`) | spin, one per disk | the disk's request in flight and its completion |
| `bcache` (`bio.c`) | spin | the buffer list, which block each buffer holds, the user counts, pins and the hit and miss counters |
| `buffer` (`bio.c`) | sleep, one per buffer | the buffer's data, from `bread` to `brelse` |
| `log` (`log.c`) | spin | the log's state: operations in progress, the committing flag, the blocks of the transaction |
| `itable` (`fs.c`) | spin | the inode cache: which inode each entry holds and its user count |
| `inode` (`fs.c`) | sleep, one per cached inode | the inode's fields and contents, from `ilock` to `iunlock` |
| `ftable` (`file.c`) | spin | the open file table's reference counts |
| `pipe` (`pipe.c`) | spin, one per pipe | the ring, its counts and which ends are open |
| `net` (`net/net.c`) | spin | the whole network stack: the card's rings, the buffer pool, ARP, the sockets, TCP's connections and timers, the interface's configuration. The network system calls run under it (`syscall.c`), and so do the card's interrupt, `net_timer`, the DHCP process and reads and writes of a socket file; a process waiting for the network sleeps with it |
| `fstest` (`fstest.c`) | spin | the boot self-test's count of processes still running |

Each thread's own fields that only it touches (its trap frame, its context, the socket file of the call it is in) need no lock, nor does a process's page table and heap while it has one thread (`exec` changes them only after ending the others); nor does what is written once at boot before any process exists (the device table, the disks found, the superblock) or read as one aligned word where a stale value does no harm (`ticks` for `uptime`). The boot self-tests run their kernel processes on hart 0 before any other hart starts.

**Lock order.** Locks are always taken in this order, so no cycle of holders waiting for each other can form, on one hart or several:

1. Sleep locks: the log's admission (`begin_op`, which waits for room in the log), then inode locks (a directory before an inode in it, so path lookup goes down the tree), then buffer locks. A process's `heap` lock is apart: `sbrk` takes no other sleep lock under it, and no other is held when it is taken.
2. One of `net`, `console`, `pipe`, `disk`, `log`, `ticks`, `fstest`: never two of these at once.
3. `files` (also under `net`: a socket call puts its new descriptor in the table under it).
4. `itable` (also under `files`: `proc_cwd` takes a reference to the working directory).
5. `ftable`, `bcache` or a sleep lock's spinlock (also taken under the levels above: `fd_get` takes a reference to a file under `files`, `log_write` pins a buffer under `log`, `iput` takes a free inode's sleep lock under `itable`).
6. `proc`: `sleep` and `wakeup` take it under any of the above.
7. `kmem`, `uart` and `uvm`, the leaves: nothing is taken while one is held, so `kalloc`, `kfree`, `kprintf` and the user copies work under any other lock.

**Tests.** `crates/xtask/tests/kernel.rs` patches `boot_fault` ("Kernel traps") to 12 to 16 and requires the five misuse panics, word for word. The `stress` program (`stress [WORKERS [ROUNDS]]`, "User programs") runs six workers, with the shell and itself every process slot, through the shared paths at once under timer preemption: the page allocator (four pages each round, filled, checked across ticks and freed), the console (a line each round), one pipe they all write while stress reads it (each byte names its writer and its place in that writer's stream, so interleaved writes are checked byte by byte) and, on a filesystem, a file each writes, reads back and removes. Without a disk, the test requires every round's line once and in order per worker, stress's verdict, timer ticks preempting every worker in the middle of its rounds (switches whose last trap from U-mode was a tick, taken while the worker was between growing its heap and shrinking it), every page back on the free list, and, whenever a process enters U-mode, no spinlock held and no `push_off` pending; on the root image (`crates/xtask/tests/fs.rs`), the same verdict, fsck finding the disk consistent and as it was, and every commit following the protocol. Another test kills two workers mid-run and requires the same of the locks and the pages, and one spoils a frame's `kernel_tp` while its process is in the kernel and requires `user_trap_return` to write it back. "Kernel SMP" runs `stress` again on 4 harts, where the locks are contended for real.

### Kernel SMP

The kernel runs on every hart Grail has (`--harts N`, 1 to 8; section 7, "Harts"), in the default 4 MiB of RAM and from the root image alike (`kernel/smp.c`, `kernel/include/smp.h`). On one hart nothing differs from before, the boot output included.

**Booting.** Hart 0 boots alone, as on a one-hart machine, up to the network's address and with every self-test, while the others wait in the boot ROM's park loop (section 7, "Boot"); the self-tests' kernel processes thus run on hart 0 only. Then `smp_boot()` reads `SYS_HARTS` from sysinfo (at most `NCPU`, 8), takes a page from the allocator for each other hart's stack (`hart_boot_sp[i]` = the physical address of its top, which `_start` loads for `start()` in M-mode; on RV32 `hart_sp[i]` is the same page; on RV64 `hart_sp[i]` is the top of a kernel stack from `kstack_alloc()`, which the hart switches to once it runs at its link addresses, "The RV64 kernel"), and wakes them all with one IPI. Each comes out of the park loop at `_start`, which loads `sp` from `hart_stacks[tp]` and leaves `.bss` alone (it is hart 0's by now); `start()` sets the hart's M-mode up as for hart 0, with its own scratch block and `mtimecmp`; and `supervisor_entry` calls `hart_main()` instead of `kmain()`: the hart's `struct cpu`, the kernel page table in `satp`, `sie.SSIE`, then it sets its bit in `harts_up` (with `amoor.w`) and enters the scheduler for good. Hart 0 waits for every bit, runs the TLB shootdown self-test (below) and prints `smp: N harts running`. The disk kernel boots the same way, since Camelot's bootloader stores the kernel's entry point in the ROM's entry word (section 7, "Boot from disk"). A hart's stack is one page, like a process's kernel stack: it holds the scheduler loop and the interrupts taken there.

**IPIs.** S-mode does not touch the CLINT, so a hart interrupts others with a machine call, `MCALL_IPI` (a7 = 1, a0 = a mask of harts), for which `machine_vec` writes 1 to each one's `msip`. `mie.MSIE` is set on every hart, so each target traps to `machine_vec` with a machine software interrupt, writes 0 to its own `msip` and raises `sip.SSIP`. A timer tick raises the same bit, so `machine_vec` also marks each tick in the hart's scratch block (word 5, the tick word), and the kernel's handler (`soft_interrupt()`, `kernel/trap.c`) clears SSIP, serves what other harts asked of it (a TLB flush is the only request there is), then swaps the tick word to 0 (`amoswap.w`, so a tick arriving in between is not lost) and counts a tick only if it was 1. An IPI that asks nothing still ends a `wfi`: that is how an idle hart is woken.

**Ticks.** Every hart has its own timer tick, which ends the time slice of the thread it runs and counts in its `struct cpu` (`ticks`). Hart 0's ticks alone advance `ticks`, the clock that `sleep` and `uptime` see, and run the network's timer, so the clock keeps its 10 Hz whatever the number of harts.

**Scheduler.** Every hart runs the same `scheduler()` loop over the one thread table, under `proc_lock`: the table is the run queue. With 16 slots and at most 8 harts a search is a few hundred instructions at worst, so queues per hart would only add the work of balancing them; with one table, a runnable thread goes to the first hart that looks, and no hart idles while another has work waiting. `proc_lock` is still held across every switch, so a thread is RUNNING on one hart at most and only that hart switches back from it; it may run on a different hart each time (its trap frame's `kernel_tp` follows it), and two threads of one process may run on two harts at once. Hart 0's loop returns to `kmain` once every process has ended, and the machine stops; the other harts' never return. A hart with nothing to run sets `idle` in its `struct cpu`, under `proc_lock`, and waits in `wfi`. When a thread becomes runnable (made by `spawn` or `thread_create`, woken by `wakeup`, or killed in its sleep), the hart that made it so wakes one idle hart for it with an IPI and clears that hart's `idle` (`kick_idle`, `kernel/proc.c`), rather than leave the thread waiting for that hart's next tick; an IPI that lands between the search and the `wfi` makes the `wfi` return at once. The `harts` system call (`docs/camelot-syscalls.md`) and program list each hart, the process it runs, its ticks and the threads it has switched to.

**PLIC.** Device interrupts all go to hart 0: the sources are enabled for context 1 (hart 0's S-mode context) only, and the other harts leave `sie.SEIE` clear ("Kernel console"). Hart 0 always exists, its handlers are short (they move bytes, blocks and frames and wake sleepers, which any hart may then run), and with one context claiming, each interrupt is handled once and in order. Spreading the sources over several harts' contexts would only pay for devices far busier than Camelot's; the PLIC allows it (a source pending for several contexts goes to whichever claims it first, section 7.1).

**TLB shootdown.** A hart may cache translations from the page table in its `satp`, so after a mapping is taken away its page may be reused only once no hart can still use a stale one. `unmap_pages()` therefore works in two passes: it clears V in each PTE (which keeps the page's address), calls `tlb_shootdown(pt)`, and only then frees the pages and zeroes the PTEs. `tlb_shootdown(pt)` executes `sfence.vma` on its own hart, asks every other hart that may hold translations from `pt` to do the same, and waits until they have: the harts whose `satp` is `pt` (`cpus[i].pagetable`, kept up to date by the scheduler and `exec`), or every hart for the kernel page table, whose second-level tables every process's table shares. Each request is a ticket: the requester bumps `tlb_asked[i]` with `amoadd.w` and sends hart i an IPI, and hart i, in `tlb_serve()`, reads `tlb_asked[i]`, executes `sfence.vma`, then stores what it read in `tlb_done[i]`, with interrupts off so that an IPI taken meanwhile can't store a later count that this store would then take back; the requester waits until `tlb_done[i]` reaches its ticket, so the flush that satisfies it began after the ticket was taken, after the change to the table. `cpus[i].pagetable` is read without a lock: the requester stores the PTEs, then loads it, and a hart switching tables stores it, then walks the new table, so each side has a `fence rw, rw` between its store and its load (`fence_rw()`, in `tlb_shootdown()`, the scheduler and `exec`); without them both could see the old values under RVWMO, and the requester would skip a hart that goes on using the old PTE. A hart waiting for its tickets serves its own requests meanwhile, so two harts shooting down each other both get through, and the caller holds no spinlock (a hart spinning on it with interrupts off could not answer) unless no other hart can be using the table. A process's page table is in use on every hart that runs one of its threads, so a thread's `sbrk` that shrinks the heap asks each of them ("Kernel threads"); the kernel page table is in use on every hart, and the boot's self-test maps and unmaps a page in it (at 0x4000_0000, whose second-level table the vm self-test left), which every other hart must answer before the page is freed (`smp: hart N missed the TLB shootdown`, counted as a failure, if one has not). Grail has no TLB, so every `sfence.vma` is free there, but the protocol is the one real hardware needs; the same goes for the AMOs' `aq` and `rl` bits.

**Panics.** The first hart to panic prints the report and stops the machine; a hart that panics meanwhile, or writes anything to the UART (`kprintf`, a process's `write` to the console, the line editor's echo: `uart_putc()` checks before every byte), stops where it is (`panic_freeze()`: interrupts off, `wfi` forever), so the report comes out whole. The panicking hart no longer takes the UART's lock, which a frozen hart may hold. `panic_begin()` turns interrupts off, sets `panicking` with `amoswap.w`, then stores its id in `panic_hart`, which holds `PANIC_NO_HART` (no hart's id, not 0, which is hart 0's) until then, so a hart that looks in between takes the panic for another hart's and stops.

**Tests** (`crates/xtask/tests/smp.rs`): the kernel boots on 1, 2, 4 and 8 harts in the default 4 MiB, printing what it prints on one hart plus `smp: N harts running` (nothing more on one), every other hart answers the boot's shootdown with an `sfence.vma` before the page is freed, the clock (`ticks`, which `sleep` and `uptime` read) still counts ten ticks a second, and the shell's `harts` lists every hart with its ticks; with hart 2 made to skip the boot shootdown's IPI, hart 0 waits for its flush at its next tick before freeing the page, and nothing is reported missing; a shootdown of the kernel page table, called by the test on hart 0 while harts 1-3 run `primes` workers in U-mode, is answered by all three before it returns; `primes 4 40000` on 4 harts has its four workers in U-mode on four different harts at once, every hart runs one, and the counts match the test's own; `stress 6 3` on 4 harts passes with each hart checked whenever it enters U-mode (no spinlock held by it, no `push_off` pending), every page back, and stress's processes seen on every hart; `systest` passes on 4 harts; the disk kernel booted from the root image on 4 harts runs the shell, `harts` and `stress` from `/bin`, with fsck finding the image consistent afterwards; a hart made to fault in the kernel while another writes `stress`'s output prints its report word for word, nothing follows it, the writer ends in `panic_freeze()` and the machine exits 0xfe; and with `panicking` set but `panic_hart` not yet, hart 0 stops at its next print, on 1 hart and on 4.

### Kernel threads

A process may have several threads, which share its address space, descriptors and working directory and run at once on several harts (`kernel/proc.c`, `kernel/include/proc.h`; the calls are specified in `docs/camelot-syscalls.md`, "Threads"). The design is a split of xv6's `struct proc` into what a program owns and what runs: everything the scheduler, `sleep` and the traps deal with (state, channel, kernel stack, trap frame, context, `killed`) moved to `struct thread`, and `struct proc` kept the address space, the files, the parent and the exit status ("Kernel processes" above). There are 16 thread slots for all processes together (`NTHREAD`) and 8 process slots; a slot of either kind is found by a linear search under `proc_lock`, as before.

**Creating.** `thread_create(entry, arg, stack)` takes a thread slot and a kernel stack page, fills the new thread's trap frame with zeros but `sepc` = `entry`, `sp` = `stack` and `a0` = `arg`, and makes it runnable: its first `swtch` lands in `thread_start`, which returns to U-mode through that frame, as a new process's main thread does. The user stack is the caller's memory: the kernel only checks that the 16 bytes under `stack` are writable and `entry` executable. A process that is being ended takes no new thread (`-EAGAIN`), so whoever ends it waits only for the threads it knows of. The tid comes from the pids' counter, so tids and pids never collide and the main thread's tid is the pid; `getpid` returns the process's, `gettid` the thread's.

**Ending and joining.** `thread_exit(status)` makes the thread a zombie holding `status` and wakes its joiners; the scheduler frees its kernel stack once it has switched away from it for the last time (as it did for a process), and the slot stays until a `thread_join` collects the status. The last thread to end ends the process instead, with its status as the exit status. `thread_join(tid, status)` sleeps on the thread's slot until it is a zombie, copies the status out and frees the slot; any thread may join any other of its process. An ended thread that nobody joins keeps its slot (but no page) until the process ends, which frees every thread slot it still has.

**Ending a process.** `exit`, a fault and `kill` end the whole process, from whichever thread gets there first: `end_other_threads` marks the process `exiting` and every other thread `killed` (waking those in a long sleep, as `kill` did for a process), then sleeps until the thread count falls to one. Each other thread ends at its next return towards U-mode (`proc_killed()` is its own `killed` or its process's), with no status anyone collects. Only then are the files closed, the working directory let go and the process made a zombie: by then no thread of it can still be using them. `exec` does the same before its new program starts, building the new address space first so that a failure leaves the threads running, and closing the executable (its inode lock and transaction) before it waits: another thread may be waiting for that inode, or for room in the log, to finish its call, and would never end otherwise. If another thread is already ending the process, `exec` fails with `-EINTR` and its caller ends with the rest. Nothing that waits for the other threads to end may hold a sleep lock or a transaction. A thread that faults ends its process as `exit(-1)` from that thread would, with the report printed by the faulting thread.

**Futexes.** `futex_wait(addr, val)` takes `proc_lock`, reads the word at `addr` through the page table (`copyin`), and if it equals `val` sleeps on its own thread's `futex` field with the address stored there; `futex_wake(addr, n)` takes `proc_lock` and wakes up to `n` threads of the caller's process whose stored address is `addr`. Since the read and the sleep happen under the same lock that `futex_wake` takes, no wake falls between them. A futex is keyed by process and user address, so it needs no table of its own; the cost is a scan of the 16 thread slots per wake.

**What threads share, and the locks it took.** Each thread has its own kernel stack, so two threads of a process can be in the kernel at once, on two harts or on one (one asleep). What they share needed locks that a process alone didn't ("Kernel locking"): `files` for the descriptor table and the working directory (a call takes a reference to the file it works on with `fd_get`, so another thread's `close` can't free it meanwhile), `heap` for `sbrk`, `uvm` for the user copies against a concurrent `unmap_pages`, and the TLB shootdown, which now finds other harts that run the same page table ("Kernel SMP"). Sleep locks remember their holder by tid. A buffer checked at the start of a call may be unmapped by another thread's `sbrk` before the call has copied all of it, so every `copyin` and `copyout` result is checked: a read or write (file, pipe, console) stops at the first chunk that can't be copied and returns the bytes it moved, or `-EFAULT` if none, and never passes on bytes it didn't copy; `fstat`, `procs` and `harts` return `-EFAULT`, and `pipe` closes the descriptors it made.

**In user space** (`kernel/user/lib/`, `kernel/user/lib/README.md`): `atomic.s` (`atomic_swap`, `atomic_add`, `atomic_cas` with `lr.w`/`sc.w`, `atomic_load`, `atomic_store`, all sequentially consistent with `.aqrl` or fences), a spinlock (`spin_lock` swaps a 1 in with `amoswap.w.aq` and spins reading until the word is 0, `spin_unlock` swaps 0 back with `.rl`), a mutex on futexes (Drepper's three-state mutex: an uncontended lock or unlock is one atomic instruction and no system call), and `thread_start(fn, arg, stack, size)`, which starts the C function `fn(arg)` on the given stack through an assembly trampoline that calls `thread_exit` with what it returns. `malloc` takes a mutex, so threads may allocate at once.

**Tests** (`crates/xtask/tests/threads.rs`, both on 1 hart and on 4, with the hart checked on each entry to U-mode: no spinlock held, no `push_off` pending): `threads 4 300` (four threads adding to two counters, under the futex mutex and under the spinlock, yielding inside the lock now and then so that a lock that doesn't exclude loses additions on one hart too) must print exact counts, with at least two of its threads in U-mode on different harts at the same moment on 4 (and on its process's page table); `threadtest` (every call's results and errors, futexes, the library's locks and `malloc` from several threads, `sbrk` from three at once, a pipe write cut short by another thread's `sbrk`, a process ended by `exit`, `kill`, `exec` and a fault while its threads sleep, wait, join and spin, a kill whose threads only wait on futexes and joins, the last thread's `thread_exit`, running out of thread slots) must pass with its exact number of checks, its faulting child reported like any killed process, each joined thread found already without its kernel stack, and every shootdown of a user page table flushed by every hart it targets before it returns and aimed at every other hart that ran the table from its start to its IPI (on 4 harts, some of them at two such harts); `threadtest fs`, booted with the root image so that programs come from the disk, must pass too: `exec` while a sibling reads the same program or writes more than a transaction's worth at a time, `exit` and `kill` while siblings read and write, `close` of a descriptor two siblings use, `chdir` while another opens, after which fsck finds the disk clean with every block and inode back. After each, every page is back on the free list and only the shell's process and thread are left.

## 11. Roadmap

1. **Grail core**: RV32IM fetch/decode/execute, ELF loader, UART, test device, `xcal.dbg`/`xcal.halt`. Pass rv32ui/rv32um.
2. **Disassembler** (shared decoder) and an emulator trace mode.
3. **Privilege + traps**: CSRs, M/S/U, CLINT timer, Sv32. Pass rv32mi/rv32si. (Landed together with step 1.)
4. **merlin-as** emitting ELF `.o`. Validate with `llvm-objdump` and ld.lld.
5. **merlin-ld**. Validate against clang-produced objects.
6. **Bare-metal hello world** written in our assembly, built entirely with our tools.
7. **M extension** in merlin-as (Grail already passes rv32um), then the **A extension** (LR/SC and AMOs, section 4.1) in Grail and the toolchain. **Xcal devices** in Grail: framebuffer window, keyboard and pointer via Win32 FFI, sysinfo, block devices and network cards with pluggable backends, and a configurable machine (RAM size, real-time clock, disks, network cards) that a host can run several of.
8. **Merlin v0**: C subset → assembly. Grow it test by test (integer types, pointers, arrays, globals, strings, structs, the preprocessor, varargs, designated initializers and programs of several files done). Done (#15): merlin, merlin-as and merlin-ld build the whole kernel and its user programs, which is how the committed `kernel/kernel.elf` is made; clang stays as a reference build (`cargo xtask kernel --cc clang`). Next: optimization (registers instead of a stack machine) to close the 5-6x speed gap with clang -O2.
9. **Camelot kernel** in C (built by clang at first, by merlin since #15): trap handling, timer, UART driver, then paging (Sv32), processes, a disk driver and buffer cache, syscalls and a user-space shell, a journaled filesystem (CMFS, `docs/camelot-fs.md`) with a host-side mkfs and fsck, file descriptors, pipes and programs run from the disk. Spinlocks, sleep locks and one lock order over every shared kernel structure (done, #61: "Kernel locking"), then the kernel on every hart of the machine, with one shared scheduler, IPIs and TLB shootdown (done, #63: "Kernel SMP").

   **Phase 9c, networking** (Ethernet and IPv4; issues #80 to #85): the network card in Grail with pluggable backends (an in-process hub, pcap capture; section 9.8), virtual Ethernet switches in Avalon connecting its VMs (protocol v3), a user-mode NAT gateway (DHCP, DNS, internet access and host port forwarding, in std Rust with no host driver) behind a Grail card or on an Avalon switch (protocol v5), and in the kernel a NIC driver, ARP, IPv4, ICMP, UDP (done, #83: `docs/camelot-net.md`), TCP (done, #84), socket syscalls and network tools (done, #85: `ping`, `ifconfig`, `nc`, `fetch`, `httpd`).
10. **Self-hosting**: our tools running on our OS, rewritten in a language Merlin compiles, with the host tools as the bootstrap and the reference (section 10, "Self-hosting"; #23): a user address space big enough for them (#134, done), the assembler (#135, done), the linker (#136, done), then the compiler, once the Camelot language exists (#122). The compiler step is paused until step 11 is done, so it is written once, for 64 bits.
11. **64 bits** (#141): Excalibur becomes RV64.
    - **What changes:** RV64IMA + Zicsr + Zifencei, Sv48 for Camelot (Sv39 too in Grail), the lp64 ABI and ELF64, the 64-bit device interface (section 9, version 2), 48-bit physical addresses and up to 1 TiB of lazily backed RAM.
    - **The kernel:** a higher-half kernel in Linux's layout, with 16 KiB kernel stacks and guard pages, and a system call ABI with LP64 Linux widths.
    - **The C subset:** `long long` and fixed-width types in merlin.
    - **How:** each layer keeps RV32 working while it moves, in order:
      - this spec (#142);
      - the shared ISA crate and ELF64 (#143, #144);
      - Grail (#145, #146 and #147, done);
      - merlin-as, merlin-ld and merlin-objdump (#148, #149 and #150, done);
      - RV64 programs end to end (#151, done): the RV64 disk boot ROM, every example built for RV64;
      - merlin (#152, done: LP64, RV64 code and `long long` on both widths), merlin-dbg (#153, done: RV64 machines and programs; its kernel case waits for #154);
      - the kernel (#154, done: a higher-half kernel in Linux's layout on Sv48, or Sv39 on harts without it, #172; its boot self-tests on 1-8 harts, from the ROM and from disk, "The RV64 kernel"), then processes and user space (#155, #156);
      - the self-hosted tools (#157), the platform (#158).

      Then RV32 is removed (#159). Paused meanwhile: #115, #116, #120, #121, #127, #133.
